PREFACE

Any archacological report, especially one containing a good
deal of reconmaissance material, is not so much a record of the
writer's personal cfforts as it is a tribute to the helpfulness,
courtesy, and generosity of others. In these few lines, therefore,
I take pleasure in thanking the pcople of El Salvador who made
possible the writing of this Memoir.

‘Yo various members of the government of El Salvador, I wish
to express my gratitude for their extremely liberal and helpful
attitude toward the work of the Andean Institute expedition. In
this connection I mention in particular Professor José A. Orantes,
Subesceretary of Public Instruction, and Señor don Constantino
Espirat, Chief Collector ol Customs,

The members of the Legation of the United States, headed by
Minister Robert Frazer, were unfailingly willing to cope with the
many problems I put before them. Their hospitality and courtesy
are a tribute to the Foreign Service.

Tam grateful to Doctor don Atilio Peccorini of San Miguel for
many delightful hours spent at his hacienda, “La Estancia,” and
for the privilege of studying his very fine pottery collection. T also
wish to thank Señores Inocente Aguirre, of Quclepa, Adolfo
Anliker, of Hacienda “La Esperanza," and Coronel Montalbo,
Conumandante of Usulutan, for permission to study their extensive
collections of pottery and stone.

I owe a very personal debt of gratitude to Señor don Fidel
Melara, his wife, doña Tina, and their three children. Five of
the happiest months of my field career were spent in their pleasant
home in San Miguel. While doña Vina amply and efficiently
supplied my living demands, don Fidel gave unstintingly of his
time in order to acquaint me with castern El Salvador. He located
and directed me to several new sites, among them Los Llanitos, and
arranged for workmen and tools for my excavation, Also, in his
capacity as foreman in the highway department, he advised me
on road conditions and was instrumental in keeping the road from
town to my dig in decent enough repair to permit daily travel.
An amatcur archacologist and collector himsclf, he was keenly
interested in all of my activities.

Señor don Euscbio Padilla of Santiago de Maria, and owner of
Hacienda “Los Llanitos,” generously gave permission for excava-
tions on his property and facilitated my work there with every
means at his command. His son, don Rogclio, in immediate charge
of the hacienda, was also unfailingly helpful.

‘The Department of Roads for eastern El Salvador furnished
me with tools and measuring instruments whenever T asked for
them, My small crew of workmen mastered a strange, and to them
somewhat ridiculous, technique of digging with amazing ease.

The expedition described in this Memoir was one of several
sent out by the Institute for Andean Research to investigate
anthropological problems in Latin America. The director of my
project, Dr. A. V. Kidder, gave freely of his time and advice,
despite the pressure of other duties. Mr. Stanley Boggs was a
capable and cheerful associate in the field. In addition to Appendix
C, he also contributed a great deal to Part II of this report.

‘The El Salvador site map and many of the line-drawings
accompanying this Memoir are the work of Dr. Erwin Raisz and
his assistants. The remaining text figures were drawn by Incs
Boggs, Stanley Boggs, Priscilla Archibald, and the author. The
stones used in various artifacts from Los Llanitos were identified
by Professors Kirk Bryan and E. S. Larsen, Jr., of the Department
of Geology, Harvard University. The Carnegie Institution of
Washington, Division of Historical Research, kindly permitted me
to use many of their photographs. The manuscript was prepared
for publication by Miss Cordelia Galt.

Besides capably shouldering my share of domestic and financial
dutics while I was in the field, I am grateful to my wife for criti-
cising and correcting sundry misspellings and flagrant errors of
literary style which appeared in the first draft of this manuscript.
Should any still remain, it is through no fault of hers.

Joun M, Loncyear, UL

Belmont, Massachusetts
March 24, 1943

BART I

GENERAL INTRODUCTION

## Page 11

_Extraction method: OCR._

ARCHAEOLOGICAL INVESTIGATIONS IN EL SALVADOR

PART I. GENERAL INTRODUCTION

This Memoir is a result of an expedition to El Salvador con-
ducted by the writer from September, 1941, to April, 1942. The
investigations were carricd out under the auspices of the Institute
of Andean Research, with funds supplied by the Office of the
Co-ordinator of Inter-American Affairs, El Salvador, and especially
that part of the Republic east of the Rio Lempa, was chosen as
a focus of study for several reasons. Investigations of a sort similar
to those planned by the writer were being carried out in Guate-
mala and Nicaragua, thus eliminating those countrics. Further-
more, El Salvador combined a small area with a good network of
roads and other means of communication, making it ideal for
reconnaissance work, Finally, the lack of previous archaeological
study in this important region made an expedition here highly
desirable.

It was decided to spend the first few months of the trip in a
reconnaissance of castern El Salvador and the latter part of the
season in an excavation of some small site. This report, therefore,
has been divided into sections corresponding to that plan.

1. PHYSICAL ASPECTS OF EL SALVADOR

The Republic of El Salvador is, in size, the smallest of Central
American countries and, in population, the most dense. That this
density of population was also a characteristic of the country in
pre-Columbian times is confirmed by the great number of archaeo-
logical sites recorded for the region, and by the fact that potsherds
and other artifacts are found scattered over the ground in almost
every section of the country.!

This circumstance is perhaps remarkable, when one considers
the volcanic nature of El Salvador. From sections seen today in
highway and railroad cuts throughout the country, it is evident
that the land was frequently covered by lava and ash deposits of
considerable depth (pl. I, i). That some, at least, of these occurred
during human occupation of the country is proved by the finding
of potsherds in humus layers covered by many meters of volcanic
deposits? The aboriginal inhabitants of such an inhospitable
region might well have been expected to move to other parts, but
to judge from their remains and the size of the population as re-
ported by the Spanish conquerors, the Indians refused to be
driven, permanently, from their land by any activity of nature.

Squier observed in 1855 that "San [El] Salvador comprehends
more volcanoes, and has within its limits more marked results of
volcanic action, than probably any other equal extent of the
earth.” The major volcanoes in the Republic stretch in a broad
band across the country not far from the coast and are eleven in
number, Their names, from west to cast are: Tamagas, Mala
Caña, Santa Ana, Izaleo, San Salvador,  Guzapa, San Vicente,
Tecapa, Usulutan, San Miguel, and Conchagua. Most of these
volcanoes are inactive and have been for many years; a few, how-
ever, have erupted with varying degrees of force during the past

? For a site fist of El Salvador, see Appendix D, Site lists for the country are also

found in Lardé, 1926; Lothrop, 19eha; Calderon, 1939-
*Lardé, 1924; Lothrop, 1927b.            *Squicr, 1855, p. 313.

century. The latter are: Izalco (1902, 1917, 1919, and 1926), San
Salvador (1917), and San Miguel (1844).*

Although volcanoes form the most obvious physical feature of
El Salvador, the mighty Rio Lempa plays as important a part in
the topography of the State. Rising in Guatemala, this river flows
from west to east through a broad basin in the center of El Sal-
vador, then turns suddenly south and divides the country into a
larger western and a smaller eastern part, The Lempa is by far
the largest river of the Central American Pacific slope and, as we
shall see later, played an important role in determining the migra-
tions of people in its vicinity.                                 z

The only other rivers of any size contained in El Salvador are
the Rio Grande, which empties into the Pacific south of Sonsonate,
and the Rio Grande de San Miguel, which forms a broad valley
south and west of the city of San Miguel, Many other valleys of
smaller rivers, however, such as the Jiboa, Torola, and Titihuapa,
were, and are, important centers of population.

West of the Rio Lempa the coastal strip of El Salvador is nar-
row, being terminated rather abruptly by a great plateau, averag-
ing about 600 m. in height. This plateau is studded by several
volcanoes of over 2000 m. altitude and is almost bisected in the
center by the great Zapotitan Valley. North of the plateau the
land drops into the broad valley of the Rio Lempa, here flowing
from west to cast, and rises again into mountains on the Honduran
frontier.

In castern El Salvador, most of the country is under 500 m. in
altitude. Except for the volcano of Conchagua, the hills of
Jucuaran, and the massif which includes the volcanoes of Tecapa
and San Miguel, the terrain is comparatively low-lying and open
until reaching the rugged, mountainous country near the Hon-
duras border (frontispiece and pl. I, 72).

‘There are four good-sized lakes in the Republic, these being,
from west to east, Guija, Coatepeque, Tlopango, and Camalotal
(Olomega). Lakes Coatepeque and Ilopango are situated in extinct
volcanic craters and add much to the scenery of the country.

The volcanic soils of El Salvador are extremely fertile, and fully
80 per cent of the country is at present under cultivation, The
principal crops are coffee, sugar cane, and hennequen, these being
grown mainly in the western, central, and castern parts of the
Republic, respectively. Subsistence crops, such as rice and corn,
are also raised.*

2. HISTORY AND LINGUISTIC BACKGROUND
OF EASTERN EL SALVADOR

The conquest of western El Salvador by Pedro de Alvarado in
1524 is too widely documented to necessitate repetition here.* It
is sufficient to say that it was an arduous procedure, as the first
“conquest” was hardly worthy of the name, and several subsequent
expeditions were necessary before the country was completely

“Calderon, 1989. pp. 4-4.

‘Calderon, 1959. pp. 19-20.
* Alvarado, 1924; Bancroft, 1882; Herrera, 1726-40; Juarros, 1323,

¢                                      ARCHAEOLOGICAL INVESTIGATIONS IN El. SALVADOR

pacified, About the conquest of eastern El Salvador, however, less
is known. The original source of information on this region ap-
pears to he Herrera? although Bernal Diaz del Castillo remarks
that he anc Alvarado crossed through the territory in 1526 East-
ern El Salvador, or the Province of San Miguel, is next mentioned
by historians in connection with a raid conducted by a band of
Spanish soldiers under the command of Martin Estete.? The pur-
pose of this raid was to take the Province of San Salvador away
fram the Kingdom of Guatemala and annex it to Nicaragua. To
this end, Estete marched from the latter country through San
Miguel Province and ultimately captured the city of San Salvador.
His success was short-lived, however, as an army from Guatemala
soon drove the invader back across the Rio Lempa, from where
Fstete escaped to Nicaragua. Pedro de Alvarado was in Mexico
at the time of this invasion but hurried to Guatemala when news
of Estete's activities reached him. By the time the Adclentado
reached his capital, in April, 1530, Estete had been driven out of
Ll Salvador." Not wishing a repetition of this invasion, Alvarado
decided to establish a settlement in eastern El Salvador, and at
the same time, to bring this hitherto unexplored territory more
closely under his rule. Accordingly, in the spring of 1530, “he...
sent Captain Luis Moscoso to cross the Rio Lempa with 120
soldiers and explore and pacify another Province which was un-
explored. ,., The Adelantado ordered him to found a town and
to it sent men appointed mayors and magistrates."Y The town
thus founded was San Miguel.

For the next forty years, the Province of San Miguel received
scant notice from historians. Beginning in the last quarter of the
sixteenth century, however, a series of documents on Central
America, written by travelers, not warriors, began to find their
way back to Europe. Several of these contain material, both de-
scriptive and linguistic, on castern El Salvador. Two of the most
important sources o£ information on this area in the latter half
of the sixteenth century are Diego Garcia de Palacio and Fray
Alonso Ponce. Both actually traveled in the region, and the reports
of these journeys include many valuable ethnological and linguis-
tic data. Some idea of the people inhabiting eastern El Salvador
shortly after the Conquest may be gained from these documents.

Palacio, writing in 1576, gives a list of languages spoken by the
natives of several provinces, among them that of San Miguel, which
at that time included the whole of El Salvador east of the Rio
Lempa According to this historian, the languages of San Miguel
were the Poton and the Taulepa Ulua.” Neither in speaking of
this province, nor later, in describing that of Honduras, does
Palacio mention the word “Lenca.” We know, however, from
colonial records, place names, and existing vocabularies, that
Lenca was widely spoken in both these regions and that it was
practically universal in castern El Salvador. The conclusion to be
reached from this, then, is that Palacio listed dialects of Lenca

as separate tongues. To determine which dialect was spoken in
the southern half of this region—the area most closely connected
with this Memoit—we 10ust tura to Ponce,

Ten years alter Palacios account was written, Fray Alonso
Ponce made his celebrated journey through Guatemala, El Salva-
dor, and Nicaragua. The chroniclers of the padre's trip give the Fol-
lowing list of the Indian languages in the Province of Guatemala,
which at that Lime included the three countries named above:

“The common languages which are in this province [Guate-
mala] among the Indians in our care are Mexican and Achi, which
is divided into four parts: Guatemalteca, Tzotuhil, Kakchikel, and
Ultateca; and besides these there are others which are Ulua,
Mangue, and Poton, and another in that of Chiapa."

When Ponce's caravan had reached a point just south of the
city of San Miguel, it was recorded: ‘The Indians of that guardian-
ship [of San Miguel] are part Poton and part Ulua, but they under
stand the Mexican language. . . ."""*

This account duplicates that of Palacio, except for the qualily-
ing “Taulepa” applicd by the litter to the Ulua dialect, and may
be taken as reasonably authentic. Like Palacio, also, Ponce's
chroniclers do not mention the word “Lenca” in their deserip-
tions of either Honduras or San Miguel, so here, again, we prob-
ably have dialects considered as sey

The journal of the Ponce expedition, however, goes further
into the question than does Palacio's letter, in that it notes the
languages spoken in the various villages through which the padre
passed, According to the account, Ulua was spoken mainly in
Nicaragua. In El Salvador, it was noted only in Tzirama (Sirama,
northwest of La Union), and in Omonleo, a village 4 leagues cast
of the Rio Grande de San Miguel. Poton, on the other hand, was
widespread in eastern El Salvador, being spoken (from west to
cast) in Oxucar, Auacayo, Xiquilisco (Jiquilisco), Ozolutlan
(Usulutan), Santa Maria, Ereuaiquin (Ereguaiquia), Xiriualtique
(Xeribaltique), Elenuayquin (on the west bank of Rio Grande de
San Miguel), some parts of Tzirama, Amapal (Amapala, on Isla
Tigre, Bay of Fonseca), Gonxagua and Teca (on Isla Conchaguita),
and Meangola (on Isla Mecanguera).** The farthest westward ex-
tension of the Ulua dialect, then, was to Omonlco, 4 leagues cast
of the Rio Grande de San Miguel, while Poton, besides being
spoken on islands in the Bay of Fonseca, extended west of the Rio
Grande at least to Usulutan and Jiquilisco. In other words, the
Rio Grande de San Miguel appears to have been the western
boundary of the Ulua dialect, leaving Poton as the tongue spoken
over most of southern San Miguel Province.

Lehmann has attempted to prove that Poton was a Maya dialect,
allied to the Chorti of Copan." This thesis, however, is accom-
panied by two misconceptions: (a) that Palacio listed three lan
guages (Poton, ‘Taulepa, and Ulua) as spoken in San Miguel in-
stead of two (Poton and Taulepa Ulua),’* and (bj that numerous

e languages.

an error in copying, since Choluteca heve refers to a district, not a language, Squier
has this correctly in bis transcription. But even with this correction, Ue Go Span-
ish texts do not agree, that of Squier's listing tico Janguages, and that of the Don
mentos”, three, Lacking an opportunity to examine the original document, it is
possible to determine the correct translation with absolute certainty, The author,
however, is inclined to accept Squiers copy (but not his translation, since Ube evi-
dence from Ponce’s “Relación” (sec below) shows that only tico Languages were
spoken in the Province of San Miguel.

GENERAL INTRODUCTION                                                         5

Maya artifacts were present in castern El Salvador, thus showing
that the Maya had actually inhabited that area in times past. The
first error is easily rectified by an examination of the original text
of Palacio (see footnote 13). The second error was not apparent
to Lehmann at the time of his writing, but subsequent research
has shown conclusively that Maya influences in castern El Salva-
dor are so rare as to be credited only to trade, and not to actual
invasion or habitation. It seems more reasonable, therefore, to
assign Poton to the Lenca tongue.

Various linguistic maps have placed Pipil east of the Rio Lempa
in El Salvador,” and Ponce's chronicle notes that the Indians of
San Miguel guardianship understood the Mexican language. The
influence of the Pipil on most cultural aspects in this region,
however, was probably not great. It is quite possible that the
Mexicans themselves never entered eastern El Salvador, although
their language did, the latter being widely used in Central America
alter the conquest, serving as the means of communication be-
tween the natives and the Spaniards.

3. SUMMARY OF PREVIOUS ARCHAEOLOGICAL
WORK

‘Yo date, practically all the archacological work in El Salvador
has been carried on by private citizens of that country, notably
the owners of cstates containing ruins of various types. The
natural result of this situation has been the amassing of a great
number of fine private collections, practically none of which,
however, contain completely typical artifacts of any given site or
are fully documented. Nevertheless, because of the dearth of
scientific investigation in the country, investigators have been
forced to base their conclusions concerning the aboriginal popula-
ions of El Salvador on these collections. Some good results, of
course, have been thus attained, but the whole story cannot be
obtained by this means alone,

EG. Squier appears to have been the first to publish anything
about the archaeological remains of El Salvador, but his informa-
tion is limited to occasional brief notes, he being more concerned
with living Indian peoples during his visit In 1891 Montessus de
Ballore published an atlas containing pictures of some El Salva-
doran specimens, together with notes on a few ruins.** Carl Sapper
later devoted a few pages to sites and artifacts of the country.”
D. Gonzalez** and L, A, Rodriguez,” local investigators, published
on various ruins, but, unfortunately, much of their material is
contained in publications almost unobtainable today. Dr. Atilio
Peccorini followed with a general article on El Salvadoran
archaeology and later with one on the ruins of Quelepa2* In
1915, Dr, H. J. Spinden, following a visit to the Republic, wrote a
general summary of investigations up to that date and introduced
some speculations on the succession of cultures in the region as
revealed by several private collections? Beginning with the
“archaic” perioxl, typificd by solid hand-modeled figurines and
certain examples of pottery vessels,* the succession included a
transitional period, a Maya period, a post-Maya period, and
finally, an Aztec period, cach distinguished by various ceramic
forms,

The fitst indication of stratified remains in El Salvador was
noted by Professor Jorge Lardé in 1924, when he published a
Poton was us left over anc was assigned to a Maya dialect, since the presence of
Maya artifacts in the region suggested to Lehmann that that language also had been
spoken there in times

* Lathrop, 19g. fig, t

Squier, 1855, 1858.

* Montessus de Ballore, 18gp, 1891.

BSapper, Bol.           * González, 1906,
“Rodriguez, 1895, 1913.

Jolson, 1940.

brief paper on the discovery of a humus stratum containing
cultural remains underlying a thick layer of volcanic ash. In
1926, Lardé and Dr. S. K. Lothrop conducted joint excavations
at the Cerro Zapote, just outside of San Salvador, and the publica-
tion of their results by Lothrop set the stage for all future specula-
tions on the archaeology of this region.”

The site of the Cerro Zapote is now too well known to Central
American archacologists to require description in detail. Suffice
to say that it consisted of two humus strata, separated by a thick
deposit of sterile volcanic ash. In the lower humus layer were
found hand-modeled figurines and sherds of Usulutan ware. In
the upper level occurred remains of a variety of cultures: “archaic”
type figurines, pottery with Maya painted designs, plumbate ware,
and “Tlaloc" vessels characteristic of the Pipil civilization, From
these finds, Lothrop concluded: (a) that an carly population,
represented by remains in the lower humus stratum, once existed
in El Salvador; (b) that the later cultures of the region, typified by
Maya and Pipil vessels and plumbate ware, found in the upper
level, were in part contemporancous; (c) that the “archaic” types
of the lower level persisted, with variations, into the Maya and
Pipil periods.

Following the above investigations by Lardé and Lothrop,
archacological research in El Salvador remained absolutely dor-
mant until 1939, when Mr. John Dimick began a scrics of excava-
tions at Campana-San Andres, a large group of mounds about
30 km. west of San Salvador! The first season's work was spon-
sored by the Middle American Research Institute of ‘Tulane Uni-
versity. Succeeding excavations have been under the aegis of the
Division of Historical Research of the Carnegie Institution of
Washington, with Mr. Stanley Boggs assisting in the field. The
Campana-San Andres project is net yet completed, so that it is
impossible to give any details of the site at this time. It can be
said, however, that the ruins, constructed largely of adobe blocks,
show evidences of at least four building phases, separated by
presumably short intervals uf time, siuce the pottery shows lew
prefound changes from the earliest phase to the last, The ceramic
complex contains many wares which at present (for Jack of com-
parative evidence) are considered indigenous to the site, while
other wares show relations with the Copan and Ulua Maya of
Honduras and with Mexico. Until excavations of comparable
magnitude are conducted at other ruins in El Salvador, Gampana-
San Andres will of necessity remain somewhat of an enigma for,
being the first and only large ruin in the whole region to undergo
scientific excavation, we have nothing with which either to com-
pare or contrast it.

4. ARCHAEOLOGICAL PROBLEMS IN EL SALVADOR

The little archaeological work thus far conducted in El Salvador
has shown that this country had a rather complex pre-Columbian

history. Evidently, it not only originated a distinctly local style of
art, but it was also invaded by forcign customs and peoples at
various times. All this has combined to present a rather muddled
picture to the archaeologist, a picture that can be clarified only
by extensive reconnaissance and widespread stratigraphic test-
digging. Until the general set-up of Salvadoran archaeology be-
comes clearer, and the relations of the various cultures to one

*Spinden's “comb-marked" pottery (Spinden, 1915. p. 458) is now known as
Usulutan ware.

2 Lardé, 1924.

* Lothrop, ap27h.

* Boggs, 19432; Dimick, 1941.

6                                        ARCHAFOLOGICAL INVESTIGATIONS IN EL SALVADOR

another ate determined, the intensive excavation of large single
sites docs net appear advisable, since we can neither appreciate
nor capitalize on the results of such work.

At present, collections remain our best source of information
on the succession of cultures in the Republic. The theories which
they suggest may briefly be discussed here. It appears that there
was, at some time, a cultural stratum in El Salvador best char-
acterized by the presence of Usulutan ware. The frequency of this
type of pottery must be very high, especially in eastern El Salvador
and, since we lack any certain evidence to the contrary, it may be
assumed that it originated here. The associations of Usulutan ware
in this region are at present unknown, but probably consist in
part of some of the “archaic” figurine types, since these have been
found together at Cerro Zapote in central El Salvador.* ‘Truc
Usulutan ware has always been considered carly, Le., pre-poly-
chrome, and evidence from Santa Rita” and Copan™ in Honduras
tends to support this theory.

‘The Maya influence is very strong in El Salvadoran painted
pottery, especially, as might be expected, in the central and western
parts of the country. The relations are almost entirely with the
southern Maya region, particularly Copan and the Ulua Valley,”
bur a few fine figure-painted vases probably stem from the Guate-
mala highlands (pl, XL 9). Some Nicoya polychrome specimens
indicate connections with the south, but these apparently had little
or no influence on the ceramic complex as a whole, Fastern El
Salvador bad less to do with the Maya. The trade pieces that
came in Írom the Yojoa-Ulua region (Appendix E) gave risc to
few imitations in the indigenous art, which was, as far as we are
able to determine, quite unrelated to that of neighboring cultures
(Part HL, Sections 3 and 6). If the type of pottery shown in plate TX.
27, is of Lenca origin,” strong influences from the north are
indicated, for this shape and style of decoration are common in
eastern El Salvador.

Mexican inlluences were marked, and of long duration, in west-
ern and central El Salvador, but of little importance in the east.
West of the Rio Lempa, slabegged tripod vases, Vlaloc jars,"
Mixtec incensce-burners (pl. X, 5, 9), and Aztec vases™ give plentiful
evidence of strong and varied connections with Mexico which
must have lasted for several centuries. Unfortunately, the relations
of these pottery types with one another, or with Maya or El
Salvadoran wares, have not been determined. “Pipil” potrery
Temains an enigma, its identity not yet determined.

Almost nothing has been done toward the investigation of El
Salvadoran pre-Columbian architecture. As will be seen later in
this report, reconnaissance and a limited amount of excavation
have shown at least three types of construction to exist: adobe
block, rough talpetate block and slab, and cut stone, Although

* Lothrop, 1927h. p. 175.

* Suong, Kidder, and Paul, 1998, pp. 45-62.
* Lougycar, 29996. p. 269.
* Lomgyeat, 1940. p 269.        Stone, 1941. p 9
* Lothrop, 1927b, Ez. +5. 5; Spinden, 1915. Eg. 79.

distribution of materials undoubtedly has much to do with the
variety chosen at any given site, two of the types, or even three,
apparently often exist side by side in some regions. ‘Their relative
ages could probably be determined through associations with
ceramics, but first, of course, we must have the ceramic sequence.

Little attention has been paid to El Salvador stone sculpture.
Richardson” briefly reviews our knowledge of monumental carv-
ing, which consists mostly of large, more or less conventionalized,
jaguar heads. These appear to be restricted to the extreme western
part of the country and are connected with the large stone heads
of the Pacific slope of Guatemala, Other stone carvings, inclading
chacmool figures and the “Virgen de Tazumal," are also limited
to the west, except for a rather mysterious carving reported by
Squier from Tehuacan,” which is now apparently Jost. The pres-
ence of all these sculptures in the western Departments of the
Republic indicates a cultural invasion with rather definite
boundaries. This should also be reflected in the pottery of the
region, but as yet we are ignorant of the ceramic types which
accompany the stones. OF smaller carvings—amulets, beads, “mace
heads,” etc.—there is even less to sty, except that they are numerous
and widespread, The presence, however, of "duck-billed” amulets,
grotesque animal figures in the round, and other bizarre specimens
in collections (pl. X11), suggests that an interesting field of investi.
gation is waiting to be developed here.

It would be interesting to determine the possibility of con-
nections between archaeological finds and linguistic divisions in
El Salvador. This would be difficult in the central and western
arcas, where successive cultural and linguistic invasions spread
over large territories, but eastern El Salvador, due to the barrier
of the Rio Lempa, was presumably not so frequently overrun. The
most recent linguistic map of Central America" shows Lenca to
have been the dominant language of El Salvador east of the Rio
Lempa. Added to this was an infusion of Pipil to the south, and
small islands of Cacaopera and Choluteca dialects to the north
and east. Any intensive study in this region should at least take
cognizance of these divisions.

American archacologists are often accused, and not always un-
justly, of making a god out of pottery. Be that as it may, it would
appear from the above discussion that the solution of many of the
archacological problems in El Salvador lies in stratigraphic ex-
cavation, with the emphasis placed on determining the distribu-
tion and the cultural and temporal sequences of the various
ceramic wares. The Republic, lying as it does between the Maya
and Chorotegan arcas, and crossed by trade routes which extended
from Mexico to Panama and even farther, is such an important
region archaeologically that the clarification of its pre-Columbian
history should not long be delayed.

PART Il. ARCHAEOLOGICAL RECONNAISSANCE

The following section presents the results of various field sur-
veys made by the writer in the fall and winter of 1941-42 and by
Mr. Stanley Boggs in 1940-42. For the purposes of the survey, the
Republic of El Salvador was divided into two parts, Boggs sur-
veying as much of the central and western section (Departments
of Ahuachapan, Santa Ana, Sonsonate, La Libertad, Chalatenango,
San Salvador, La Paz, Cuscutlan, Cabañas, and San Vicente) as his
time permitted, while the writer covered the trans-Lempa region
(Departments o£ Usulutan, San Miguel, Morazan, and Ta Union).
The material is presented alphabetically, by Departments of the
Republic.

With a few exceptions, all the sites listed below were actually
visited and studied. Some, however, such as Cibuatan and El
Ermitaño, though not personally investigated, are included here,
because the writers possessed new or otherwise not casily available
information concerning them. Such ruins are distinguished from
those visited by an asterisk (*). Alternative spellings or names of
sites are included parenthetically after the most common name.

1 DEPARTMENT OF AHUACHAPAN

+Isla El Cajete or Barra de Santiago. Location, A small island
in a shallow bay on the coast, in the jurisdiction of Jujutla.

Rovrr. By boat from La Libertad along the coast, airplane to
the beach near Aldea Santiago, or by horse-back from Apaneca
or Salcoatitan, which towns may be reached by auto from
Ahuachapan.

Data, Cut stone objects, pottery, and some mounds are said to
exist here. Apparently, the stone objects relate to those of Cara
Sucia. The only pottery observed was a stamped Maya flask, found
in 1912 by a telegraph inspector named Caceres and presented to
Robert Kuhn of Santa Ana (pl. XIII, 10). Similar vessels have
been reported from Copan, Honduras,! the Guatemala highlands,?
and eastern El Salvador.*

2. DEPARTMENT OF CABAÑAS

San Franciseo del Monte, Locattoy. A town 20 km. northeast
af Tabasco. This is a very mountainous and, for El Salvador,
barren area.

Route. By horse-hack from Tobasco (one-half day trip). Hobasco
is easily reached from San Rafael los Cedros on the Pan-American
highway, requiring about three hours from San Salvador.

Data, Large pyramidal mounds under the modern town. Some
of these mounds are over 6 m. in height. No clear plazas or courts
could be made out, as the mounds themselves are on very uncven
ground, Eight mounds were counted. Pottery and obsidian chips
ate very plentiful on the surface,

3. DEPARTMENT OF CHALATENANGO

*El Ermitaño. Location. A cave in the hill of Ocotal, in the
jurisdiction of Dulce Nombre de Maria. The latter town is 24
km, west-northwest of the city of Chalatenango, while Ocotal is
3 kim, north of Dulce Nombre.

Rove, There are three routes: (a) By horse-back from the city

Rovre. By auto to Hobasco (Department of Cabañas), thence

across river or upriver to ruins,

4. DEPARTMENT OF CUSCATLAN

*La Haciendita. Location. A hacienda on Rio Chanqueso, near

Rourx. By auto on the Pan-American highway to kilometer

Data. ‘The site includes several sub-groups of mounds of which

Mound construction is of two types: adobe block cores, with
wall facings and floors covered with cement-like mortar, and
talpetate masonry walls apparently covered with mortar. The
structures include terraced pyramidal temple bases, rooms, and
walled courts. At least four periods of building activity are mani-
fest in the adobe structures and two in the stone platform of
Mound 8,

Carved stone figures and tenoned parrot or serpent heads are
associated with the adobe construction but may not have been
made by the adobe builders. Two burials with ceramic and stone
grave furniture were found, as well as several caches, one contain-
ing finely carved jadeite ornaments. Much pottery was found scat-
tered over the ruins, in the adobe blocks, and in stratified deposits
below the clevated South Plaza and near Mound 8.

La Presa. Location. On the Hacienda La Presa, 26 km, north-
west of Santa Tecla in the jurisdiction of Clilamatal, southwest
of Hacienda Zapotitan and about 4 km. south of the Pan-American
highway.

way for 21.5 kim, thence by a road through. Hacienda Zapotitan
to Hacienda La Presa.

Lanp Owner. Señoras doña Nini de Meza Ayau and doña Irma

El Recreo. Location. On Finca El Recreo, along the upper

Route, By auto from Santa Tecla via the Pan-American high-
way to the private road to Finca El Recreo.

Data. Mounds, isolated and in small groups, near the river. One

San Pedro, Location. On Hacienda San Pedro, near and in the

Rourr. By anto from Santa Tecla via the Pan-American high-

burned adobe blocks are also visible. Fragments of stone “club
heads” have been found. In the hacienda house and in Mr, Soundy's
collection in Santa Tecla are examples of these and also figurines
and whistles. Other mounds have been reported on the hacienda,
but these are unconfirmed.

Sitio del Niño. Location, On Hacienda Sitio del Niño, about

Data. Onc large group and some isolated mounds. Pottery and

Zapotitan. Location. This hacienda is about 21 km. northwest

Data. Large numbers of mounds in three or more groups. The

pana-San Andres Group, but there are exceptions. One Zapotitan
group contains about twenty-five mounds. Much pottery and some
stone fragments are found on the surface.

San Felipe. Location. On Finca San Felipe, 8-10 km. west-
southwest of the town of Opico, on the west bank of the Rio

Santa Emilia. Location. On Finca Santa Emilia, west-southwest

Tula. Location. On Hacienda Tula, in the jurisdiction of San

Rourke. By auto from Santa Tecla to the settlement of Fl Jiote,

Dara, “La Sabana” possesses good soil, and a constant spring

6. DEPARTMENT OF LA PAZ

*Santiago Nonualeo. Location. A town q km, west of Zacate-
coluca, situated in a wide sandy plain,

Route, By auto from Zacatecoluca,

Dara, Sol” states that the plain south of Santiago, which he calls
the plain of Talponga or Jalponga, was inhabited by the Nonualoo
Indians. On this plain are found many crudely carved stone figures
and quantitics of coarse pottery. The stone figures are most com-
monly of monkeys, either cating. or riding on the back of a human,
but more rarely they are figures of men dressed in ceremonial cos
tumes. This same type of sculpture, Sol notes, is found in other
locations, such as near Antiguo Cuscatlan, Talnique, and coastal
points between Nonualco and La Libertad.

Jiménez* states that in Chacaztal, near Santiago Nonualco, are
ruins of a large Indian town, which was probably that of Numulcu,
noted by Velasco in 1571.

7- DEPARTMENT OF LA UNION

Conchagua Vieja. Location. On the northeastern side of Isla
Conchaguita, in the Gulf of Fonseca (pl. 1, 7). The site is on a
ridge 150 m. above sca level and about 1 km. inland from the
beach (fig. 2).

Route. To the city of La Union by auto or train, thenee by
launch to Isla Conchaguita. A boat can be rented from the Em-
presa “Carias” of La Union for about ten dollars for the round

northeast part of the island, a good trail leads south to the ruins.

Data. The site consists of a large number of stone house mounds
and the standing walls of an old colonial church (pl. 1, 8, 9). The
latter faces west on a small plaza, with a cross-stand at the far end
of the plaza (pl. I, 10). The construction of the church and cross-
stand is crude brick covered with mortar. The ruins were exten-
sively planted to maizillo at the time of the writer's visit, so that

details of the arrangement of house mounds could not be observed.

Large numbers of potsherds and several metates and obsidian
blades were found on the surface around the ruins. Sherds and
obsidian were also observed in the banks of the trail a short dis-
tance from the beach (pl. 1, 77). The great majority of pottery from
Conchagua Vieja is a coarse plain brown ware. Very few decorated
sherds were found. Many of the sherds recovered {rom the banks of
the trail, however, were painted, and almost all of these were finer
in paste and technique than those from the site itself,

Remarks. Conchagua Vieja is an excellent contact site with a
definite historical background. On June 22, 1586, Fray Alonso
Ponce preached in the church here during his journey from Nicara-

*Teca. Location. On the northern side of Isla Conchaguita,
in the Gulf of Fonseca, The site lies about 100 m. back from shore,
some 20 m. above sea level (fig. + and pl. I, 7).

ROUTE. See under “Conchagua Vieja” for the best means of
reaching Isla Conchaguita. From the landing beach, a trail about 2

Dara. Although not actually visited by the writer, the site could
plainly be seen from the boat as we neared the island. As far as
could be noted, the remains of Teca consist of several low terraces
and mounds of stone in a poor condition of preservation. The
area of the sito has been cleared of trees and bush,

Remarks. Teca is a contact site, having been inhabited at the
time of Fray Alonso Ponce's visit to the island in 1586.

8. DEPARTMENT OF MORAZAN

Cerro Coroban. Location, Cerro Coroban is the most western

Rourk. From San Francisco Gotera north and cast 4.5 km. by

auto to Lolotiquillo, The summit of Cerro Coroban is about 4

tain directly from Lolotiquillo and the other ascending the north-
west slope, reached from a point on the trail from Lolotiquillo to

Dara. On the summit of Cerra Coroban can be seen traces of

Remarks. This is undoubtedly a small fortress site, The lack
of water on or near the summit of Cerro Coroban makes the laca-
tion unsuitable for habitation, and this is supported by the ab-

Santa Rosa (Pueblo Viejo). Locarios. On the Finca Santa

Las Moritas. Locwrton. An atchacological zone with its center
about 16 km. south of the city of San Miguel.

Rourr. By car south from San Miguel on the road to Playa de
Cuca.

Dara. Las Moritas is a small archaeological zone, extending in
a valley along both sides of the road for a distance of about 3 km.
In this zone have been found painted pottery (pls, VIL, VIII, and
TX} and stone, a few tombs, and some mounds. The only mounds
known to the writer are a group of low structures, east of the road,
on the property of don Feliz Ghaves. Surface sherds collected near
these mounds are all of wares found commonly at Los Llanitos
(see Part 111, Section 5).

Los Llanitos. Location. This site is 22 km. south of the city of
San Miguel, on the west side of the road to Playa de Cuca, and in
the jurisdiction of La Canoa.

Route, By car south of San Miguel on the road to Playa de
Cuca, The site lies in a pasture about 500 m. west of the road and
1 kin. south of the village of Los Llanitos (fig. 18).

Lanp Owner. Señor don Eusebio Padilla of Santiago de Maria.

Data. A ball court and symmetrical group of mounds of stone
and earth construction. Much pottery and stone (fig. 19 and pl. 1,
13, 14, 15).

Remarks, This site is discussed at length in Part III of this
Memoir, which treats of the excavations undertaken there,

Quelepa. Location. The center of this archaeological zone lies
north of the village of Quelepa, 8 km. northwest of San Miguel.
‘The ruins follow the valley of the Rio San Esteban or Moncagua.

Rovre, By car from San Miguel to the west, on the old road
leading to Quelepa and Moncagua.

Laso Owners. Various persons, as the site extends over a num-
her of different haciendas. Hacienda Obrajuelo, on which the most
important ruins appear to be located, is owned by Señor don Julio
H. Avila of San Salvador.

Dara. Quelepa is without doubt the most important site now
known in easter El Salvador and figures largely in all collections
from that region. Judging by collections alone, the ruins appear
to cover a long space of time and contain evidence of several dil
ferent. cultures, the pottery wares ranging from true Usulutan,
evidently quite common, to specimens of decided Mexican influ-
ence, The archacological zone extends from Moncagua east to at
least Hacienda Obrajuelo, which is about 2 km. east of the village
ol Quelepa. Architecturally, the site is not impressive, only a few
low terraces and mounds having been located. Natives report the
existence of stone roads and tunnels as well, but these have not
been confirmed. Several tombs have been found, especially on
Hacienda El Salitre.

Remarks, The best collections from Quelepa in eastern El Sal-
vador are those in the possession of Dr. Atilio Peccorini, who first
reported on the site,** and Señor don Inocente Aguirre of Quelepa.
Coronel Montalbo, Commandante of Usulutan, has a few very
fine stone objects from here (pls. VII, VILL, IX, and XIE). Sherds
picked up in the zone by the writer include specimens of Usulutan,
fine line polychrome, and Nicoya polychrome wares, besides com-
mon utility wares, supposedly indigenous to the site.

Rio Ojos de Agua. Location, South of San Miguel 4.7 km.

10. DEPARTMENT OF SAN SALVADOR

Acelhuate. Location. On the Oriente railroad line from San
Salvador, at a cut in the bank of Rio Acelhuate, about g km. east

Rovtr, By auto via the Pan-American highway to the eastern

huate bridge.

Dara, Lothrop illustrates this deep cut? and states: “In April of
1924 Professor Lardé was good enough to tell the writer of these
discoveries and to take him to a deep cut on the railroad above the
Rio Acelhuate where potsherds could be seen in the underlying
humus and pertinent geological strata could be observed." The
writer visited this site in 1940 and again in 1942 and on neither

stratum indicated by Lothrop. It is possible that in cightcen years
cnough crosion has occurred here to wash away any sherds which

the similarity of the cut in 1924 and 1942 would seem to preclude

*Cihuatan (Sihuatan, Ciguatan, or Siguatan). Location, On
the top of an isolated hill, clongated east and west, about 4 km.
from the house of Hacienda San Diego. The hacienda is in the
jurisdiction of El Paisnal, a town 48 km, north of San Salvador,

Data. A short excavation in 1929, under the auspices of the
Department of History, Ministry of Public Instruction, of the Fl
Salvadoran Government produced nearly all the information now
available with regard to the ruins of Cihuatan. Señor don Antonio
E. Sol, then Director of the Department of History and leader of
the expedition, has written several short reports, and Señor don
Augusto Baratta, now Director of the Museo Nacional, drew a plan
of the ruins and sketched several structures and details of con-
struction.**

The hill on which the ruins are found has an area of about 80
manzanas and may be climbed from any side, It is limited on the
east by the Rio Acelhuate, which certainly furnished water for the
ancient inhabitants.

Three extensive plazas, at least partially enclosed by masonry
(terraced ?) walls, and a ball court were found, as well as an unex-
plored area of ruins lying south of the mapped portion (fig. 4).
Excavations, so far as it is possible to determine, were restricted
to the “Plaza de los Sacrificios,” the largest of the enclosures, and
to some structures immediately east of this.

ov altars but were not exposed. A boundary wall of boulders was

plaza boundary was found along the other three sides. wo niches
or indentations in this boundary were noted near the south end
of the west wall, but apparently the entrance to the plaza was never
sought.

mame, The mound is also locally referred to as “La Pyramide.”

large, flat stones. On the pavement surrounding this edifice were

North of Plaza 11 and east of Mound 1 are three mounds, of

Construction, which was studied on the sides and steps of Mound
1, the “Templo de los Idolos,” the walls of the “Plaza de los
Sacrificios,” and in an excavation made atop Mound 1 by treasure
hunters, seems to have been entircly of talpetate blocks or slabs
with squared faces, generally laid in regular horizontal courses
and bound with mortar. An exception may be noted in the basal
construction of the “Templo de los Idolos,” which combines the
horizontal-course masonry of the cornice with slabs set on end in
mortar to form a battered-wall foundation. In many places, stone-

"The Museo    jomal has a few pieces of thick-walled pottery, decorated with

_Extraction method: OCR._

16                                     ARCHAEOLOGICAL INVESTIGATIONS IN EL SALVADOR

work was found partially covered with a very hard plaster, which
Sol believes to have been compounded of ground marine shells and
pumice, Large quantities of both were found about the ruins.
Core construction, of Mound 1 at least, was of talpetate slabs and
boulders (in adobe?).

Pottery was apparently very plentiful at Cihuatan, although al-
inast none of it is now available for study. Scattered on and around
the “Templo de los Idolos,” for example, were more than seventeen
broken zoómorphic clay figures of the type shown in plate XIII,
ga. and bh, These Sol considered representative of a phallic cult."*
“Tlaloc faces” were found "in a place which seemed destined for
games or a point of reunion of their assemblics," and figurines
were found above the steps of Mound 1. Aside from these brief
notes, Sol gives no description of his finds, Today, only about a
dozen pottery fragments and a cast of one of the zoómorphic clay
figures are found in the Museo Nacional, while a small case in the
Instituto Nacional contains a few more specimens.

Remarks, With so little information, especially on pottery, it is
obviously dangerous to attempt the assignation of Cihuatan to any
particular culture or period, It is perhaps safe to say, however, that
the blue-painted Tlaloc faces, undoubtedly from small jars, are
probably of the same period as the zodmorphic figures, which were
found on and around (that is to say, in a late position) the “Templo
de los Idolos.” Unfortunately, the date of the El Salvadoran version
of the Tlaloc vase is not yet proved. In the writers opinion, the
relatively coarse type, usually painted blue, and found late in El
Salvador ruins, developed from the hard brown polished type (pl.
XIII, 12) which is clearly associated with the slab-leg incised vases
found in carly strata at Tazumal and considered directly related
to Teotihuacan via Kaminaljuyu (Esperanza period), In no Fl
Salvadoran ruin, as far as the writer knows, are the hard brown
and the coarse blue-painted types associated in the same stratum.
The Tlaloc faces at Cibuatan, therefore, suggest that this was a
late site, but they represent only a late period here and may not be
at all connected with actual construction phases.

11. DEPARTMENT OF SAN VICENTE

San Francisco, Location. On Hacienda San Francisco. The
hacienda is in the locally termed Valle San Francisco, along several
of the upper tributaries of the Rio Amate, about 5 km. east of the
Tehuacan railroad station and 6-7 km. northeast of the town of
Tecoluca, The ruins lie on a small flat arca about 20 m. above
the “Rio” San Francisco and 100 m, south of the hacienda house,
on both sides of a cart road,

‘This is the southeast slope of the Volcan San Vicente (Chichum-
tepet) and is now largely cattle country, with very little brush.
Altitude could not be measured, but it is probably slightly more
than 450 m. above sea level. On clear days the ocean and coastal
swamps can be seen from the site.

Route. By auto from San Vicente to Hacienda La Purisima
Concepcion or Hacienda Opico, or by train to Tehuacan station,
thence to Hacienda San Francisco by horse-back or foot, about a
half-hour trip from Tehuacan station. This station is 12 km. south
of San Vicente, and can also be reached by auto.

Lann Ownnr. Señor don Carlos Meyer of Cojutepeque.

Dara, This untouched site, in the same geographical arca and
about 15 km, cast of the Tehuacan ruins, consists of at least eleven
low mounds. As well as could be determined by sighting with a
Brunton compass, they are oriented 18 degrees east of magnetic
north. The accompanying sketch map (fig. 5) was made with the
compass and a surveyor’s chain.

Mounds 1-5 are the most prominent and may represent the bases
of small temples. Mounds ro and 11, together with their north and

quite clear, but the exterior measurements are somewhat obscure,
partially due to the fact that a shallow irrigation ditch runs along
the north and east sides and has undoubredly cut away some of the
original structure. Mounds 6-4 may represent low house platforms,
altars, or tombs. Approximate heights of the mounds are as follows

All mounds seein to be constructed of water-worn boulders and
slabs set in adobe mortar. No plaster was exposed, Near the north
end of Mound 11, a north-south wall line of small boulders could
be seen near the long axis of the structure, while toward the south
end of the same mound, an eastern slope of boulder paving was
noted. This may indicate that the exterior slopes of the ball court

zone here quite completely. The entire ball court appears at pres-

this may be due to erosion filling the enclosed area with debris
from the boundary mounds.

Potsherds were not at all plentiful on the surface, The few seen
were almost entirely of plain red-slipped or plain grey domestic
wares. A few red-painted on light cream slip sherds were found.
As far as could be determined, only one complete vessel has been
found at the site. This isa smal! brown ware olla with globular body,
funnel neck, and two vertical loop handles on the upper curve of
the body. This vessel, in the possession of a workman on the nearby
Hacienda La Purisima Concepcion, closely resembles in form the
so-called Lenca jugs of eastern El Salvador.

12. DEPARTMENT OF SANTA ANA

CHALCHIUAPA ZONE. Locarion. On the east and north sides
of the city of Chalchuapa, 16 km. west-southwest of Santa Ana, in
the valley of the Rio Pampe (Rio Chalebuapa). This is a rich agri-
cultural valley and, though not as large as the valleys of Zapotitan
or the lower Lempa, capable of supporting a very large popula-
tion. The elevation of the Pampe Valley is G50 m. above sea level.
‘The following five sites are included in this zone.

Route. Chalchuapa may be reached by auto from Santa Ana via
the Santa Ana-Abuachapan highway, or by railroad.

Casa Blanca Group. Location. On Finca Casa Blanca, at the
northeast corner of Chalchuapa, along the north side of the Santa
Ana-Ahuachapan highway (fig. 6).

Rovre. By auto or on foot from Chalchuapa.

Lanp Ownrr. Dr. Ruben Rodriguez y Castro.

Data. This group is, in number of mounds, the largest in the
Chalchuapa Zone but is one of the least known, archacologically.
Lardé noted the existence here of fifteen mounds, of which eight
were large.” In April, 1942, the writer counted sixtecn mounds
and was informed by an employce of the finca that other smaller
structures lay both east and west of these. In addition, to the west,
between the mounds and the road from Chalchuapa to Rio Pampe,
are flat areas which have produced many sherds and some complete
polychrome vessels, some of the latter containing burned human
bones. Some of the sherds found on these flats are from incensarios,
while others appear to be fragments of monochrome burial urns.

The principal mounds of this group lic in an area approximately
goo m. wide east and west and extending about 0.5 km. northward
from the railroad tracks. Due to the absence of the finca owner,
no measurements could be taken here, although a cursory inspec-
tion of the site was mad,

No clearly defined plazas were scen, The three largest mounds
have their long axes north and south and are about 10 m. high and
30 by 25 m, in base dimension. All the other mounds are consider-
ably smaller. Construction is basically of adobe and stone, but no
facing bas been exposed. Two mounds bave been partially exca-
vated, but the others are apparendy untouched.

A plain stone shaft was recently discovered near the base of one
mound. It is set vertically in the ground and is broken at its upper
end. The monolith measures about 1m, in width on its broadest
side. To the west is a similar shalt, lying horizontally. The latter
may be a fragment broken from the vertical stone, or perhaps a
displaced part of the basal construction around the shaft. No traces
of carving were found, so the shalt, if originally a stela, was either
plain or painted.

Remarks, Stone sculptures known to be from this group are a
chac mool, now in the Museo Nacional, and several stylized jaguar
heads and human-elligy heads in the garden of Finca Casa Blanca.
Photographs of the chac-mool figure haye already appeared in the
literature,* and the jaguar heads are of a type illustrated by Rich-
ardson

Probably much of the pottery in the Corso, Mendoza, and
Mathcis collections is from the Casa Blanca Group, as workmen on
the finca ate known to have sold many vessels excavated while
cleaning around the coffce trees. It is impossible, however, to de-
termine which specimens in these collections are from Casa Blanca,
and which are from the Tazumal or El Trapiche Groups. Only
Utree vessels seen by the writer are definitely from Casa Blanca.
All were found in the flats west of the mounds, and two, at least,

El Trapiche Group. Location. On Finca San Antonio are six
mounds and near the Beneficio Las Victorias is a seventh. Both
the finca and the beneficio are slightly less than 2 km. north-north-
east of Chalchuapa, near the Rio Pampe.

Roure. By auto or foot from Chalchuapa,

T.ann Owners, Finca San Antonio is owned by Señor don Abram

Dara. The terrain surrounding the El Trapiche Group is very
flat and, since most of the mounds are in cut-over pasture land,
their sizes and outlines are easily determined (fig. 8). Mounds 1-3
have not been seriously disturbed, but all the others have been
more or less plundered by treasure hunters, coffee workers, or peo-
ple in search of building material.

Mound 1 is perhaps the largest single mound in western El
Salvador and probably represents a pyramidal temple substructure
(pl. XUL 75). Ms location and size suggest that it forms the nor-
thern pole of the Chalchuapa Zone's axis, with Mound 1 of the
Tazumal Group as the southern pole, The other mounds of the

No complete stone or pottery specimens are known from this
group, although workmen on the Finca San Antonio informed the
writer that several ollas were excavated from Mound 6 and either

the surface and all appear to be types common to the later phase
of Tazumal. Fragmentary stone “club heads” and footed metates
and other objects of common use are also found.

REMARES. A stone figure which Richardson notes as a chac-mool
type* and which the Museo Nacional considered a couchant jaguar
has for many years been attributed to the El ‘Trapiche Group.
Upon further examination, this sculpture has been found to have
come from Finca El Socorro, that is, to be from the Tazumal
Group, probably from near Mound 6.**

SALVADOR

GUIJA ZONE. Location. On the shores and islands of Lake
Guija, on the Guatemalan frontier. This area is generally moun-
tainous, in most cases the hills coming steeply down to the shores
of the lake. Igualtepeque and Teotipa are small, hilly islands.
Azacualpa, on the other hand, is a relatively flat bluff between the
Volcan San Diego and the northeast shore of the lake (fig. 9), The
three sites included in this zone are given below.

Roure. By auto or train from Santa Ana to the El Desague rail-
road station at the east end (and outlet) of the lake. From here, a
boat may be taken to Teotipa and Igualtepeque, and one may
visit Azacualpa by cither boat or horse-back,

Azacualpa, Location. On a bluff rising above the northeast
shore of Lake Guija, slightly cast of north from Igualtepeque
Island (fig. 9)-

Dara, Azacualpa consists of three groups of mounds numbered,
from east to west, I to LL.

Group I comprises a large plaza with three small mounds along
the west and south sides (Mounds 1-3), and ten low platforms, eight
along the north and east sides and two slightly west of the center
of the plaza (fig. 10). The mounds are not over 3 m. in height, and

the platforms are never over 0.60 m. Structure 4, slightly larger
in area than the other platforms, shows a step on the west side,
probably the foundation of the construction. ‘This step or base is
paved with slate-like stone slabs covering a rubble core. The plat-
forms along the east side of the plaza were extremely difficult to
see, due to corn stalks and tall grass covering the arca. It is pos-
sible, therefore, that excavation would show more of these struc-
tures than are indicated on the map. Very little pottery could be
seen on the surface here, and no excavation has been done at this
group.

Group 11 is about midway between Groups I and ITI and slightly
north of an imaginary line connecting the latter two. It consists
entirely of low platforms arranged with little apparent regard to
plazas or other formal plan. The writer was able to count six cer-
tain mounds (fig. 11) and noted a few dubious rises to the north of
these. None of the mounds has been excavated,

Group HI is the only true mound group at Azacualpa (fig. 12).
With the possible exception of a small rise north of Mound 6,
nothing here appears to represent a platform of the type seen in
Groups I and IL.

Mounds 1-3 are g to 4 m. high, and Mound 8 is about 5 m. high.
All the others vary between 1 and 2.50 m. in height. All were con-
structed of slabs of coarse volcanic stonc, with roughly cut faces,
laid in courses to form terrace walls covering boulder-and-adobe
cores, a system exactly comparable to that of the Isla Igualtcpeque
ruin (pl. XIII, 1). With the exception of Mound 8, no excavation
has been done here.

## Page 28

_Extraction method: OCR._

ARCHAEOLOGICAL RECONNAISSANCE,                                                 2

Mound 8, by far the largest structure in the group, is a terraced
temple substructure, On the north side, six terraces could be made
out, but large holes have been dug into the east and west sides,
obliterating the construction at these points. Except that larger
boulders were used here in facing the terraces, construction is like
that of the other Azacualpa mounds and Igualtepeque. Above the
topmost terrace, covering perhaps a third of the top of the struc-
ture, is a floor of coarse, soft lime plaster. It is likely that all walls
and floors were covered with this material as, in a few places on the
northern terraces, fragments of it can be seen. No steps were found
during the excavation of cither the west or east sides, according to
local people. The south side, which is yet unexcavated, may possess
these.

Isla Igualtepeque. Location. Ruins on Igualtepeque Island, at
the east end and near the outlet of Lake Guija.

Laso Owner, Señor don Flavio Valiente.

Dara. While noted in various site lists," and in various short
newspaper and imagazine articles," no description at all detailed
has yet appeared concerning this site.

Isla Igualtepeque is roughly oval, with its long axis approxi-
mately north and south, and it lies very near the castern shore of
Lake Guija, near the outlet. The architectural remains are situated
on a flat saddle near the center of the island, between two hills,
and consist of two mounds within a rectangular, walled enclosure,
with terraces rising from the lake shores on the west and south
sides of the island to the level of this plaza (fig. 13).

‘The only portion of the site even partially excavated is the west
side of Mound 1 (pl. XTIL 4) where a stairway and one terrace has
been exposed, Construction here is of boulder-and-adobe core
faced with a very hard, white lime mortar, in places 4 cm. thick
(pl. XTIT, 8). The terrace occurs about half-way up the side of the
mound, which is approximately 4 m. high; consequently, at least
one other      ace probably exists above that now exposed.

Mound 2 has not been excavated and suggests an altar by its
small size and clevation, as well as by its position. Both mounds
ave located in a rectangular plaza bounded by a low stone wall,
broken in places possibly by the original constructors. The wall
is made of flat slabs and rough boulders of scoriaceous stone (pl
XML 7), On the west side of the plaza, this wall rises very abruptly
from the edge of the saddle, presenting a nearly vertical face from
the outside, Here it has been built up from some distance below
the plaza level to give an impression of great height. Elsewhere the
wall is quite low, seldom exceeding 1 m, above the present ground
level of the plaza.

The approachterraces mentioned above are at present largely
evergrown, but the upper three terrace faces north of the plaza
show construction identical with that of the plaza wall. Both the
plaza wall and the abruptness of the terraces suggest that this site
was deliberately fortified,

Very few objects of pottery have been reported from Igualte-
peque, One fragmentary figurine appears to be of Maya type, simi
lar to those found in quantity in ash deposits near San Salvador

(with Maya polychrome pottery), as well as in the Chalchuapa
Zone.

Stone objects from the ruins consist entirely of broken circular
“mace heads" and fragments of metates and manos. On the north-
west shore of the island, at the base of a volcanic stone outcrop
which forms the northern hill, are a number of large boulders
profusely carved with pictographs. At least two hundred carvings

Dara, This small site has been known for many years, but as

All the mounds are low and much eroded. They are possibly
house mounds but are quite distinct from the platform types of
Azacualpa. Mounds 1 and 2 are the largest and measure approxi-
matcly 2.40 m. in height. No other mounds are more than 1.50 m.
high, and they are gencrally lower than this, Mound 5 is so dis-
proportionately long that the writer is not certain it is ancient,
though Señor Valiente assured him it was not made during his
ownership of the hacienda. Mounds 1 and 10 have been excavated
from their south sides. The only construction visible here is of
boulders set in adobe mortar. No facings of slabs or cut stones are
apparent.

Five complete pottery vessels have been found here. One is a
small brown ware jar with an effigy face modeled on the neck
(similar to pl, VII, 7), and another is a miniature, ted-slipped
bowl. There are three polychrome bowls, one apparently from a
burial in Mound 10, and the others from Mound 1 and from exca-
vations along the highway. Two of these are similar in shape and
design, one being pictured in plate XIL, 772, 6. This latter vessel
is painted in hematite red, orange, and black on a light orange
slip and is almost identical with other bowls found at San Andres,
San Salvador, Chalchuapa, and Quelepa. This type is also very
similar to vessels of the Acropolis Phase at Copan, Honduras.”

mounds and the amount of debris estimated to have fallen. The
construction of the site was such that not even outside corners of
substructures could be determined without extensive excavation
and, conscquently, this was not attempted except on mounds
chosen for special study. The latter mounds are marked with an
asterisk in the table of measurements,

‘The principal mounds of Los Llanitos are grouped around a
plaza with a north-south axis. To the east of this assemblage lie a
few other mounds, mostly of the low, rectangular house-mound
type. Thus, although the site is definitely a unit, its scattered ap-
pearance presents a very informal aspect.

The site is very definitely oriented, most of the mounds having
their long axes running north and south and deviating from true
north by about 12 degrees east. The few mounds which run east
and west are arranged approximately at right angles to the others.
Wherever possible, bearings were taken with a transit along
exposed corners or walls, but even under the most favorable condi-
tions, absolutely accurate bearings were impossible, since the con-
struction of the site was not conducive to straight walls or definite
corners.

Potsherds, fragments of obsidian, etc., are found over a large
area surrounding the site itself, although outlying mounds appear
to be lacking. An especially heavy surface deposit of sherds was
located near the southwest corner of the small corn field north of
the site, but time, unfortunately, did not permit an excavation to
determine if this deposit represented a sub-surface rubbish heap.
Other sherds were noted in the fields cast of the automobile road
to Playa de Guca and on the small knoll northwest of the site.

About 500 m, due west of the ruins is a rather definite and
regular oval depression, measuring some 40 m. on its long axis.
‘The walls of this depression were cut in part into the bed rock,
which is pumice in this region. I seems very likely that this hollow
is a result of quarrying activities by the ancient inhabitants of Los
Llanitos, forall the stone used in the mounds is pumice. Here again,
however, lack of time prevented excavation which might have
confirmed this theory.

ARCHAEOLOGICAL INVESTIGATIONS IN EL SALVADOR

PREVIOUS HISTORY AND EXCAVATION

Prior to the writers first visit to Los Llanitos in November,
1941, the site was unknown to archaeologists and had never ap-
peared in literature pertaining to El Salvador, Residents of the
city of San Miguel spoke in a general way of ruins lying to the
south, but as there are several different groups in that region, no
effort was made by them to differentiate these by name or definite
location.

‘The Los Llanitos mounds have been known to some people for
many years, and, of course, have excited a certain amount of
curiosity among the inhabitants of the river valley in which they
lie. There is good reason to believe that many of the stone slabs,
with which the site is plentifully supplied, have been appropriated
in past years by the modern natives for various domestic purposes.
Since humus and debris are practically non-existent on the upper
slopes of the mounds, constructional stone would readily be ex-
posed by any attempts at cultivation, and as easily carted away to
serve as door-post stones, flooring, etc. Only the unsuitability of
the pumice, used exclusively in the construction of the site, for
metates saved the mounds from worse vandalism.

A certain amount of legend has become associated with the
ruins in the minds of the present-day inhabitants, and, for this
reason, little actual excavation was attempted before the writer's
efforts in the carly part of 1942. Judging from the talk of laborers
and ordinary farmers, Los Llanitos appears to possess, in common
with other sites in that region, a guardian spirit in the form of a
tiger or jaguar, capable of harming anyone molesting the ruins.
Luckily, the writers workmen were selected mainly from the
nearby hacienda or from road gangs and were sophisticated enough
to laugh at these tales.

Only one mound at Los Llanitos had actually becn dug into
before the writer began excavations, This was Mound 4, a fair-
sized conical structure. In August, 1941, an unidentified man,
looking for “treasure,” had dug a hole some 114 m. in diameter into
the center of the mound from the east (pl. 1, 25). Finding only
fragments of pottery and a single plain vessel, he abandoned the
excavation and desisted from exploring any of the other mounds.

The writer first visited Los Llanitos early in November, 1941,
and made five subsequent trips to the site between that time and
the start of excavation, one of these trips being for the purpose of
making a map of the ruins. Excavation was begun on February
3» 1942, and continued until March 15 of the same year. Con-
sidering that only six weeks were spent in actual digging, the
results of the project were more than could ordinarily have been
expected, The reasons for these were first, the richness of the
site, especially in ceramic remains, and secondly, the willingness
of the workers and their remarkably quick grasp of archaeological
technique.

2. DETAILED DESCRIPTION OF RUINS

The greater part of excavating time at Los Llanitos was spent
on the ball court, the outstanding structure of the site and of
especial interest, because it extended the previously known south-
ern range of this type of architecture. The most southern ball
court known to archacologists up to this time was that reported
by Sapper from Leon de Piedra, on the cast slope of Volcan San
Vicente, just west of the Rio Lempa in El Salvador

Only three mounds, aside from the ball court, were investi-

‘Sapper, 1897. Leon de Piedra is today better known by the name of Tehuacan.

gated, but, unfortunately, time did not permit a complete excava-
tion in any case. The construction of the mounds was so unusual,
at least to the writer, and the general state of preservation so poor,
that all digging of necessity proceeded very slowly. Different types
of mounds, furthermore, were chosen for investigation in order
to gain as complete a picture of the site as possible; construction
varied considerably from mound to mound and prevented the
drawing of any general architectural conclusions, In the following
discussion, the excavated mounds will be considered separately and
such generalities as are possible will be taken up at the end.

## Page 34

_Extraction method: OCR._

EXCAVATIONS AT LOS LLANITOS

BALL COURT

The ball court lies at the northern extremity of the mound
complex of Los Llanitos and the structure as a whole is aligned
12 degrees 10 minutes east of north (fig. 19). Before excavation, few
structural details were visible. The court appeared as two long
mounds (Mounds 1 and 2), sloping gently and cvenly on all sides,
with here and there a few rough blocks of pumice showing above
the surface (pl. Il, 4). There was a markedly depressed area, rec-
tangular in shape, to the north of the mounds, but the ground
to the south extended without a break into the plaza bounded by
Mounds 3, 4, 5, 6, and Ga. The mounds and surrounding area
were uniformly covercd with short grass, interspersed with a few
low bushes.

The excavated court measured 58.20 m. in length from end wall
to end wall, and 26.60 m. in width, the latter measurement being
taken across the south end court. The mounds were 34.90 m. long
and 9.60 m. wide at the original ground level? but had varying
heights, duc to the disintegration of the structures. The maximum
and minimum heights of Mound 1 were 2.15 m. and 1.74 m.,
respectively, while those of Mound 2 were 2.28 m. and 1.57 m.

‘The playing alley, i-c., the area between the two mounds, was
7.60 m. in width. The possibility of the presence of a low bench,
or terrace, extending out from the mounds from their inner sides
and thus encroaching upon the playing surface will be discussed
later. If these terraces did exist, the one extending from Mound 2
measured 2.65 m. in width, while that from Mound 1 was only
1,85 m. in width. The playing alley would then have been only
3-50 m. wide,

‘The end zones, or areas north and south of the mounds
bounded by the low end walls, were evidently meant to be equal
in size (fig. 20). The south zone measured 10,70 m. in length and
25.90 m. in width. In the north zone only the length could be
obtained from the amount of excavation done there, but that
measured 10,65 m,, and it may be assumed that the width was
equally close to that of the south zone. The boundary wall of the
south end zone averaged 30 cm. in height; that of the north zonc
averaged 50 cm. Wall widths ranged from 1.00 m. on the south
to 1,40 m, or even wider on the north.

The playing alley of the ball court bore no evidence of a
mortar or stone floor, and it must be assumed that the game was
played on a dirt floor, hardened by tamping. On either side of the
playing alley rose a wall 50 cm. in height. These walls were faced
throughout their length by roughly squared stone slabs set on
end, their lower ends sunk some 20 cm. below the original surface
of the ground (pl. IL, 2, 5, 7). At the time of excavation, all these
slabs had cither fallen or broken off a short distance above the
base, and the upper portions of those so broken were frequently
found lying on the playing alley of the court.

During excavation, a stratum of small pieces of pumice and

crushed mortar was noticed extending outward from the vertical
walls into the playing alley. This stratum was most clearly defined
on the eastern side of the court where it assumed a thickness of 40
cm, at its junction with the vertical wall of Mound 2, and, from
this point, narrowed consistently as it progressed westward, until
it pinched out at a distance of 2.65 m. from the vertical wall of
Mound 2. The corresponding straturn from Mound 1 was not
nearly so well defined and extended onto the playing alley for a
distance of only 1.85 mm. (fig. 21). These two strata may possibly
represent low terraces extending out from the mounds onto the

20£ the two mounds, only Mound 2 was excwated sufficiently to be capable of
measurement. Mound a, being a complementary structure, is asstimed to have the
same dimensions as Mound 2.                                                          .

playing alley (fig. 20). Similar terraces arc almost invariably as-
sociated with ball courts in other Central American regions.*
Ordinarily, however, the terraces are equal in width. The con-
siderable discepancy between the terraces of Mounds 1 and 2 at
Los Llanitos may be due to the extreme disintegration of one, On
the other hand, it may indicate that these strata do not represent
terraces at all but are merely the remains of plaster which may, at
one time, have covered the vertical slabs bounding the playing
alley. Since, however, no trace of plaster covering was found in situ
on any slab or wall at the site, the writer is inclined to believe that
the strata actually represent the horizontal terraces.

From the tops of the walls on either side of the playing alley.
sloping terraces, faced with large, rather crudely squared pumice
slabs, extended for a distance of 3.40 m., reaching a height of
1.35 M, at their upper extremity (figs. 20, 21, and pl. II, 6). The
slabs of which the terraces were constructed averaged 15 cm. in
thickness but varied widely in length and width. Presumably, six
longitudinal rows of these slabs were required to face each terrace,
but the ravages of time and the pilferings of the natives have re-
duced these six rows to three (fig. 27), the latter being the upper:
most rows, which were covered by debris fallen from higher parts
of the mounds (pl. 11, 3). Evidently no attempt was made to lay
the slabs in regular courses, nor to fit them together with any
degree of carc.* One slab, however, was cut in such a way as to
form an angle around the corner of the adjoining slab (pl. 11, 7).
Unless it was “art for art’s sake," the reason for this single example
of careful masonry is unknown.

Above the sloping terraces the mounds continued to rise in a
vertical wall. These walls extended to a height of 1 m. above the
terraces, and the amount of debris fallen from them and now lying
on the slopes of the mounds is so small that we cannot assume their
original height to have been much greater. No trace of a slab
facing was found on these walls.

Nothing remaining of the original tops of the mounds, it was
impossible to ascertain whether buildings had been placed upon
them or not. Since no remains of stone buildings were found in
other parts of the site, however, it may be assumed that the tops
of the ball court mounds were flat, cither without structures or
bearing temporary shelters of wood and thatch.

The original appearance of the mound slopes farthest removed
from the playing alley could not be determined. Stone slabs were
not used on these surfaces, and consequently, there was no definite
line of construction for the excavator to follow. These portions of
the mounds may have been stepped down in wide terraces, al-
though it is more likely that they sloped in an unbroken line
from the top to the ground, thus conforming to the outline of
most other ball courts in Central America. No traces of stairways
were found on the outside slopes of the mounds.

The ends of the mounds appear to have sloped from the top
platform down to a level corresponding to that of the upper end
of the slab-faced terraces facing on the court. From here down
there appear to have been vertical walls. Slabs were found facing
these walls from the corners nearest the playing alley back to
approximately half the width of the mounds (fig. 20 and pls. IL,
To; IIT). These slabs were by far the largest of any found at the
site.

Like the playing alley, the end zones appear to have had floors
of hard-packed dirt. The walls bounding these zones were con-
structed of roughly squared pumice blocks, held  together by
