adobe mortar (pls. II, 22-14; III}. Fragments of stone slabs occa-
sionally were set on end and aligned with the inner face of the
wall. A gap of considerable width was found in the wall bounding
each zone (fig. 20 and pl. II, 72), that in the southern wall being
3.30 m. in width, and that in the northern wall 2.40 m. Neither
gap lay on the center line of the ball court, and no explanation
for their presence can be given. That they were not caused by the
break-down of the wall was shown by the absence of fallen stone
anywhere in their vicinity.

No trace of ball court markers, rings, or other decorative stone
was found, although excavations were made wherever these objects
were likely to occur.

Before construction of the ball court, the inhabitants of Los
Llanitos graded the surface of the ground, which at that time
sloped gently from north to south. After grading, the north end
of the court was only some 80 cm. higher than the south end.
During the course of this grading, bedrock must have been en-
countered in the northern end of the court and in places smoothed
off so as to be below the level of the hard-packed dirt playing
surface (fig. 20 and pl. 1V, z, 2).

The mounds were constructed of alternate vertical layers of
rock and dirt fill. Each mound contained four longitudinal stone
walls, constructed of pumice blocks, roughly squared for the most
part, and held together by a mortar of adobe (figs. 20, 21, and pl.
IV, 3, 7). The core of the mound was a wall 2 m. thick, extending
upward to the maximum height of the mound. Between this core
and the wall forming the mound side farthest from the playing
surface, was a smaller wall 60 cm. in width and about 1.60 m, in
height. The bases of both this wall and the boundary wall, pre-
viously mentioned, were some 70 cm. higher than the base of the
wall forming the mound core (fig. 21). On the other side of the
core only one wall was present: that forming the side of the mound
bordering on the playing alley of the court. The base of the latter
wall was at the same level as that of the core. The spaces between
the four walls were filled with a mixture of dirt, small stone
fragments, and cultural material, such as potsherds and obsidian
blades and flakes. The only exception to this was the fill found
directly under the slabs of the sloping terraces. This consisted of
line volcanic ash, rather looscly packed, and almost sterile. Here
and there throughout the sections of the mounds irregular layers
of crushed mortar and small bits of pumice were noticed (fig. 21).
The significance of these, however, could not be determined, and
their position made it extremely unlikely that they represented
floors.

The broken pieces of plaster found in the previously mentioned
strata and in the horizontal terraces extending out upon the play-
ing alley showed that the inhabitants of Los Llanitos were familiar
with this material. They evidently did not employ it in wall cover-
ing, however, for no wall or slab at the site bore traces of a plaster
coating,

STRUCTURE NORTH OF THE
BALL COURT

Just north of the wall bounding the northern end of the ball
court a low structure of rough-hewn pumice blocks extends east
and west (fig. 20 and pl. IV, 4). Since it lies wholly to the cast of
the center line of the ball court, there may have been a similar
structure to the west, but as excavation was not conducted in the
latter region, this can only be conjectural. No trace of the struc-
ture appeared above the surface, it having been completely cov-

EXCAVATIONS AT LOS LLANITOS                                                  3

ered by dirt washed down from the higher land to the north and
west.

The structure was composed of three buttress-like forms joined
by a narrow wall, the whole being 14 m. in length, with each but-

Fic. 21. Detail drawing of a section through the center of Mound 2 and the playing alley of the ball court, Los Llanitos,

tress measuring about 1.50 m. by 1.80 m. The connecting wall
varicd from go cm. to 50 cm. in width. The height of the structure
averaged 50 cm., and no loose stone was found in the vicinity to
suggest Lhat at one time it might have been higher, The construct-
tion was similar to that found elsewhere in the site: rough stone
blocks held together by adobe mortar.

Since this structure faced on the ball court, it seems probable
that the two were related in some way, but the use to which this
buttress-and-wall arrangement could have been put is very obscure.

‘The only possibility occurring to the writer is that it may have
delimited stalls, or boxes, for persons attending the games, the
low walls perhaps being supplemented by brush-and-thatch shel-
ters,

‘The space between the north boundary wall of the ball court
and the low buttresses was a veritable mine of potsherds, well over
10,000 being recovered from an area 8 m. by 2 m. All were quite
badly water-worn, indicating that they had been washed into
place from a rubbish heap originally located on the higher land
to the northwest.

MOUND 3

Mound 3 is situated just south of Mound 2 of the ball court
and is similarly aligned north and south (fig. 19). Like Mounds
4. 5. and 7, it was conical in shape before excavation, with very
little of its original construction evident, The dimensions given
(table 1) for this mound are estimates in part, since only the west
side, facing the plaza, was excavated throughout its length.

The brevity of the excavating season terminated work on
Mound 3 before very much of the construction could be deter-
mined, Along the front of the mound (the west side, facing the
plaza) ran a rough stone wall, 1.00-1.20 m. in height in the center
and sloping sharply down to 50 cm. in height at the ends (fig. 20
and pl. IV, 5). Its width was 80 cm. This wall stood as a unit, as
no traces of walls were found on the sides of the mound, The
interior of the mound, also, appearcd to be devoid of stone, the
fill being a fine-grained, almost white volcanic ash, completely
lacking in cultural material of any kind.

A brief dig on top of the mound revealed there another stone

wall, paralleling the front retaining wall described above. Behind
this upper wall extended what appeared to be a pair of buttresses
(fig. 20 and pl, IV, 6). They were so crudely constructed as to have
the appearance of random fallen stone, but they were symetrically
placed in relation to the wall and were quite equal in size, thus
supporting the above theory as to their function. The fill behind
the wall and around the buttresses consisted of clay-like soil con-
taining a great many sherds and stone artifacts.

Mound 3 probably had a flat top, the upper wall serving as a
retainer for the leveled-off fill behind it. Although it would be
natural to expect a stairway on the western side, no trace of such
a structure was found, A large mass of loose stone found just out:
side the western boundary wall, however, may have represented
the remains of a stair, demolished by earthquakes or erosion.

The most interesting find associated with Mound g was the
pointed end of a wooden post or beam, located in the stone debris
outside the lower wall (fig. 20 and pl. IV, 9). This was the only
fragment of wood found during the excavations. Some action of
nature, probably chemical, had hardened the post into a semblance
of stone, although it was not petrified, Almost certainly not in situ
when found, the post no doubt had once served to support a
shelter or canopy attached to the mound.

MOUND y

Mound 7 is located between Mounds 4 and 10 and bears ap-
proximately the same alignment as Mounds 1, 2, and 3 (fig. 19
and pl. IV, &). The dimensions listed in table 1 for this mound
are estimates, except for height, since the substructure was not
excavated.

Digging in this mound began with leveling off the top, in an
effort to find a floor, post-holes, or other evidence of a super:
structure. When it was found, however, that the workers had
breached a fairly definite wall, leveling was stopped, and the in-
terior face of the wall was traced, in the expectation of uncovering
a room.

The wall, when cleared, extended around four sides of a rec-
tangle, enclosing an area roughly 5.55 m. N-S and 4.30 m. E-W,
but no trace of a door could be found. The conclusion was that
either the wall was merely an interior facing for the cache found
in the enclosed arca (see Part III, Section 5), or else the door had
been so cleverly blocked up that no trace remained, The latter
would not have been a difficult task, since the masonry, although
coursed, was very irregular (pl. IV, zo, 12).

Inside the “room,” a stratum of irregular lumps of adobe was
reached at approximately 50 cm. below the surface (fig. 22). These
had been burned, and several of them bore impressions of small
parallel poles, indicating that they were probably the remains of
a thatch and adobe roof. Any structure associated with the lumps,
however, must have been erected on the summit of the mound,
as burned roof debris is usually found at, or near, the floor level
of the room it covered. In this case, the floor must have been of
earth, for no trace remained,

Below the adobe stratum was a hard fill consisting of brown
clay-like dirt mixed with a few bits of adobe, small pumice frag-
ments, and a large number of sherds, This extended to an average
depth of 1.20 m. from the surface, at which level the burned
“floors” and traces of the fire accompanying the deposition of the
cache began. At a depth of 2.20 m., the substructure of the mound,
composed of large, irregular pumice blocks, was encountered, It
could have been no more than a low terrace, evidently, for it was
below the present ground surface outside the mound and must
have been very little above the original ground level. The wall
enclosing the “room” extended down to the substructure, although

## Page 38

_Extraction method: OCR._

EXCAVATIONS AT LOS LLANITOS                                                 33

its construction became very crude below 1 m. from the surface.
‘The only definite floor was one of white mortar, found at a
depth of 1,60 m. It was well made, about 5 cm. in thickness, and
fairly level (fig, 22 and pl. V, 6). In the general region of the
cache, it had been broken through to accomodate the deeper
vessels. The original function of this floor is problematical, since
it was much too deep to serve any structure built on the finished
mound, ‘The only possibility is that the mound was once much
lower and that, when the cache was deposited, the walls of the
room were built up, and the-intcrior was filled with debris.

MOUND 10

As a sample of the low, rectangular type of structure, Mound
10 was chosen for excavation. This mound lies near the south-
castern limit of the ruins (fig. 19 and pl. IV, 8) and is aligned with
its long axis 3 degrees 4o minutes south of east. Before excavation,
Mound 10 was barely visible above the surface, since dirt had
filled in around the structure from the higher ground to the north-
west, but originally, it was probably about 1 m. in height.

The mound was composed of two thick boundary walls along

the walls, the mound consisted of a fill of brown clay-like dirt, very

3- DESCRIPTION

The government officials of El Salvador kindly gave permission
for the writer to export a large sample collection of sherds. Sherd
counts and preliminary ware classifications were therefore made in
the field, and further studies were made on the selected sherds
bronght back to the States, These selected specimens include all
rims, handles, bases, and legs recovered, as well as other sherds
showing definite body forms or designs. This collection is now in
the Peabody Museum of Harvard University. All complete speci-
mens were turned over to Sefior don Euscbio Padilla of Santiago
de Maria,

Almost all the slips used on Los Llanitos vessels were soft, and
extremely subject to weathering, with the result that groups
labeled “weathered, probably painted.” and “probably poly-
chrome,” figure rather prominently in sherd counts. The wares
listed below, however, must represent all, or very nearly all, of
the ceramics used at the site, since they were checked against all
the available material. The form of presentation follows in a
general way that cmployed by Thompson.* The same may be said
for the terms used in describing the pottery.

STRATIGRAPHIC INDICATIONS

The sherds were carefully segregated throughout the excava-
tion by levels determincd either by arbitrary measurements or by

“Thompson, 1939, chapter TV.

rich in sherds, At a depth of 1.25 m. this fill, in the central part of
the mound, gave way to a stratum of sterile light yellow volcanic
ash, which doubtless represented the limit of clearing for the con-
struction of the mound.

No trace of a structure was found on top of Mound 10, but it
seems probable that its function was a terrace for houses built of
perishable materials, since low, rectangular mounds of this type
were generally used for that purpose in Central America.

SUMMARY

From the foregoing description of the various mounds exca-
vated, it is possible to make a few generalities on the architecture
of Los Llanitos. The unit of construction is a wall of roughly
squared pumice blocks, sct in a mortar of wet adobe. This unit is
supplemented by a fill of brown clay-like soil, mixed with cultural
material. The only addition to the above are the stone slabs of
the ball court. There is no evidence of any plaster, or other facing
device, having been used on mound exteriors.

The long, comparatively low mounds, such as 1, 2, and 10, all
appear to have been constructed on the same general plan: a series
of walls parallel to the long axis of the mound, connected by a fill
of debris. The pyramidal mounds (3, 7, etc.), on the other hand,
may have followed the construction of Mound 4, with four vertical
walls enclosing a mass of fill. On this type of mound, however,
our information is far from complete. Since no superstructures
were found, it must be supposed that they were simple wattle and
daub affairs, much like the native houses of the present day. ‘The
burned roof adobe found in Mound y supports this view.

Nowhere in the site is there any evidence of more than one
period of construction, aside from the possibility discussed in the
description of Mound 7, although the general crudity of construc:
tion and the rather advanced disintegration of the structures would
tend to obscure data of this sort, Since the pottery is uniform
throughout the dig, the writer is of the opinion that Los Llanitos
is a one-period site,

OF POTTERY

construction, but no significant differences in ware, form, or decora-
tion were found to exist between any of these strata. The conclu-
sion, therefore, is that Los Llanitos is a one-period site, a fact
which the mound construction helps to verify.

WARES
Unslipped Ware
Surface. Color ranges from light to dark brown. Surface is
smooth with flakey orange or white paint frequently appearing
over part of the vessel. Surfaces are often badly weathered.
Paste. Light to dark brown in color, dark cores frequent, Tex-
ture is coarse.

Larce Storace Bowts. Illustration. Fig. 24, b, h, L

Surface, Neck interiors and sometimes neck exteriors and shoul-
ders painted white or orange.

Form. Large, thick-walled vessels, with incurving sides and
rather high necks, which produce a composite silhouette. The
bowl sides are slightly constricted at the juncture of neck and
body, but not enough to give the vessels the appearance of storage
jars. Necks are cither outcurving or incurving (bowl-shaped), the
former being more common, Pronounced basal angles are often
found, especially on vessels with incurving necks. Lips are usually
round, but sometimes beveled on the interior. Bases are dimpled.

Modification. Bowls arc cquipped with two vertical handles of

Form, Large vessels with constricted openings and high outcurv-
ing necks. Bodies are globular and bases are dimpled. Occasional

Size. Lip-to-lip diameters range 10-28 cm., average 18-24 cm. At

but are usually entirely on the shoulder.

Surface, Neck interiors and exteriors and shoulders usually

Rust Ware

Surface, Govered on one or both surfaces with a poor yellowish-
orange slip, which has frequently worn away. Where the slip has
weathered, the surface is coarse and granular, with a rusty color.
The temper shows promincntly on the surface.

Paste. Dark brown, usually with a black core. Texture coarse.

Form. Large simple bowls.

Size, Diameters at the mouth average 30-53 cm.

Modification. Bowls arc rarcly equipped with handles, of the
vertical, semi-ribbon type.

Frequency. Rare, only 27 selected sherds.

Red-Brown Burnished Ware

Surface. Covered on one or both surfaces with heavy reddish-
brown slip. The slip is burnished and often crazed. Not much
variation in color.

Paste. Dark brown, usually with black core.

Form. The sherds appear to be about evenly divided between
large storage bowl and storage jar shapes, described under un-
slipped ware.

Size. Storage bowls and jars tend to be a little larger than those
of unslipped ware.

Decoration. Rare, Shallow curvilinear grooving on bowl and
jar necks,

Frequency. Forty selected sherds, or 20 per cent of the total.

Orange Ware

Surface. Smoothed, and covered on one or both sides with a soft
white slip, afterwards stained orange. The orange stain has largely
disappeared. Most sherds yield casily to weathering, and the white
slip has worn off in many places,

Paste. Medium brown in color, sometimes with darker core,

Form. Bowls with simple, flaring, or outcurved walls. About half
the rim sherds are outcurved, and these usually belong to vessels
supported by legs, probably tetrapod. A few sharp basal angles
appear. Bases are both dimpled and fiat. Also present in this
orange ware are sherds of two jars with high necks and outcurved
rims.

Size. Diameters, lip-to-lip, of bowls range from 16 cm. to 36 cm.,
but average 20-22 cm. Mouth diameters of jars are 6 cm. and g cm,
One bowl measures 4.5 cm. in height from base to lip.

Modification, Handles rare, all of the vertical semi-ribbon type.
Legs fairly common. A few are solid cones or knobs (fig. 24, s, 1), but
more often they are of the hollow conical or cylindrical type, the
latter usually being furnished with a chiscl-shaped tip (fig. 24, 1, 0).
‘The hollow legs range from 5 cm. to 10 cm. in length and have a

very characteristic “hump” on the outside surface, just below the
joint with the vessel body.

Decoration, Grotesque faces are occasionally formed of applied
clay fillets on vessel exteriors. Simple grooving is also fairly com-
mon.

Frequency. The second most common slipped ware, 226 selected
sherds, or 10 per cent of the total.

Remarks, As in many wares with a soft slip and fugitive paint,
the actual sherd count here is not absolutely accurate. Orange
ware forms are also common in Los Llanitos polychrome ware, and
without doubt many sherds of the latter have been classified as

Red on Orange Ware

This ware corresponds in almost all particulars to orange ware,
plus the addition of red paint in rim or basal bands, or rarely in
simple designs below the rim. It is probable that this ware should
be combined with orange ware.

‘The only red on orange form not found in orange ware is a
two-handled jar with vertical neck and globular body. This is a
very common form at Los Llanitos. Lip-to-lip diameters range
10-22 CM, average 12-16 cm. One reconstructed jar is 18 cm. high.
Decoration consists of a red rim band and red stripes painted on
the handles (fig. 24, ¢).

Red on Hard Orange Ware

Surface. Smoothed, and covered, usually on both sides, with a
fine hard orange slip. Almost hal! of the sherds are badly discolored.
Paste. Light to dark brown in color, rarely with a black core.
Form. Bowls, usually simple, occasionally with exterior angle.

Size. Diameters at lip range 1824 cm., the average being near
the smaller extreme.

Modification. Legs very rare, small solid conical.

Decoration. Red-painted rim and base bands common. Also de-
signs of wavy lines, bands, dots, and stcpped pyramids. Naturalis-
tic motifs are very rare.

Frequency, Ninety-one selected sherds.

Miscellaneous Dichrome Wares

RED ON CREAM WARE. Two sherds of this ware are from
high-necked bowls with human-cfigy handles. The rim and the

handles arc painted red, and a band of red triangles encircles the
neck.

RED ON CREAM-ORANGE WARE. The fifteen sherds of
this ware all come from thin-walled, simple bowls with tapering
lip and flat base. Lip-to-lip diameters are almost invariably 22 cm.
On both exterior and interior, just below the lip, simple «designs
are delicately executed in red paint. These usually consist of a
broad horizontal band, bordered with fine lines or dots. The cen-
tral band may be solid red, but is often composed of a running
series of elongated S-frets (fig. 25, q, 7).

RED ON YELLOW-ORANGE WARE. Two rim sherds are
from simple bowls 30 cm. in diameter. A yellow-orange slip was
applied to the exterior and runs over onto the interior, where it
extends down from the lip about 3 em, Decoration is confined to
the upper exterior wall and consists of a simple linear pattern in
red, alternated with wide, shallow diagonal grooves made in the
wet clay with the fingers or a stick. On one sherd, the grooving was
done before application of the slip; on the other, after application
of the slip.

## Page 41

_Extraction method: OCR._

36                -                     ARCHAEOLOGICAL INVESTIGATIONS IN EL SALVADOR

Los Llanitos Polychrome

Surface. Both surfaces covered with a thick white slip, later
washed with a light to medium orange stain. The slip is often badly
weathered, or flakey. The colors used in the designs are deep
orange, muddy red {almost purple on many specimens), and black,
which has often faded to deep brown.

Paste. Medium brown color, rarely with darker core. Fine-
textured,

Fooren Bowts. Illustration. (Fig. 24, f. Fig. 25, a-e; pl. VL 3,
12,13.)

Form. Shallow bowls with flaring or outcurving sides, definite
basal angles, and rounded bases, Rims are often outcurving. Bowls
are supported by three (?) or four legs. A few vessels have incurv-
ing sides.

Size. Lip-to-lip diameters range 16-34 cm., average 22-28 cm.
One tetrapod bowl is 12.5 cm. in height from floor to lip.

Modification. Bowls are equipped with three (?) or four legs,
solid or hollow of the long conical, or long slab type (fig. 24, m-p,
5, t; fig. 25, g, h). A few legs are short conical or slab, and one leg
is modeled into an animal's head. As in orange ware, the long
conical and slab legs bear the characteristic “hump” just below the
joint with the vessel body.

A single high annular base is assigned to this ware. It measures
20 cm. in diameter and 6 cm. high and is pierced at intervals with
rectangular holes (fig. 25, 0). This is the only annular base found
in the excavations.

Small vertical handles appear commonly on footed bowls. Pre-
sumably two to a vessel; they are placed on the sides, with the lower
attachment resting on the basal angle.

Decoration. Carelessly executed designs are painted on both
exterior and interior of vessels. Motifs are quite simple: bands,
wavy lines, zig-zags, G-frets, hatchure, etc., but they have been
applied in such a hasty and sketchy manner that they are very
distinctive. Grotesque animals sometimes appear on vessel in-
teriors. The usual exterior decoration is a series of Free wavy lines
or bands running horizontally between the rim and the basal angle.
The exterior may also bear a grotesque human face in appliqué
{pl. VI, 3). The outside surfaces of the legs usually bear a simple
line design, but occasionally they are painted to represent a face.
All the designs are painted in muddy red and black; the deep
orange merely serves to separate these colors, or to border the main
motif.

Frequency. Footed bowls of this ware are very common at Los
Llanitos numbering 253 selected sherds, or 11 per cent of all
selected sherds from the site.

Remarks, “Humped" legs, of the type described above and under
orange ware, ate present in the Peabody Museum collections from
the Ulua Valley, Honduras. The writer knows of no parallels,
however, cither for the shape of these vessels as a whole, or for
their style of decoration.

‘Tuck Bows. Illustration. Fig. 25, f, i, j.

Form. Similar to that of footed bowls in this ware, but much
larger and with much thicker walls.

Size. Diameters at the lips range 38-52 cm. with most of the
specimens at the larger extreme, Wall thickness averages 1.4 cm.,
sometimes reaches 2 cm.

Modification. No legs were found attached to these bowls, but
the supports are presumably larger than those of footed bowls. One
broken solid conical leg, measuring g cm. in diameter at its upper
end, has been assigned to this form, and it seems probable that

footed legs of the type shown in fig. 24, q, 7, may also belong
here.

Decoration, Similar to that on footed bowls, but at times, even
more brilliant and sketchy. The fragmentary design shown in figure
25, j, apparently a crude face, is found on sherds of two different
bowls. On vessel exteriors, besides a painted design, large grotesque
faces are commonly applied.

Frequency, Uncommon, 20 selected sherds,

MISCELLANEOUS Forms,

One miniature jug measures about 7.5 cm. in height. A small
vertical handle, or pierced lug, is placed near the rim. The jug is
painted, rather crudely, on the exterior only.

Three large vertical strap handles indicate that vessels similar
to the vertical-neck jars of red on orange ware were decorated in
Los Llanitos polychrome technique. In this ware, however, they
are somewhat larger in size, Designs, in all instances, are badly
weathered.

Three sherds of composite-silhouette bowls belong in this ware.
‘The change of silhouctte of the sides is marked by a bulge about
3.5 cm. below the lip. They are decorated inside and out with
painted designs, now badly weathered. One-half of a complete
specimen measures 18 cm. in diameter and g cm. in height (fig.

24, a).

Deep Orange and Black on Orange Ware

Surface. Both surfaces covered with hard orange slip, on which
designs are painted in deep orange and black.

Paste. Brown in color, no dark cores.

Form. Simple, rather thin-walled bowls. One composite-sil-
houette bowl. Two sherds of vessel covers belong to this ware,

Size. Bowl diameters at mouth range 23-36 cm., average 23-26
cm. Both cover sherds, although from different specimens, measure
26 cm. in diameter.

Modification. One solid slab leg is 5.5 cm. wide and g.8 cm. high.

Decoration. The small size and weathered conditions of most
of the sherds prevents accurate identification, but the designs
visible are all geometric broad line motifs.

Frequency. Forty-cight selected sherds,

Red and Deep Orange on Orange Ware

Surface. Both surfaces covered with a light orange slip, upon
which designs are painted in red and deep orange.

Paste. Brown color, no dark cores.

Form. Bowls, the great majority having a composite silhouctte.
Thin walls, flat bases,

Size. Lip-to-lip diameters average 20 cm.

Decoration. Usually, geometric designs in both broad and thin
line technique, on exteriors and interiors of bowls, Bands, crosses,
loops, and hatchure are common, and a very degenerate glyph band
often appears in the exterior of composite-silhouette bowls, just
below the rim. One sherd bears a very good Maya face in profile
(fig. 25, $), but life motifs are otherwise Jacking, as far as can be
made out. Designs are usually outlined in red, with a filler of
deep orange.

Frequency. Thirty-one selected sherds.

Fine Line Polychrome Ware

Illustration. Fig. 24, e: fig. 25, En.

Surface, Both surfaces covered with rich creamy-orange slip,
upon which designs are painted in decp orange and black.

Paste, Well-fired, medium brown in color without dark cores.

EXCAVATIONS AT LOS LLANITOS                                                7

Form. Footed bowls with outcurving sides, very sharp basal
angles, sometimes approaching a flange. Bases are Mat.

Size. Lip-to-lip diameters range 22-32 cm, average go cm. Diame-
ters at the basal angle average 24 em. One vessel measures 4.5 cm.
from lip to basal angle.

Modification. Although basal sherds show scars of leg attach-
ments, no legs were found which could be assigned to this ware.
From attachment scars, the legs appear to have been hollow and
fairly round in cross-section, Lt is not known whether vessels were
supported by three or four legs,

Decoration. All designs visible are geometric, executed in fine
line technique and carefully drawn. The most constant and charac-
teristic motif is on the interior wall, where a series of “tallies” or
“counters” hangs from the rim. These vary in design from bow] to
bow! but all are apparently intended to convey the same idea, On
the exterior, various designs made up of triangles, circles, frets,
etc., can be made out. One bow! bears a small animal head and
two front paws applied to the basal angle.

Frequency. About 20 sherds.

Remarks, Variant examples of fine line polychrome ware are
reported from Copan,* the Ulua Valley" and Lake Yojoa* in Hon-
duras, and from cental El Salvador.” There can be little doubt
but that the Los Llanitos examples are associated with these.

Illustration. Fig. 25, 5, €.

Surface. Smoothed, and covered on the extcrior with a deep
orange slip. This slip runs over the rim and down the interior
surface for a distance of 3-4 cm, below the rim. Painted decoration
occupies only the slipped areas.

Paste. Light brown in color, no dark cores, hard-fired,

Form. Large, deep bowls with slightly recurved sides and thick-
ened rims.

Size. Lip-to-lip diameters range 16-32 can., but average 26.52 cm.

Decoration, Exterior: very simple rectilinear designs, with a sin-
gle color covering large areas. Very little slip color visible. A groove
customarily runs around the vessel just below the rim, and other
grooves and deep circular depressions are found elsewhere on this
surface. Some of the grooves have been filled with flakey white
paint, but generally both grooves and depressions are painted ap-
proximately the color of the slip.

Interior: decoration usually absent. When present, it consists
of one or two broad black or red bands just below the lip. A thin
wavy line has been scratched through these bands into the clay
before firing.

Frequency. Thirty-eight selected sherds.

Miscellancous Polychrome Wares

RED AND BLACK ON ORANGE WARE WITH USULU-
TAN ‘TECHNIQUE. Two simple bow! sherds are slipped orange.
On the exterior they bear curvilinear designs in red and black,
while the interior is decorated with broad lines in Usulutan tech-
niquc.

RED AND BLACK ON WHITE WARE. The few sherds of
this ware are all from a single simple bowl, 14 cm, in diameter at the
lip. Tt bears a white slip, upon which a design is painted in glossy
red and black. The design is probably naturalistic, although its
exact nature cannot be made out.

ULUA POLYCHROME WARE. Two basal sherds, each bear-
ing a solid slab foot, in form and color (decp orange and black on
orange) are typical of specimens from the Ulua River, Honduras.
One slab foot is plain, and slightly set in from the wall of the
bowl or vase. It bears traces of a coating of pink plaster (fig. 24, j).
The other specimen is modeled into the form of a kneeling leg and
foot (fig. 24, 1).

ORANGE AND BROWN-BLACK ON YELLOW WARE. A
single sherd from a bow! 20 cm. in diameter at the lip is slipped
yellow on both surfaces. Complicated geometric designs in brown-
ish-black and light orange are painted on this slip in rather care-
less technique. The specimen is hard-fired with a glossy finish
(fig. 25, u).

FUGITIVE POLYCHROME WARE. Twenty-one sherds of
well-fired, thin-walled pottery, are so weathered that neither de-
signs nor colors can be accurately identified, ‘hey are certainly
polychrome, however, possibly deep orange and black on cream.
About half the sherds are from simple bowls with slightly thick-
ened rims and the rest are from composite-sifhoucttc bowls (fig.
24. €). One sherd represents a bowl with an incurved rim. Regard-
less of shape, they all measure 18 cm. in diameter at the lip.

from the rubbish deposit between the north boundary wall of the
ball court and the structure just north of that were omitted from
the count, since they were so weathered that ware definitions were
usually impossible. The selected sherds from this deposit alone
amounted to 3600, or more than all the others combined.

MISCELLANEOUS POTTERY OBJECTS
Figurines

About two dozen fragments of figurines were recovered in the
excavations, all of them of the solid, hand-made type. More than
half of these are of the cleft foot varicty, described by Lothrop as
“upper level archaic." The remainder are plain “archaic” forms
(such as those in pls. X1, 73; XL, 3), variations of which are found
in many Central American regions.

Spindle Whorls

Eleven spindle whorls, whole and fragmentary, were recovered
in the excavations, One is conical, and the rest are plano-convex
in form." All are decorated with crude, incised designs, many of
which are very similar to designs observed on whorls from
Quelepa.*

Pottery Discs

Two discs cut from unslipped ware sherds measure 4.1 cm. and
5-3 cm, in diameter, One is partially perforated through the center.

SUMMARY

Much of the pottery from Los Llanitos must be considered sui
generis at present, since lack of any intensive study in surrounding
regions leaves us litte with which to compare it. This summary,
therefore, will confine itsclf merely to pointing out similarities or
differences between the Los Llanitos ceramic complex and that of
neighboring areas, the latter being represented almost entirely by
objects in private collections. Observations of wider import will
be left for the chapter on conclusions at the end of this report,

The Los Llanitos unslipped large storage bowl, by far the most
popular form in this ware, is not characteristic of any other Middle
American site known to the author. Future investigations, how-
ever, especially in castern El Salvador, may show this to be untrue.
Unslipped utility pottery does not figure largely in private collec-
tions due to its low artistic value, and complete vessels, especially
desired by amateur enthusiasts, would be almost impossible to
find, since the large size of the storage bowl, and the constant use
to which it was undoubtedly put, would be extremely conducive
to breakage.

The unslipped storage jars, on the other hand, do not differ
from those found universally in Central American sites, The small
number of storage jar sherds (about 100) at Los Llanitos, how-
ever, is surprising. Globular jars with greatly constricted open-
ings must have been, as they are today, the principal water con-
tainers of Gentral America, and we should expect their sherds to
make up a large percentage of any pottery complex. Undoubtedly,
the small number at Los Llanitos can be partially accounted for
by the following reasons. First, only sclected sherds (rims, bases,
and shoulders) were counted. The storage jar, having a constricted
opening, naturally produces fewer rim sherds than the storage
bowl, with a wide mouth. Shoulder sherds of the two forms are

“Tothrop, 1927b, figs. 11, 17. See also this volume, pls. IX, 2), 37, 327 XII, 5, 6,
for other examples of this variety.

M See Langyear, 19420, for definitions of these terms.

# Longyear, 1942h, fig, 1, €, fe, rahe

often indistinguishable, and basal sherds always. Thus many stor-
age jar sherds may be included in the storage bowl count. Secondly,
most of the Los Llanitos sherd inaterial comes from the ball court
and Mounds g and 7, all more or less ceremonial structures, in
which utility pottery would not necessarily occur in large numbers,
Nevertheless, the scarcity of storage jar forms at Los Llanitos is
worthy of note. The sub-type of this shape, bearing a filleted face
on the neck is very common in El Salvador (pl. VIII, £, 5, 6, 37,
40), and variants are found to the east and south in Middle Amer-
ica. It is not, however, a characteristic of the Maya region to the
west and north. “These “face-neck" jars are usually smaller than the
undecorated forms, although some are quite large.

No comparative material for rust ware or red-brown burnished
ware exists, At present, we can only postulate that they are local
variants of the unslipped utility pottery.

Los Llanitos orange ware forms have not been noted by the
author in eastern El Salvadoran collections. ‘he characteristic
“hump” on the legs, however, also appears on pottery legs from
the Ulua Valley, Honduras? but the latter differ from the Los
Llanitos specimens in ware and in many details of shape. Orange
ware is very closely allied with Los Llanitos polychrome in both
ware and shape and, as stated in the descriptions of these wares,
weathering renders them indistinguishable, one from the other.
In fact, there is really nothing to indicate that all orange ware is
not merely weathered Los Llanitos polychrome, In view of the
large number of sherds of this class, however, it was thought best
to assign them to a separate ware, pending further investigation.

The next pottery for which we have comparative material is
red on cream ware. This is frequently encountered in collections
from eastern El Salvador, especially Quelepa. The Los Llanitos
specimens compare almost exactly in form and decoration with
that shown in plate IX, 76,

What has been said of orange ware of course applies to Los
Llanitos polychrome also. The painted designs on the latter, how-
alienate this ware even farther from the corpus of Central
American pottery. If we must look for an outside influence, it
might be said that the general coloring resembles that on southern,
rather than northern pottery, and that the applied faces on the
exteriors of vessels also relate to the south, but that is all, The
designs appear degenerate, but even this cannot be claimed, as we
have no knowledge of the prototype of this ware.

Deep orange and black on orange ware is probably Maya in
inspiration. Shapes and painted designs are both rather indefinite
but can casily be duplicated in Maya collections, The two sherds
of vessel covers, assigned to this ware, are unique in Los Llanitos
pottery, but similar ones have been found at Copan. Red and
deep orange ware scems definitely Maya. Composite-silhonctte
bowls are common in the southeastern Maya region, the technique
of filling in a red outline with deep orange is frequent at sites such
as Copan, and the face which appears on one sherd (fig. 25, p) is
in the style of Copan, the Ulua Valley, southeastern Guatemala,
and western El Salvador.

The associations of fine line polychrome were noted under
the description of that ware. In the Later Phase at Tazumal, Boggs
describes a rapid style, fine line polychrome which must be very
closely related to the Los Llanitos specimens.** Some of the Tazu-
mal forms are “double-curve bowls with four feet, basal ridges,
flaring rims, low-round lips.” This description is not too different
from that of the Los Llanitos shapes, Boggs docs not mention any
“counters” or “tallies” on the interiors of his specimens but this

* Collections in the Peabody Museum of Harvard University,
* Longyear, 1940a, p. 8g.
* See Appendix C.

## Page 45

_Extraction method: OCR._

EXCAVATIONS AT LOS LLANITOS                                              39

does not necessatily mean that they do not exist. Fine line poly-
chrome, because of its easily recognizable characteristics and its
wide distribution in the region of the southeastern Maya frontier,
should be an important diagnostic ware in this area. Unfortunate-
ly, its position in time is not yet well-known.

No comparative material is known for red and black on deep
orange ware. The combination of polychrome painting and Usulu-
tan technique (red and black on orange ware with Usulutan tech-
nique) has been seen in El Salvadoran collections, however, and is
also reported from Copan," When in this combination, the Usulu-
tan decoration is always of the broad line form. The Los Llanitos
specimens should be quite late in date, for while true Usulutan
ware is considered carly in Central America, the association of
this technique with painted pottery is late at Copan and Tazumal.*
‘The two basal sherds of Ulua polychrome ware at Los Llanitos
are undoubted importations as they conform in every respect to

the pottery of the Ulua Valley and western F] Salvador and are
entirely different from the other Los Llanitos sherds.

The cleft foot figurines recovered in the excavations are very
widespread in El Salvador, having been found from Quelepa all
the way west to Ahuachapan. To the writer's knowledge, this type
has not been reported outside of the Republic, but this is probably
due to lack of investigation in regions immediately surrounding
El Salvador. At any rate, the presence of so specialized a figurine
form throughout this area suggests either extensive trade or, more
likely, widespread acceptance of a certain tenet of the figurine
cult, in which the cleft foot was an important factor. The other
solid “archaic” figurines from Los Llanitos are too weathered to
permit of detailed description, but this general type is also found
throughout El Salvador, as well as in Guatemala and Honduras,
and probably represents the basic symbol of agricultural or other
simple rites.

BONE

Only one bone was found during excavation, Lt is the rib of

5 CACHES

Los Llanitos is situated in a fairly populous district, and the
main trail from the hacienda to the river passes just to the cast of
the site. We had plenty of visitors every day, since the excavations
aroused much interest among the natives, and in the  evening,
after the writer and his men had left the scene, curious boys toured
the ruins, investigating the progress of the dig. Artifacts left in situ
overnight, therefore, were likely to be gone by morning, and con-
sequently, any caches found had to be removed the same day.
Where this was not possible, specimens not taken out were covered
with enough loose dirt to safeguard them against amateur investi-
Bator,

The vessels found in the caches were, almost without exception,
quite unlike the pottery recovered from the other excavations at
the site. For this reason, they have not been included in the regular
pottery discussion, but are described individually below.

Cache 1, Mound 2. Just inside the northeast corner of Mound 2,
a small cache of polychrome sherds was found beside a small thin
stone slab. The only other object was a crude polishing stone. This
cache, unforumately, was completely removed by a workman be-
fore the writers attention was called to it, so original positions,
€tc., are not known.

The sherds that were found are from two vessels. One is a thin-
walled orange bowl, bearing an exterior decoration of solid gro-
tesque monkeys, alternately red and black. The other vessel is
more unusual, and of greater interest. It is a cylindrical vase, with
thickened lip and flat hase, a form characteristic of the Ulua
Valley-Lake Yojoa region of Honduras, At the lip it measures 16
em, in diameter and its height is 18-20 cm, The fragmentary ex-
terior design is executed in a bold, careless technique in deep
orange, red and black on an orange slip. The decoration consists
of two figures, one a striking representation of a scorpion and the

other a dancing figure composed of crude kidney bean-shaped
blobs (pl. VI, 30). The contrast between the two is as great as that
between fresco painting and graffiti, and the whole presents a very
unusual technique for this type of pottery.

Cache 2, Mound 2. This cache was located in the fill between
the two easternmost longitudinal walls of Mound 2 at a depth of
65 cm. below the surface (fig. 20 and pl. V, 1). It consisted of a
number of brown ware incensarios, both whole and broken, lying
on top of a large convex stone slab. Another slab, set upright just
to the east of the cache, had tilted outward due to the pressure of
earth from above (pl. V, 2, 3, 7). Apparently, this earth pressure
had also broken the vessels in the cache, since the sherds of each
were closely grouped together and were easily mended.

‘The incensarios are made of a coarse brown clay and are, with
one exception, of the “ladle” type, with a single, long, horizontal
handle. All are decorated with spikes. The exception to the“ladie™
type incensario is Vessel 2 (pl. VI, 1) At present, all that is left
of this vessel is 2 heavy annular base, which probably supported
a large vase. Vessels 3 and 5 are quite similar. The handle of the
former is missing (pl. V1, 9), but that of Vessel 5 is a long solid
cylinder, terminating in a crude hand (pl. VI, 5, 6). The small
hole in the base of this incensario probably furnished a draft for
the material burned in the bowl. The most ornate incensario is
Vessel 4, which bears a handle modeled to represent a crouching
tiger (pl. V1, 4, 7, 8). The whole is covered with a cream slip. Ves-
sels 2 and 6 are not incensarios. Both are flat bowls supported by
three solid slab legs, but, while Vessel 2 is plain brown ware (pl.
VI, 2), the other is very badly weathered polychrome (pl. VI, 70).
The litte design and color remaining on the latter bowl resem-
bles that on Vessel 7 of Cache 1 in Mound 7 (see below), but other-
wise, these two bowls are very dissimilar.

_Extraction method: OCR._

EXCAVATIONS AT LOS LLANITOS                                                 4)

After the pottery specimens had been removed, the underlying
stone slab was turned over. In the soft dirt directly below it were
found three artifacts of obsidian: a large tanged point, finely
chipped on both surfaces, a crude concave scraper, and a frag-
mentary blade (pl. VI, 12).

Cache 1, Mound 7. A cache of six painted bowls and an elaborate
incensario cover was distributed throughout a considerable arca
in the southwest part of Mound 7 (fig. 26). Although thus scat-
tered, there is no doubt that the vessels belonged together, for
all were related to a single large slab and were further tied in by
a series of burned “floors” and ash strata.

The bowls and the slab of the cache had been placed, together
with a large amount of combustible material, well down in the
mound. ‘The only true floor found during excavation, one of white
mortar, had been broken through to accommodate the slab and
the decpest vessels (pp), V, 6), Before covering, the combustible ma-
terial was lit and the fire allowed to burn. All the bowls show the
effects of this in smoke-blackened areas, especially on the interiors.
The dirt surrounding the vessels was burned hard, and charcoal
and ash were everywhere abundant. This fire probably also ac-
counted for the numerous burned “floors” in the upper part of
the cache (Gig. 22 and pl. VII). These fragmentary “floors,” or thin
fire-hardened strata, wove in and out among the vessels and were
extremely hard to follow and chart, especially so since every pot
had to be removed as soon as uncovered, necessitating the destruc-
tion of any burned strata lying nearby. The section and photo
gtaphs showing these strata, therefore, were taken from the walls
of the excavation after the specimens were removed and, although
they depict the general situation existing in the cache, do not
bear an especially close relation to the vessels themselves. The
burned “floors” were all uniform in appearance and averaged 1.5
em. in thickness. They were formed of some kind of vegetable
substance, burned hard by the fire and colored a bluish-grey there-
by. The material was extremely porous due to the burning-out of
the plant fibers. The top 2 mm. of the strata were even harder
than the rest and were light tan in color. The surface on this side
was smooth, and overlying dirt cleaved easily from it. The under-
surface was rough and harder to follow.

After the fire had burned in the cache, the interior of the mound
must have been filled in with dirt and rubbish. The pressure of
this overburden had cracked and deformed all the vessels by the
time of their discovery, making it difficult to repair them neatly.

The slab associated with this cache was by far the finest found
in the ruins (pl. V, 10). Made of an altered volcanic rock, probably
latite, it measured 56 cm. in height, 49 cm. in greatest width, and
was wedgesbaped in section, tapering evenly down from 10 cm. in
thickness at the top to 3 cm. at the bottom, The slab had not been
finished off on the edges, these all being more or less rough, but
the surfaces were well smoothed and clearly showed the grain of
the stone. The lower end of the slab rested on the scattered pumice
blocks forming the substructure of the mound, but the stone was
otherwise unsupported aside from the dirt which was packed in
around it.

Bowl number 1 from the cache (pls. V, 4; VI, 24, 27) is supported
by four solid conical legs and bears a yellow-cream slip. The decora-
tion is executed in deep orange paint and consists of two continu-
ous rows of grotesque animal heads, one row above the other. The
bowl! rim bears short lines and the interior has horizontal bands.

Bowl 2 (pls, V, 5; VI, 18, 19) is a tripod with solid conical legs,
and is painted in red and black on an orange slip. The exterior has
semi-lunate motifs above each leg, with complex scrolls between,
extending from the rim to the underside of the vessel. Each leg is

painted to represent a face, The rim is decorated with short black
lines, and the interior with plain horizontal bands.

Bowl 3 (pl. VI, 75), also a tripod on solid conical legs, bears an
unusual design in deep orange, black and cream on an orange slip.
A broad cream band on the vessel exterior is decorated with alter-
nate red and black pyramided horizontal lines, every other pyramid
being inverted. The legs have solid black panels. Like the bowls
mentioned above, this one has short lines (black) on the rim and
bands on the interior.

Bowl 5 (pls. V, 6; VI, 23) is supported by four solid conical legs
and bears the most complicated design of any in the cache. The

On the exterior, the stain has been omitted from a wide band, and
on the white surface thus obtained, a paneled rectilinear design

Bowl 6 (pls. V, 8; VI, 27, 22) is a Wipod with solid conical legs.
‘The exterior has a decoration of triangles painted in brownish-

and deep orange. The interior of the bowl, slipped in deeper
orange than the exterior, has groups of short black bands on the

Bowl 7 (pls. V, 8; VI 26, 32, 32), also a tripod on solid conical
legs, bears an unusual decoration in deep orange and black on an

orange slip. The exterior has a paneled semi-lunate motif above
cach leg. The legs are decorated with grotesque faces and the
spaces between the legs are occupied by semi-lunate figures hanging
from a band alternately striped black and deep orange. Just below
the outcurve of the rim is a series of black dots. The interior rim
is also dotted and three black bands run around the inside of the

Vessel 4 differs radically from the other specimens in the cache
(pls. V. 5; VI, 24, 25, 28, 29). It probably served as the cover of an
incensario and is modeled to represent a “tiger man.” The face is
donc in excellent technique and bears elaborations of incised cir-
cles on cach cheek, a nose-plug, and two small pellets of clay on
the forehead, The only tiger characteristics are the cars, which
protrude above the top of the vessel. The base of the cover is open,
with a finished rim. ‘This rim is interrupted in the rear by a slot
1.1 cm. wide and extending upward 4.7 cm. In the center of the top
is a hole 1 cm. in diameter. This specimen was covered with blue
paint, traces of which can still be observed.

Excepting only Vessel 4, all the objects in this cache are markedly
similar in form and decoration. They are all bowls supported by
solid conical legs, and the presence of both tetrapods and tripods
in the same deposit no longer possesses the significance which
would have been attributed to it a few years ago. Rim and interior
bands appear on almost every bowl and exterior decorations,
while varied, are all geometrical with the exception of Vessel 1. No
close comparisons can be made between these bowls and other pot-
tery from Central America, nothing like them having been recov-
ered even from nearby regions, such as Quelepa, Chinameca, and
Usulutan. In general appearance, however, the writer believes that

Bowls 1 and 5 most closely resemble Central American pottery
forms and techniques heretofore known, while Bowls 2 and 7 are
most dissimilar.

Miscellaneous Finds. In the loose stone fallen from the western
side of Mound 3 were found the fragments of two vessels. The
sherds were scattered throughout an area of about 114 m., and it is
reasonable to suppose that the vessels were once located some-
where on the side of the mound, from where they were thrown
down and broken as a result of the collapse of the stone struc-
ture.

One vessel is a large dish, supported by three massive legs.
The latter are hollow cylinders, equipped with rattles and deco-
rated with grotesque modeled faces on their upper portions, the
faces being inverted when the vessel is standing upright. The dish
is slipped in white, stained orange, upon which a decoration is
painted in red and black. All the colors are extremely fugitive
and have completely disappeared from the exterior, but traces of
black rim bands and a central red circle can be discerned on the

The other vessel is a deep, flat-bottomed bowl, one of the few
legless specimens found at the site (pl. VI, 76, 20, 27). 11 bears
designs in brown and orange on a white slip. On the exterior are
four panels, two of them filled with cross-hatchure and the other
two containing circles enclosing a dotted cross. Just below the rim
runs a band of formless scrawls. The interior of the bowl has a
broad band of S-shaped frets, while in the center are traces of
what are probably animal figures, although the vessel is here too
fragmentary to permit of positive identification.

6. CONCLUSIONS

ARCHITECTURE

We cannot compare the architecture of Los Llanitos with that
of neighboring sites, none of the latter having been excavated.
The only other ruins in El Salvador about which much is known
are Tazumal, Campana-San Andres, and Cihuatan. None of these,
however, resemble Los Llanitos in construction. Tazumal mounds
are built up of adobe or adobe-and-boulder cores. with cement
facing:"* most of the mounds at Campana-San Andres are com-
posed of large mould-made adobe bricks and are faced with a
cementlike mixture;” and Cihuatan construction consists of cut
stone blocks and slabs set in mortar.** The pumice block core and
dirt fill of the Los Llanitos mounds, therefore, has no parallel at
present in the region. Without doubt, however, further excavation
would reveal comparable construction at other sites.

Tn arrangement, Los Llanitos structures are perfectly regular.
They are definitely oriented and bound clear-cut plazas or courts.
The mounds are of three general types, cach occupying a definite
place in the total plan. The ball court and high “ceremonial”
structures surround the main plaza, while the low platform like
“house mounds” are grouped around smaller courts in outlying
areas. This arrangement is one often termed “typically Maya,” but
it is by no means confined to sites in arcas of Maya culture. The
fortress of Tenampua, for example, in Lenca territory and re-
putedly a Lenca site, has a plaza arrangement with a ball court and
other differentiated structures,** and further investigation in non-
Maya areas would doubtless reveal similar plans. Thus we cannot
determine cultural affinities by the appearance of a site alone but
must augment this by a consideration of other material remains.

See Apperutix C.

> Bogs. >

POTTERY

The pottery of Los Llanitos may be divided into two groups,
the larger composed of ceramics indigenous to the site or to the
surrounding region, and the smaller made up of imported or
exotic wares, brought in from outlying arcas, and differing in
form and decoration from the main body of Los Llanitos material.
The indigenous pottery is important in that it defines the ceramic
tradition of the site and the country under its jurisdiction. In this
instance, it is of especial importance, since Los Llanitos is situated
in territory which archacologically is virtually unknown. The
imported group determines the relations of the site with other
sites or cultural areas and, should some of the wares come from
regions with a well-defined time-scale, they will help to date the
site as well.

Judging by frequence of occurrence, the indigenous pottery at
Los Llanitos should include the following wares; unslipped,
orange, red on orange, and Los Llanitos polychrome. It probably
also includes rust ware and red-brown burnished ware. The cera-
mic tradition expressed by this group of pottery is a fairly definite
one. The unslipped utility ware has three forms: large storage
bowls, storage jars, and simple bowls, Decoration is by incision
and appliqué, the former being represented by simple wavy lines,
and the latter by knobs and grotesque faces. The slipped and
painted wares have as their principal shape, various shallow bowls,
supported by three or four legs. Slips are soft and extremely sus-
ceptible to weathering. ‘The designs, executed in paint which
tends to fade and alter in color, are grotesque, carelessly drawn,
and rather degenerate in appearance. They consist mainly of

* See under Cibuatan, Part 1.

™ Popence, 1925.

## Page 49

_Extraction method: OCR._

o —

EXCAVATIONS AT LOS LLANITOS                                            43

curvilinear geometric motifs, but conventionalized animals also
appear. Appliqué decoration is infrequent and consists of filleted
faces, similar to those on unslipped ware. Taken as 2 whole, the
indigenous pottery of Los Llanitos is characterized by large, thick-
walled vessels with soft surfaces and slipshod, seemingly de-
generate decoration. Handles and legs are almost universally used,
the former on unslipped ware and the latter on painted vessels.
The culture, as expressed by its pottery, has little artistic merit.

Imported wares at Los Llanitos for which we have comparative
material arc the following: deep orange and black on orange, red
and deep orange on orange, fine line polychrome, and Ulua poly-
chrome. Other wares, such as red on hard orange, red on cream
orange, and red and black on deep orange, have no known counter-
parts elsewhere, but are believed to be importations, as they do
not conform to the Los Llanitos tradition. ‘These imported wares
are characterized as a whole by rather thin-walled bowl forms,
good to excellent technique in painted decoration, and, excepting
fine line and Ulua polychrome, absence of footed vessels.

Contact between Los Elanitos and the Maya area was slight,
both distance and the broad Rio Lempa probably forming bar-
tiers to extensive intercourse. The Maya people known to the
Los Llanitoans were doubtless those of central and western El
Salvador, since some of the trade pottery at the site (fig. 25, f)
closely resembles specimens from the westcrn half of the Republic.
The two sherds of Ulua polychrome need not, as their name might
suggest, imply direct contact with the Indians of the Ulua drain-
age, as these forms and colors are also known in western El Salva-
dor,” The fragmentary vase found in Cache 1 of Mound 2 is
typically Ulua polychrome in shape, but the coloring, and
especially the design, are completely foreign to western Honduras
ceramics, and indeed, have not been reported from other regions.

Fine line polychrome ware presents an interesting
It is found throughout a wide area in El Salvador and Honduras,
yet its place of origin is unknown. Complete vessels of this ware
are quite common in the Lake Yojoa-Ulua Valley region, where
they differ radically from the standard polychrome pottery of the
area. The typical Ulua-Yojoa vessel is a thick-walled cylindrical
vase or deep bowl, with a lip which slopes in sharply from the
outside2* Fine line polychrome vessels, on the other hand, are
thin-walled vases and bowls, with unmodified lips They are
always decorated on the interior with an encircling series of long
“counters” hanging from the lip, whereas the Ulua-Yojoa thick-
walled specimens, aside from two or three plain horizontal bands,
are undecorated on the interior, The exterior designs on fine line
vessels follow a very set scheme. Almost invariably, they consist
of a series of panels, in each of which two extremely convention-
alized human figures are depicted, one crossing the other so as to
form an “X.” This position is probably connected with some kind
of ceremony, and for the present, may be designated by the term
“dancing."** Single figures, arranged in a procession, are sometimes
substituted for the “dancers,” but otherwise, the vessels are the
same. Associated with the human figures are two bantls of ex-
tremely conventionalized heads, one running around the base of
the vessel, the other around the rim.

The Peabody Muscom possesses a collection of several thousand
polychrome sherds from the Ulua Valley, excavated by G. B.
Gordon in 1896-97. Only about a dozen of these sherds, however,
are fine line polychrome, the remaindcr being mostly the thick-

Strong, Ridder, and Paul, 1088,       5, d, es Longyear, 19422,      he
* Serong, Kidder, and Paul, fg               !    ‘en

motifs on Gme Ene polychronac vessels.

walled Ulua type. Fine line polychrome sherds have also been
found in more recent excavations at Santa Rita, on the Ulua
River, but their number is not given." Nevertheless, it seems clear
that this type of ware is not common in Ulua sherd deposits bat
is found mainly in the form of compicte vessels. This implics that
these bowls and vases, decorated with “dancers” and processional
figures on the outside and with “counters” on the interior, had
some kind of ceremonial significance which entitled them to extra
care and inhibited their use by the common people. The large
percentage of complete specimens suggests that their deposition
in the ground was prepared, i.c., that they were placed in burials
or caches. The divergence, both in form and decoration, of these
vessels from the common Ulua painted wares points to an outside
origin. This source is unknown to us at present, but it should lie
somewhere in El Salvador or western Honduras.

As stated in the pottery summary, Los Llanitos fine line poly-
chrome is closely related, at least in shape, to the corresponding
ware (rapid style, fine line polychrome) at Tazumal. This supports
the contention that Los Llanitos' limited contacts with the outside
world were, in large part, with central and western El Salvador.

Linguistically, Los Llanitos is located in what was formerly
Lenca territory.™ Its affinities with the Lenca in an archaeological
sense, however, are not casy to define. Almost no excavation has
been conducted in south-central Honduras or castern El Salvador—
the arca of pre-Columbian Lenca dominance—and no “pure”
Lenca site has ever been reported upon, Tenampua, on the
Comayagua River in Honduras, is in Lenca territory and may be
a true Lenca site, but the amount of excavation done there per-
mits of no definite conclusions. Certainly, we have no body of
artifacts to which may positively be ascribed the term “Lenca,”
although certain El Salvadoran and Honduran pottery forms and
decorations, non-Maya in appearance and occurring in or near
Lenca territory, may be of Lenca origin. Several investigators in
this field have suggested various ceramics which meet these condi-
tions.

In castern El Salvador, Lothrop notes that figurine heads of a
“sub-Maya style” and a class of unpainted pottery, decorated with
small relief figures or by incising,” may be representative of Lenca
style, He also suggests that Usulutan ware, found most commonly
in eastern El Salvador, may be a Lenca product.”

In the Lake Yojoa region of Honduras, according to Strong,
Kidder, and Paul, “it seems highly probable” that Yojoa Bold
Animalistic polychrome ware is Lenca.”

Mrs. Doris Zemurray Stone, writing about the Sula-Ulua plain
in northern Honduras, has lisited 2 number of ceramic waits
which she believes to be of Lenca inspiration. These include
human and animal figurines of mixed types, “bulge bowls" with
loop handles and with geometric designs, the use of raised heads
and wings as a ceramic decoration, and the application of painted
scroll designs on vessels.

In Los Llanitos ceramics, these so-called "Lenca” features are
not common. Completely absent are: Usulutan ware, Yojoa Bold
Animalistic vessels, and scroll designs. Raised heads appear as
decorations, but only in the sense meant by Lothrop (on the neck
or body of vessels) and not around the rim, as Mrs. Stone illus-
trates. “Bulge bowls,” ic., vessels, with more or less globular
bodies, vertical necks, and two vertical handles joining neck to
body, are represented at Los Llanitos by only two sherds of red

4                                        ARCHAEOLOGICAL INVESTIGATIONS IN EL SALVADOR

on cream ware. The two-handled jars with vertical necks, described
under red on orange ware, may be a variant of this form, but the
possibility seems remote. Lothrop's unpainted pottery with relief
or incised decoration is too vagucly defined to be of use as a
determinant of Lenca or non-Lenca culture, The Los Llanitos
storage jars with faces applied on the neck, however, probably
belong in this category. 1 this is the case, they are by far the
most common “Lenca” pottery form at the site. Most of the Los
Llanitos figurines are of the cleft foot variety. The remainder,
however, can only be described as “archaic” in type and can be
duplicated in many other regions of Central Amcrica. While they
fit in with some of the specimens described as “Lenca,” they are
equally at home with figurines classed as “Maya” or “archaic.”
‘The “Lenca™ figurines, in other words, do not yet appear to have
any very definite characteristics. They have been described as
“sub-Maya” and “of mixed types,” but these terms mean little or
nothing in our present knowledge of figurine types and distri-
bution.

Thus it would appear that “Lenca” pottery is not common at
Los Llanitos. This can mean only that (a) Los Llamitos is not a
Lenca site, or (b) that if it is a Lenca site, we do not yet know
what is Lenca pottery and what is not.

Relations between Los Llanitos and the cast are very vague.
One fragmentary vessel-the tripod dish found on the western side
of Mound 3—appears to have definite castern characteristics in its
large, hollow, grotesque effigy legs. The painted designs, however,
are too weathered to be of use for comparison. Aside from this,
there is litle that bears a decided eastern tinge. Perhaps the
purplish-ted and brownish-black colors used on Los Llanitos
polychrome vessels might be said to show a certain resemblance
to some of the paints used on Nicaraguan and Costa Rican pottery,
but this is much too indefinite to scrve as a basis for any con-
clusions. We must let the question of eastern influences go, then,
with the confession that, if there are any at Los Llanitos, we are
unable to recognize them.

SUMMARY AND DATING

The best estimate of the present population of the region
surrounding Los Llanitos may perhaps be gained from the vital
statistics of the pueblo of Chirilagua, under whose jurisdiction
lies a great deal of this territory. Chirilagua is situated in the
midst of the hills of Jucuaran, about 10 km. south of Los Llanitos
as the crow flies, The district embraced by the town includes the
central and castern parts of the Jucuaran Hills and Lake Camalotal,
or Olomega, to the northcast.* The northwestern boundaries are
not given in our source, but we believe they extend at least as
far as Los Llanitos. This arca, then, contains both highland and
valley, but inter-communication among its inhabitants is constant
and casy, as it no doubt was in the past. A large part of this region,
at least, was probably influenced by Los Llanitos in pre-Columbian
times, for although it was certainly not the only site in the vicinity,
it was one of the most important.

The population of Chirilagua and the territory under its juris-
diction is, according to the 1941 statistics, 5245. The number of
archacological remains, as well as accounts of early travelers in
eastern El Salvador, show that this section of the Republic was
densely populated before the Conquest, so an estimate of 4000-

™ Jiménez, 194%, p. 127,

™ Jiménez, 1948, p. 187.

2 Linné, 1929, pp. 254-55. Map 1.

* Information of Dr. A. V. Kidder.

* Butler, 2930, pl. VI, j.

5000 for the population of the Chirilagua region at that time
would probably not be far wrong. This may seem a large number
of people to be served by such a relatively small site as Los Llanitos,
but the need for a large city or ceremonial center was not as great
in eastern El Salvador as it was, for instance, in the Maya area or
the Valley of Mexico, In most of Central America, it is to be pre-
sumed that the common people lived in ancient times much as
they do now, in scattered groups of a few houses each, near the
milpas on which grew their sustaining crops, They repaired to the
larger centers only for religious purposes, or to attend fairs and
markets in order to trade their wares. In the Los Lianitos region,
judging from the lack of carved monuments and claborate cere-
monial structures, religion was not as formalized nor as elaborate
as among the Maya, and consequently needed Jess paraphernalia
in the way of “nunneries,” temples, and “palaces” than did that
of their neighbors to the west and north. A small ball court and
a few mounds upon which temporary structures could be erected
to suit the occasion was evidently all the religion the Los
Lianitoans required. The low “house mounds” surrounding the
site possibly bore the thatch and daub residences of the few
priests and chieftans necessary for the control of the town.

The arts and crafts of the natives resident in this region, to
judge from the material remains left us, were not elaborate. Stone-
working was confined almost entirely to cutting slabs of soft rock,
or quarrying out rough blocks to be used as fill. The single
polished slab of fine, hard stone (in Cache 1, Mound 7) is not
typical, nor is it likely that it was made by the people of Los
Llanitos. Only a fragment of an originally larger piece when found,
it was evidently in the same condition when placed in the cache,
for no other portions of the slab were uncovered in the excava-
tion. This leads to the natural conclusion that the original slab
was made for some other purpose than the cache, and probably
by people other than the Los Llanitoans. The latter merely found
the fragment somewhere and, appreciating its workmanship,
erected it in one of their cercmonial deposits. No examples of
sculptured stone were found at the site.

From the amount of pottery recovered in the excavations, it
may be deduced that the ceramic industry of Los Llanitos was an
extensive one. As noted above, however, it had little artistic merit,
and this was evidently appreciated by the inhabitants themselves,
since the vessels found in their caches differ considerably from the
sherd material, and presumably represent imported wares. From
where most of these were imported is, at present, an unsolved
question. Variants of the incense-burners in Cache 2, Mound 2,
however, are found over a wide area. Ladletype burners occur
from the Valley of Mexico to northern South America. and
incensarios with spiked decoration have been reported from
Kaminaljuyu”* and the Alta Verapaz” in the Guatemala highlands,
Copan, Honduras," Yucatan,” and several sites in El Salvador.”
The combination of spiked decoration with ladle form—the most
common Los Llanitos type—has, however, a more limited distribu-
tion. Aside from the above site, it is known to occur only at
Campana-San Andres,” in the western half of El Salvador. The
Los Llanitos specimens, therefore, may cither have originated in
the west or been inspired by the wares of that region.

Other Los Llanitos industries can only be inferred. The pres-
ence of spindle whorls indicates that cotton was spun and, pre-
sumably, woven, Manos and metates suggest maize as the prin-
cipal crop and staple food, and an occasional projectile point of

EXCAVATIONS AT LOS LLANITOS                                                45

obsidian shows that the hunting of game supplemented this diet.

Any dating of the occupancy of Los Llanitos must, at present
remain vague and unsatisfactory. Since pottery wares considered
more ot less typical of the site are not found in other, better known
regions, we must depend on imported or exotic vessels and sherds
for our correlations. To begin with, however, we can eliminate
certain periods from consideration, largely on the basis of nega-
tive evidence.

No trace of Spanish colonial influence is present at Los Llanitos.
This circumstance almost certainly places the site in pre-Conquest
times, since the southern half of eastern El Salvador was invaded
in 1530 and extensively occupied by Spanish clergy and settlers
from that time on. Any village existing in this territory after the
early part of the sixteenth century, then, should be expected to
show signs of Spanish influence. This should be especially true of
towns in the vicinity of Los Llanitos, since through this area
passed one of the principal routes between San Salvador and
Nicaragua.

If Los Llanitos was not late enough to have been influenced
by the Conquistadores, neither was it occupied at a very early
time, In the first place, true Usulutan ware, a type of pottery
associated with carly remains at various aboriginal centers in El
Salvador and Honduras, does not appear at Los Llanitos. Secondly,
polychrome pottery is found throughout the entire depth of
cultural deposits at the site, indicating that the town was in-
habited only after the addition of multi-colored wares to the
carlier monochrome and bichrome ceramics. Thirdly, all the im-
ported pottery at Los Llanitos for which we have comparative
material from other sites, occurs in relatively late deposits in the
latter. It is to this last point that we must now turn, in order to
narrow down the time of occupation of Los Llanitos.

Red and deep orange on orange ware is one of the very few
Los Llanitos ceramics which may be traced back to Maya origin.
In general form and design, it fits in with the common polychrome
wares at Copan. ‘The latter may be characterized as follows:
simple- or composite-silhouctte bowls, decorated on the exterior
with stylized glyph bands, and vertically compressed human
figures, the latter seated or reclining. The interior bears a varicty
of large stylized glyphs and rosette patterns. Designs are usually
executed in hematite red and black paint on an orange surface;
and a deep orange is frequently used to fill in motifs outlined
in red.* This same class of pottery has been found at Gampana-
San Andres® and in Later Phase deposits at Tazumal.* It is also
commonly observed in private collections from central and
western El Salvador. Los Llanitos red and deep orange on orange
ware corresponds to this type of polychrome in many respects.
Although the red paint used is not hematite red, and no black
paint is employed, vessel shapes and the technique of decoration
are essentially the same (fig. 25, $). At Copan, hematite red and

© Boges, 19492.

* Longyear, agqoa, table 4, The 11.3.0.0.0 correlation of Maya and Christian
dates has heen used throughout this chapter. The Goodman-Martinee-Thompson
correlation would place all Christian dates two hundred and sixty years earlier in

time.

“ Longyear, 19408, fig. 11,0, got         * See Appendix C.

black ware is typical of the Acropolis Phase, which dates from
about 9.8.0.0.0 (853 A.D.) to 9.18.10.0.0 (1060 A.D.).“ At Tazumal,
itis found in the Later Phase, which appears to correspond roughly
to the Acropolis Phase at Copan. On the basis of red and deep
otange on orange ware alone, then, Los Llanitos should be ap-
proximately contemporaneous with the Acropolis Phase also,

Fine line polychrome has been found in stratigraphic excava-
tions at Copan and Santa Rita, Honduras, and Tazumal. At the
first site, it appears in deposits of the Acropolis Phase, sub-phase A
(9.8.0.0.0 to 9,12.5.0.0, or 853 to 937 A.D.).* At Tazumal, it is re-
ported from the Later Phase.* The excavations at Santa Rita
revealed fine line polychrome sherds only in the two lowest
polychrome levels.** Nothing like an exact chronology has been
established for the Ulua Valley, but trade pieces, found at Copan,
indicate that Ulua polychrome wares are contemporaneous, at
least in part, with the Acropolis Phase,“ and certain tie-ups with
the Tepeu ceramics of the Peten hint that they may have lasted
until at least 10.8.0.0,0 (1247 A.D.).""

Incensarios decorated with spikes, similar to those found in
Cache 2, Mound 2 at Los Llanitos, are late wherever found in the
Maya area. At Kaminaljuyu, they are assigned to the Pamplona
Phase, roughly equivalent to the Peten Tepcu,” In Yucatan, they
certainly do not appear until after 9.14.0.0.0 (971 AD), and the
majority of them date considerably later than that, Spiked
incensarios are found in the Alta Verapaz in Period H, Chipal £,
or just before 1000 a.p.,** and at Copan, Honduras, they are re-
ported only from post-Acropolis Phase deposits, making them later
than 9.18.10.0.0 (1060 a.0.). The Campana-San Andres material
has not yet been published, but it may be said here that the site as
a whole seems to run from the Acropolis Phase at Copan up to
possibly Aztec times. Spiked incensarios from Tazumal are all
Later Phasc.* The evidence, therefore, points to spiked incensarios
being later than goo a.p., and most of them are probably con-
siderably later.

All the foregoing gives us little on which to base a date for
Los Llanitos. Not enough is known of eastern El Salvador archae-
ology to postulate temporal relations with any degree of accuracy.
It would probably do more harm to force this issuc at present
than to let it go with no comment at all. The inner craving of
every archaeologist, however, the need to assign some sort of date
to every cultural manifestation he encounters, will not be denied.
The author therefore, and with due hesitancy, places the occupa-
tion of Los Llanitos in a period approximately contemporancous
with that of the Acropolis Phase at Copan—in other words, from
about g.8.0.0.0 (853 A.D.) to g-18.10.0.0 (1060 A.p.). Further than
that, the evidence at hand docs not permit him to go. The actual
duration of the occupancy, whether it lies entirely within the dates
above or whether it overlaps one or the other—these questions are
for future research to solve,

By StanLEY MH. Boces
