APPENDIX C. EXCAVATIONS IN CENTRAL AND
WESTERN EL SALVADOR

By Srantey H. Bocas

The present reconnaissance and cxploratory excavations in cen-
tral and western El Salvador were intended to test the accuracy of
the older site lists, to locate ruins which have recently come to light,
and to discover as much information as possible about these rem-
nants of former civilizations, In the time allotted—February to
June—some of these aims have been achieved in a small way. Cer-
tain sites formerly noted separately were found to pertain to archac-
ological zones and in some cases information was found referring
to the cultures involved in such groupings of mounds. A few pot-
tery types found in collections could be assigned to certain ruins
and in some cases to specific strata or building periods. What
might be called “sculptural areas” are beginning to cmerge from
this reconnaissance; for example, the “death sculpture” area, of
unknown cultural affiliations, may now be delimited with some
degree of success (Part II, p. 10). Certain pottery types, such as
the “scraped-slip” ware, were found to extend over a much wider
territory in El Salvador than hitherto supposed. At the same time,
many ruins reported formerly were found cither to be non-existent
or wrongly located.t

It is apparent that notes on the older site lists should be made
public and that all lists should be revised frequently as new in-
formation comes to light, The present list has been checked care-
fully but there can be no doubt of its inaccuracy in some respects:
for example, most of the information on the Department of Ahua-
chapan is derived from earlier site lists, modern newspaper ac-
counts, magazine articles, and conversations with finca owners and
other inhabitants of the district, Perhaps the only value of a list

of this kind is that it exposes the lack of information and may lead
to more detailed accounts in the future?

Excavations at Hacienda Tula and at Mounds 1 and 2 of the
Tazumal Group occupicd altogether two months, The value of
these was in the demonstration of connections between certain
pottery types, the suggestion of ceramic periods at Tazumal, and
the connection of some well-known El Salvadoran wares with a
distinct type of architecture, Further excavation may lead in time
to the establishment of definite El Salvadoran culture areas which
were not entirely the results of northern or southern invasions.

It is hoped that the raw materials of the reconnaissance and
excavations will prove of some value to the reader. Unfortunately,
comparative material from Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua, and
Mexico is entirely lacking in El Salvador, and publications on
archaeological subjects are extremely hard to obtain here. This has
necessitated a report almost solely factual in content, although
many comparisons with these facts undoubtedly exist which would
show connections and relationships unsuggested here.

‘The writer is extremely indebted to the following persons, with-
out whose assistance this report could not have appeared: Professor
José Andres Orantes, don Jorge Hurtarte Monzón, Ing. Augusto
Baratta, don Salvador Aguillón, don Carlos Portillo, don Rogelio
Arriaza, don Flavio Valiente, Dr. José Valiente, don Mauricio Ga-
llardo, don Bernardo Alfonso, Dr. Oscar Emeterio Salazár, and don
Eugenio Aguilar h. The constant co-operation of the Ministry of
Public Instruction and the unfailing kindness of El Salvadorans
everywhere was of the greatest aid throughout the investigations.

I. TULA

This small site lies on the Hacienda Tula of don Bernardo Al-
fonso, about 20 km. south of Santa ‘Lecla, in the Department of
La Libertad, A detailed description of its location and general ap-
pearance can be found in Part II of this Memoir.

A complete plumbate jar (pl. XTV-A, 2), now in the possession
of don Mauricio Gallardo of Santa Tecla, was found on Hacienda
Tula, not far from the owner's house. Believing that there might
be some connection between the makers of the plumbate vessel
and the builders of the low mounds which comprise the site of
Tula proper, the writer test-trenched the two largest mounds, with
the kind permission of Señor Alfonso.

Two test trenches, cach dug to sterile soil in Mounds 1 and 2,
supplied pottery and information on the structure of the mounds.
Mound g, attached to the cast side of Mound 2, may be no more
than a modern refuse deposit since rocks are more common here
than on cither of the excavated mounds, All three hillocks, how-
ever, bore some rocks on the surface, and it is likely that weeds and
stones taken from the flats during cultivation were deposited here.

* See Part TT for reconnaissance reports,

"See Part IL and Appendix D,

STRATIGRAPHY

In both of the tested mounds, the stratigraphy was clearly marked
and similar. That in Mound 2 is as follows:

Mound 2

Hard brown humus

White volcanic ash mixed with earth
White volcanic ash

Pumice and cinders or igneous pebbles
Dark brown earth (humus)

Grey clay

Talpetate

All sherds at Tula were found to proceed from the lower por-
tion of the white ash layer, a situation not at all uncommon in El
Salvador but especially common in the area about San Salvador it-
self.* No complete vessels were found in the excavations, which sug:
gests a temporary camp of people wandering about during an erup-
layer, 3 m. deep. Numerous vessels in the Salazár collection and all in the de Sola

collection were excavated from ash deposits in the barrios of San Jacinto, Santa
Anita, etc., of San Salvador.

## Page 56

_Extraction method: OCR._

54                                      ARCHAEOLOGICAL INVESTIGATIONS IN EL SALVADOR

tion in search of safety, water, and food. The pottery caches of
complete vessels in the San Salvador area may have resulted from
people leaving their possessions when they fled from the menace
of an eruption in their immediate vicinity, While this is entirely
supposition, the fact that sherds of like types occur in ash deposits
of like nature at least supports the time clement involved; depth
of the deposits suggests eruption of a volcano near modern San
Salvador, possibly the now considered extinct Volcan San Jacinto,

Mound 1 at Hacienda Tula shows some difference in its ash
stratum from that of Mound g, although these are clearly of the
same time and deposition, lie on level terrain, and are very close
together. The lower part of this layer in Mound 1 is laminated,
possibly due to chemical action. The brown earth in the upper

part of the ash in Mound 2 (not apparent in Mound 1) is cer-
tainly the result of animal and ant burrowings. The sherds on the
surface of both mounds had been removed from the ash deposit of
Mound 2 by animals, and distributed over the surface of the
mounds by modern cultivation of the savannah. This is proved by
the fact that some surface sherds fitted other sherds excavated from
the Mound e ash.

Structurally, the Tula mounds show: (1) That the mound ap-
pearance has no bearing on the archaeological material. (2) ‘That
only one period of temporary occupation is involved. (3) That this
brief occupation occurred during an eruption, cither while the ash
was falling or in a brief interim between the deposition of pumice
and ash.

STONE IMPLEMENTS

‘The only stone implement which was found was a fragment of
a “club head.” It is circular in form and made of black scoria.

The perforation which was made through the center of the head
is of hour-glass shape.

POTTERY

Not one even halfcomplete vessel was found, and the sherds
excavated bear no apparent relation to the plumbate jar found
some 2 km, from the site and mentioned previously. Sherds were
of like types and in approximately equal numerical proportions
from the two mounds: consequently, the illustrations of the Tula
types as well as the following typology relate to the material in toto
rather than by provenience.

I, Polychrome

A. COLOR. DULL RED AND DARK PURPLE ON LIGHT TO DARK ORANGE
SLIP.

Illustration. Fig. 50, a, a’,

Surface. Usually hard-fired, highly polished, and surface lustrous.
Rarely, paint is dull and poorly fired and will not resist cleaning
brush,

Paste. Usually dark red in color, evenly but not very hard-fired,
of medium-coarse texture.

Form. (a) Tetrapodal double-curve bowls. Feet are usually solid
but rarely are hollow. The bottom is usually shallowly round, oc-
casionally flat. Rim always flares and the lip is generally sharply
rounded, Wall sherds of the Tula specimens vary in thickness from
48 mm., 3 ci. below the rim. Basal angle is marked but never
ridged nor sharp. (b) Annular-based, double-curve bowls. Except
for the base, this form is identical in every respect with the tetra-
podal bow! form.* Base of the Tula example was 3.2 cm. high, 7
mm. thick, both measurements being in the range of other ex-
amples of this form. (c) Double-curve vase. At Tula, only rim and
hasalangle sherds were found, The type is flat-based with a
rounded basal angle, concave side walls, and flaring rim. Lip is
usually sharply rounded. Thickness of side walls varies from 5 to 7
mm. An example in the Soundy collection from the Chalchuapa
Zone represents the form very well, though design varics from the
central El Salvador style in several particulars,

Decoration. Geometric designs, both interior and exterior in
bowls, exterior only on other forms, Commonest design is in hori-
zontal bands with vertical or oblique broad purple lines, and dots,
often separating spiral or wave elements. Exterior design of bowl
forms is, in all examples seen, cither a single wavy red line en-
circling the vessel, or a series of widely separated small red rec-

‘This base, may or may not be painted: if painted, the design is a broad, red en-

tangles. Horizontal, broad red rim and basal-angle lines frame the
purple design on the interior. (Only fragments of rims and side
walls were found at Tula, Examples of bow] types in the Glub In-

interior bottom of the vessels.)

3, COLOR. DULL RED AND BLACK ON LIGHT ORANGE SLIP.

Surface, Hard-fired but not highly polished,

Paste. Apparently same as foregoing.

Form, Bowls, tetrapodal or annular-based.

Decoration, Same as foregoing with black replacing purple in
the design,

C. CoLok. RED AND ORANGE ON LIGHT ORANGE SLIP.

Surface. Not very hard-fircd, polished. In places not eroded,
lustrous.

Form. Small, simple-silhouette bowls of type with round base.
Lip is sharp and rim is slightly inturned, Wall thickness 4 mm.,
2.5 cm. below rim.

Decoration. Stylized Maya glyphs with heavy red line outlines
and, in red fine line arcas inside the glyphs, orange filling, This
design occurs in a horizontal band below the rim on exterior of the
vessel. Interior shows only a narrow red rim band.

D, Cotor. DULL RED AND ORANGE ON LIGHT ORANGE TO CREAM
stip. Overfiring has in some cases darkened the cream slip to
orange.

Surface, Polished, in places lustrous. Not normally’ hard, ap-
parently.

Paste. Medium-coarse, dark red, evenly but not hard-fired.

Form, Probably a double-curve bowl, to judge from rim and
body sherds, possibly footed or annular based, Rim flares and lip
is rounded. Side walls are 7 mm. thick, 5 cm. below rim.

Decoration. Exterior shows red decoration on cream or orange
slip: two concentric rectangular broad line elements in horizontal
band below two parallel broad red rim lines. Design probably re-
peated four or more times to encircle entire vessel, Interior shows
element of two concentric semi-circles in red repeated many times
to embellish rim. Below this clement are three broad red horizontal
lines encircling vessel. Simplicity of design and obscurity of color
makes comparisons with other El Salvadoran types altogether im-
possible.

APPENDIX C. EXCAVATIONS IN CENTRAL AND WESTERN EL SALVADOR

E, Conor. BLACK on DARK ORANGE sL1P. (Included in polychrome
hecause the one sherd found is obviously from a wellknown El
Salvadoran polychrome, the usual type showing red and black on
orange, The Tula sherd is from the body of the vessel and is not
large enough or perhaps fortunate cnough to show all colors.)

Surface. Smooth, never obviously polished, and never hard-fired.
Black paint is so thick that it makes ridges perceptible to the touch.
Slip is very thin.

Paste. Very fine, white, powdery to the touch, either untempered
or with some extremely fine temper, It is not hard-fired. This Tula
example is not typical of the paste usually found combined with
this type of painting or decoration. ‘The usual type is medium-
fired, with rather coarse paste, grey or light brown in color.

Form. Most common form is a simple-silhouctte bowl with round
base and sharp, round lip, The Tula sherd is from the body and its
thickness is 5 mm.

Decoration. Tula sherd shows only part of a design, probably
a stylized glyph or a geometric figure on the exterior. Interior plain
slipped orange.

IL. Dichrome

A, COLOR. BRICK RED ON GREY CLAY.

Ilustration, Pl. XIV-A, 5.

Surface. Generally smoothed, rarely polished. Not hard-fired
generally.

Paste. Usually rather coarse, seldom hard, but nearly always
evenly fired and generally grey, occasionally dark brown, Many
large inclusions are usual.

Form, Globular-body storage jars with vertical or funnel necks.
Tula examples are apparently from vertical neck forms, Bases are
usually round; lips are thickened and round. Two vertical loop
handles on the upper body curve are almost always present.

Decoration. Commonest decoration is of a face on the neck of
the vessel, Face is formed by filletting the nose, cars, and brows and
is sometimes added to by punctate car-plugs, nostrils, mouth, and
eyes. Brows, ears, and nosc, in the Tula specimens, were painted
red. Below the face, on the upper half of the body, a geometric
design based on the truncated triangle element and usually formed
by broad red lines with either fine or broad line filling ornament
is common. No sherds of the body were found at Tula but several
sherds from the neck indicate the type. Painting is usually careless,
the lines broad and uneven. Handles of the vessel have small red
rectangles painted on their exterior aspects.

B. Cowon, THIck WINTER SLIP ON CLAY, OCCASIONALLY WITH
SECONDARY THIN RED SLIP COVERING THE WHITE, This is type of the

“scraped slip  technique, so common in San Andres and at Tazu-
mal Mound 1.

Illustration. Fig. 30, c.

Surface. Smoothed, never polished, at Tula not hard-fired, but
sometimes smoke-blackened. The white paint is so thick that it
forms ridges where scraped: these undulations are perceptible Lo
the touch,

Paste. Tula specimens are evenly-, but not hard-fired, red-brown
to grey color with many large inclusions.

Form, Cooking vessels of various forms, At Tula the most com-
mon forms scem to have been deep bowls or storage jars. Bases are
customarily round in all forms, Lips vary greatly, are usually
grooved in bowl forms, round in open-mouthed jars, frequently
flat in storage jar types. Wall thickness is gencrally greatest in jar
and bowl forms, where it may be 1.4 cm. In storage jars wall thick-
ness averages 7 mm,

55

Decoration, Tula designs consist entirely of low wavy lines
encircling the vessel horizontally on the exterior. This design was
formed while the white slip was semi-dry by using cither three or
four fingers of one hand or a brush or stick so cut that parallel
stripes could be imparted by scraping the slip. This results in
a negative design, clay color, except when the vessel is secondarily
slipped. In the latter case, the entire vessel is red with the wavy
lines of the scraped white slip appearing only as low corrugations
of the surface. Decoration is usual on only the upper half or two-
thirds of a vessel and at Tula was confined entirely to the exterior
surface.

C. CoLok, RED SLIP OVER WHITE SLIP.

Surface. Smooth and usually lustrous. Not hard-fired, however,
and the red is frequently rubbed off at least partially. When sur-
face is unmarred by scratches or erosion, ellect is of a red-orange
slip.

56                                     ARCHAEOLOGICAL INVESTIGATIONS IN EL SALVADOR

DISCUSSION

Stratigraphy at Hacienda Tula indicates that only one period is
involved, The pottery, of types common to El Salvador but, with
the exception of three polychrome sherds, unreported elsewhere,
allows a certain number of inferences to be drawn concerning con-
temporary wares, and, to a certain extent, of the time clement
involved.

Tula polychromes la and Ib and dichrome Ila are all represented
by complete specimens from the Club Internacional collection,
which contained, in the same deposit with such types, Maya vessels
such as those figured by Spinden* and Longyear.* Dichrome Ta
is most common in central and especially eastern El Salvador and
has been considered by Lotbrop" as probably Lenca in inspiration,
though without clear proof of such connection.

Tula polychrome Ie is, in decoration, though not in ware, of the
type figured by Longyear? as relating to Copan Acropolis Phase,
Sub-phase A, and is very common in central and western El Salva-
dor. Dichrome IIb has been found in quantity during excavations
at the Gampana-San Andres Group and in the experimental trench
at Tazumal Group, Mound 1." At the latter site, a change of ware
is noticeable between the early and the late ceramic phases though

this “scraped-slip” technique is utilized in decoration throughout.
From the Tazumal excavations it appears that Tula Dichrome
Ib relates in technique to that of the Later Phase of Tazumal.
Dichrome Te relates to Campana-San Andres sherds contemporary
with the Ia or “scraped-slip” ware. Monochrome wares at Tula
are very similar in shape and not noticeably different in ware from
those of Campana-San Andres and central El Salvador generally.

1t must be apparent, therefore, that Hacienda Tula, late Tazu-
mal and the Campana-San Andres Group are at least roughly con-
temporaneous on the basis of the pottery. This contemporancity
may be extended to a number of other pottery types found over
most of central and eastern El Salvador which the writer hopes to
describe later.

The connections with Copan are very clear but the chronology,
ceramically, of Copan has yet to be defined absolutely. Longyear
places Maya types such as those mentioned in the foregoing in the
Acropolis Phase, probably dating around g.18.10.0.0 or later.’
Unfortunately, we cannot be sure as yet whether Copan ceramic
chronology greatly precedes or whether it follows that of central El
Salvador.

Il, TAZUMAL

Mounds 1 and 2 were excavated during April and May, 1942."
Work was concentrated mainly on the north and west sides,
especially of the platform, of Mound 1, and on the northeast cor-
ner of Mound g. To facilitate the recording of pottery and stone
specimens, the excavated arca of these mounds was staked out in
five-foot squares and these sections were numbered according to a
grid. Wall lines and floor levels were plotted by transit—the former
from sixteen different triangulation points related to the grid
system, the latter from an arbitrary level of 100 ft., established

at the northwest corner, atop a modern water reservoir on the
platform of Mound 1. Excavations were purely exploratory in
nature, not only because of the brief time available, but also be-
cause it was felt that such investigations could provide the basis
for thorough study at some future date but certainly could not set-
tle the many questions sure to crop up in an extensive site. The
north side of the platform of Mound 1 was studied with especial
care because it has been used as a mine of material for modern
buildings in Chalchuapa and erosion was destroying it rapidly.

ARCHITECTURE AND CONSTRUCTION

The construction of the excavated Tazumal mounds is extremely
similar to that encountered at Campana-San Andres. In both cases
adobe was the principal core material and a cementlike mortar
formed the facing for walls and floors. Adobe blocks or bricks,
however, are much rarer in the Tazumal structures and apparently
were not made by the mass-production methods of San Andres,
since there is more individual variation in the blocks at Tazumal.
Here what may be called the large blocks averaged 1.2 by 1.2 by
-5 fe, while the smaller filling blocks averaged -8 by .9 by .5 ft.
When such blocks were used, stones were not, although in all cases
adobe mortar was binding material. Blocks were found to form
the floor at the base of the northeast corner of Mound 2 and to be
the terrace core on the west side of the platform of Mound 1, at
least in the area of N42-44-18-19W. Elsewere, in the excavated
portions, cither adobe mortar alone or this mortar with boulders
was used.

An important variation in construction is the difference between
the carliest terraces and steps and the latest terraces of the Mound
1 platform. The former are constructed with a very large propor-
tion of mortar, while the latter contain relatively many more
stones, especially in the vertical walls. Furthermore, the amount

of facing “cement” is much greater in the carlier constructions
than in the later, Mound 2 walls excavated were entirely of the
“late” type as was the terraced temple substructure rising over the
platform of Mound +. Finally, all steps known to be late on Mound
1, such as, for example, the west steps of the temple substructure,
are formed with many stones, lite adobe, and lite “cement.”

Stones used in the core are generally water-worn boulders or
rough pieces of scoria. ‘To form the “late” vertical walls, however,
slabs of a slate-like rock locally termed iaja (slab), were generally
laid horizontally (with the chin side outward) in combination with
boulders presenting a flat side outward. Over this stone wall was
spread the facing “cement.”

The cement-like mortar is in most cases rather coarse in tex-
ture, light grey in color, and contains a high proportion of small,
rough pebbles appearing to be derived from crushed scoria or
other volcanic rock. It is probable that lime was combined with
crushed stone to make this “cement.” Walls faced with such ma-
terial are very smooth, at least as much so as modern cement con-
structions finished by use of steel trowels.

Two walls were found faced with a lime mortar which departed
considerably from the Tazumal norm, In both cases the facing
Lake Yojoa, Honduras. Technique of this example resembles Tazumal Group
Early Phase type of “scraped-slip" more than that of Tula but is apparently the
same technique.

APPENDIX C. EXCAVATIONS IN CENTRAL AND WESTERN EL SALVADOR                            59

material was extremely fine, nearly white, and almost velvety in
smoothness. One example, the basal terrace of Mound 2, was con-
siderably croded and, because of moisture, very soft. The other,
an early, vertical wall on the west side of the platform of Mound
1, was very hard. Obscurity of the building phase of the latter
prevents use of constructional identification in establishing an
architectural sequence.

In architectural plan, Mound 1 is extremely complicated, due
to changes made at least six times in the platform and three or
more in the temple substructure, Futhermore, destruction of the
upper part of the north side of the platform by vandals makes a
complete plan, of this portion at least, well-nigh impossible. ‘The
map and proliles of Mounds 1 and 2 (figs. 28, 29), show what is
known at present—a tantalizingly small amount. It appears cer-
tain that the earliest constructional phase differed radically from
the later, so radically, in fact, that the writer is inclined to believe
that several platforms were joined to form the final one. Following
the earliest (IVa) phase yet found, however, the plan of the plat-
form did not change greatly until the final building phases (T and
11b). Phases [Vb to Ia secm to involve a platform having three or
more terraces, of which the lowest was steeply battered, the upper
two vertical-walled, In Phase Ila the entire north side of the plat-
form secms to have consisted of a flight of nine steps surmounted
by at least two vertical terraces. OF Phase IIb only four basal steps re-
main, together with a fragmentary wall. Phase I shows the same
idea as Tb. Whether the steps of these two latest building periods
rose to the full height of the platform-floor or whether they rose
only part way, Lo connect, via a landing, with steps of la cannot
be determined. An interesting feature is the balustraded flight of
steps of Phase IIa. This type of stair construction is almost identi-
cal with that noted on Mounds 2-4 at the Campana-San Andres
Group,

The interior of the platform is a plaza, enclosed by a stepped
or terraced wall. The north side, interior, of the platform, as well
as the west side, near the northwest corner, is stepped in the latest
phase (which may here be of any time after Phase TTD), partially
stepped (as at the north side near the northeast corner) and par-
tially terraced in the next preceding phase, and merely terraced
in the earliest phases (IVa and b). The cast side and the south side
of this platform-plaza show only two vertically walled low terraces
in the excavations made but other, carlier features may be con-
cealed. The west side trenches exposed no enclosing walls, terraces,
or steps between stakes Nig and N32. South of stake Nig, three
vertical low terraces were found in the latest phase of construction
and at least two in an earlicr phase, North of stake N32, two con-
structional phases of steps or very low terraces were found.

In sections N5g-56.14-15E, the east side of a north entry to the
elevated platform-plaza was found, The west side of this entry
had been destroyed, and its breadth reduced at least once. Appar-
ently, during the later phases (Ma and later), one rose from the
exterior base of the platform at least nine steps to a narrow terrace,
then surmounted this low terrace to the level of the interior plat-
form-floor and entered the plaza directly. How many such en-
trances there were can be shown only by further excavation, though
we know an approach from the west side existed.

The temple substructure rising from the platform-floor con-
sists of a number of vertical terraces on all sides, with a flight of
very low, shallow steps in the center of the west side. At Icast two
flights of superimposed steps exist here, the latest possibly con-
temporary with the final terrace constructions of the temple base.

Many of the terraces and steps on the west side have been destroyed
by the modern search for building material and the erosion conse-
quent upon such mining. A curious feature on this west side is
the existence of a final-phase rectangular “block” of boulder-and-
adobe masonry covering the Jatest steps and projecting to the edge
of the platform. This is very similar to the situation found on the
north side of Mound 1 at the Campana-San Andres Group (see
p- 10).

Excavations at the base of the northeast corner of the temple sub-
structure revealed terraces of at least three building phases. At
the southwest corner two are clear, and a third may exist; on the
west side the steps of two phases, followed by the masonry block
or “porch” noted above, indicate at least three. Since this evi-
dence was found very near the surface, it is likely that the temple
substructure was rebuilt at least as many times as the platform
underlying it. The top of the pyramid has been seriously damaged
but on the slopes some squared building stones and what appears
to have been a roof drain were found. This strongly suggests that
a masonry temple once existed atop the mound,

Only two instances of wall decoration were found, What is ap-
parently the earliest, and by far the most interesting, is a fragmen-
tary incised design found on a vertical wall in section N42-43-19W,
on the western exterior face of the platform of Mound 1. This
wall was of a fine, hard mortar, and the shallow incising had been
done while this mortar was still moist. Later the wall had been
painted white. The wall seems to have been a rectangular projec-
tion or pilaster from the west face of the platform and represents
the earliest building phase yet found in this part of the mound.
Tt was covered by at least one phase, and possibly two, of construe-
tion (fig. 29, b). The upper design strongly resembles some plumed
sacerdotal figures on polychrome Maya pottery and seems ta be
connected with a mask, or possibly a glyph. The lower figure
resembles a bird figure with, possibly, a human head.

Phase Ila construction, on the north side near the northeast
corner of the platform of Mound 1, bears the only other decorative
elements found in the Tazumal Group. ‘This consists of a cornice
of “cement”-covered adobe at the upper edge of the lowest terrace
surmounting the nine steps leading from ground level to the plat-
form-plaza. The use of cornices to embellish the edge of terraces
is common at Campana-San Andres. In combination with the
simple lines, rounded corners, and steps, the effect is simple and
very pleasing.

Very little may be said concerning the orientation of Mound 1.
The map of this structure indicates that building lines changed
considerably as additions were made to the earliest constructions
as in some cases (c.g., the Phase Ila north steps of the platform)
the building lines were not constant during the same period. This
may be partially accounted for by the type of masonry involved,
since adobe is more difficult to lay in a straight line than is squared
stone.

Mounds 3 and 4, in their alignment, strongly suggest ball court
mounds. This is supported somewhat by the fact that at the west
end of what may be termed the court appears a long, low mound
partially enclosing the area. Any mound which may have enclosed
the eastern end zone has been destroyed by the modern cemetery
wall. Mound g has been more than half destroyed, but its basal
dimensions are still fairly clear. The two mounds are of similar
heights. Unfortunately no excavation to determine whether they
actually represent ball court mounds is possible, for both are cov-
ered with modern graves.

to                                     ARCHAEOLOGICAL INVESTIGATIONS IN EL SALVADOR

STONE OBFECTS

The best known stone object from the Tazumal Group is the
stela, otherwise known as the “Virgén.”"* Apparently, this monolith
was found somewhere at the base of the west side of Mound 1, but
whether this means the base of the temple substructure or the base
of the platform is conjectural. Barbarena speaks only of the base
of the west side or slope, while an old inhabitant of Chalchuapa,
who swore that he was present when the figure was removed by
Barbarena, stated that the stone lay face downward on the west
side of the platform, near where it drops to ground level. The
writer is disposed to believe, from the plan of Mound 1, that the
stela originally stood at the base of the platform rather than upon
it, although this suggestion is founded mainly on the fact that in
the latest phase, no space on the platform centered on the staircase
existed duc to the large block projecting westward. Furthermore,
it seems more logical to place such a large, heavy figure of this
type at the base rather than atop a structure. At Copan and
Quirigua stelae are not uncommon topping elevated constructions.

The Tazumal stela is at present in the Museo Nacional in San
Salvador, and its basal portions are covered by a cement base. Its
exact height, consequently, cannot be given. As nearly as the
writer was able to measure, however, it is 12.3 ft. high, 3.5 ft. at
its widest, and 1.4 ft. thick. The reverse is smoothed but uncarved.
The front bears a standing human figure in ceremonial costume
and bearing some sort of ceremonial bar or staff. The left arm,
the nose, and the upper end of the staff in the right hand have been
broken. Both sides of the figure bear what have been called glyphs
in narrow bands extending from the top to the present base. On
the right side of the figure, four partial carvings may be seen, and
on the left three are preserved, though in very poor condition.
Whether or not these represent glyphs is problematical, but if
they do, they vary greatly from the usual Maya form so commonly
found on pottery both at Tazumal and elsewhere in El Salvador,
Guatemala, and Honduras. Possibly, they represent glyphs of
some other language or dialect, Pokoman Maya, noted by an early
ecclesiastical letter as perhaps spoken in the Chalchuapa Valley

Another well-known stone figure, likewise brought from the
Chalchuapa Zone by Barbarena, is that called the “couchant
jaguar” by the Museo Nacional and considered a chac-mool type
by others* Don Andres Hernandez, a resident of Chalchuapa,
saw this figure together with the stela and the Casa Blanca chac-
mool taken by Barbarena to San Salvador about forty-cight years
ago. According to his information, this monolith was found in
the coffee plantation now known as the Finca El Socorro, in other
words near Mound 6 of the Tazumal Group.

‘This stone measures 4-4 ft, in maximum length, 1.4 ft. in great-
est thickness, and, as well as can be determined, 1.9 ft. in height.
Its condition is fragmentary and the design intended by the sculp-
tor is by no means clear. It docs not, however, seem to represent
either a “couchant jaguar” nor yet a “chac-mool” figure, It is the
writer's opinion, very possibly erroneous, that this monolith was
meant to portray two human or anthropomorphic figures back-to-

back. The remaining fragment of the sculpture presents only the
upper torso and arms of such figures. Lack of the heads and lower
body make impossible exact identification of either the exact posi-
tion (i.e., seated or standing) or the type of figures represented.

Another large sculptured stone from this group is a fragmen-
tary large head, highly conventionalized but possibly derived from
the stylized jaguar heads, This head, now in the Mendoza collec-
tion in Chalchuapa, was found at the east end of Mound 4 several
years ago. It is, in its shattered condition, about 3.5 ft. high and
more than 2 ft. in diameter. ‘The stone used was coarse black scoria,
varying greatly from the type used in the stela and the carving from
near Mound 6. Its style also differs completely, Whether it is con-
nected with the ball court or is to be considered later than Mound
4 is undeterminable at present.

The writer's excayations in Mounds 1 and 2 produced surpris-
ingly few stone objects and nearly all were fragmentary. Many
obsidian chips and flakes, two of the thumb-nail scraper form,
three obsidian arrow-heads (two leaf-shaped and one with triangu-
lar form, notched base), stone rings or “club heads,” and metates
and manos of scoria or granitic stone were common.

Club-heads were of the type extremely common over all of cen-
tral and western El Salvador: circular, plain, or fluted exterior sur-
faces, hour-glass-shaped shaft holes, In size these vary from .3 to .y ft.
in diameter and from .1 to .5 ft. in height. The shaft hole also varies.

Metates were of three types: (1) Large basins 1.5 or more ft.
long, 1 ft. wide, and .6 ft, high at the maximum, but generally
somewhat smaller, The grinding area is deeply cut into the rectan-
gular stone block. (2) Flat, rectangular slabs. These are usually .1-.3
ft. thick and differ individually in dimensions but are less than 1.5
ft. long and 1.0 ft. wide. (3) Footed metates. Only rather small
fragments were found in the debris of Mound 1. Feet are rec-
tangular in cross-section, .2 [t. high. The body or slab portion
is slightly more than .1 ft. thick and rectangular. The stone used
in the footed form is much finer than the scoria used in the basin
and slab types.

Manos or grinding stones are cither rectangular (oval or rec-
tangular in cross-section) or cylindrical (round in cross-section),
Only three complete rectangular forms were found; these ranged
2-6 ft. in length and .15-.4 ft. in breadth. The thickness of all
specimens of this form varied from .1 to .2 ft. The sole complete
cylindrical form measured .4 ft. long, .2 ft. thick. Apparently,
almost any hard stone was used for these, but the majority were of
scoria or granitic rock.

Pestles with flat or slightly rounded grinding surfaces of stone
such as that employed in metates and manos were found through.
out the excavations. Usually, these were plain but in three instances
the upper portion—that grasped by the hand—was crudely sculp-
tured to represent some sort of head, apparently a type of animal.

With the exception of a tiny jadeite disc, probably a bead, no
ornamental stone objects were found. Only one fragment of a
dark green stone celt appeared,

BURIALS

Four burials were found during the excavations at Mound 1.
All were post-construction interments and only in the case of
2-42 had any special effort been made to protect the corpse from
molestation. ‘These were located as follows:

APPENDIX C. EXCAVATIONS IN CENTRAL AND WESTERN EL SALVADOR                          61

Burial 1-42, on the cast side of the temple substructure, was found
to lie .6 ft, above the latest floor in debris from the mound. There
was no preparation of the grave, Bones were almost completely
decayed, so much so that only the parietals, parts of the frontal
and occipital, and the line of the spinal column could be traced.
From this scanty information, it appears that the body was laid on
the right side (contracted ?) in a northeast, southwest axial line,
head northeast and facing northwest. Inverted over the skull was
a brown tripodal bowl with solid slab T-form feet, while a small
orange-brown double-curve bowl was inverted over the thorax.
Both had been crushed by stones and rubbish falling from above.

Burial 2-42, on the platform west of the temple substructure
had been prepared somewhat but contained no grave furniture.
Tuto the latest floor of the platform had been cut a narrow oval
-7 by 3.0 ft. in dimensions and .2 ft. deep. The long dimension
ran east and west, In this the corpse was deposited extended on its
back with head to east. This burial was of a child of about cight
years of age, to judge from the teeth. Over the skeleton was erected
a crude masonry tumulus of boulders and adobe mortar, This
covering, at its base, measured 4.7 [t. east-west, 2.3 ft. north-south.
Its outlines were very irregular and the height was nowhere more
than 2.5 ft. above the floor level. The skull of this burial was rela-

tively well preserved, owing to the fact that it lay above the floor
level and was protected by the covering masonry. The long bones,
ribs, vertebrae, etc., however, were in very poor condition.

Burial 3-42 occurred near the southeast corner of the temple
substructure and was found in debris above the plaza-floor level.
Large, coarse, red scoriaccous rocks lay below this skeleton and
may represent a sort of altar or stone pile to support the corpse.
Aside from this possibility, there is no indication of special prepata-
tion of the burial, nor were any objects interred with the body.
This skeleton was loosely contracted and lay on its right side. The
long axis of the body was northeast and southwest with head north-
cast, face to north. All of the bones were badly disintegrated; how-
ever the skull fragments suggested a young or early middle-aged
person.

Burial 4-42 was found in cleaning the lower terraces on the west
side of the temple substructure. The grave was simply cut into
terraces 3 and 4 (above the platform-floor) and no covering was
provided. No grave furniture was found. The skeleton was in
extremely bad condition, partially due to decay but mainly because
of the debris which had fallen on it. The evidence was that this
corpse had been deposited loosely contracted on its right side, with
head to the cast, facing north, feet to the west.

POTTERY

The pottery of Tazumal Group, Mounds 1 and 2, may be di-
vided stratigraphically, and, to a considerable extent typologically,
into two separate groups:

I. EARLY PHASE. Including 1184 sherds and a number of
partially complete vessels. This material was found in a rubbish
deposit underlying the constructional levels of the platform (fig.
2g, €) and resting on sterile soil. The deposit contained much char-
coal and many obsidian chips besides an unusually heavy concen-
tration of sherds. Below the constructional levels, this test trench
was less than 10 ft. square. The rubbish deposit covered slightly
more than half this area and in no place exceeded 1.5 ft. in thick-
ness,

IL LATER PHASE. Including 29,665 sherds, two complete and
several partially complete vessels. Over go per cent of these sherds
were found in debris while cleaning steps, terraces, and the like.
The remainder are sherds of fill, “pot-holes” (see page 68), and
the two vessels from Burial 1-42. Sherds from fill (Phase IVa, etc.)
were separated but showed no significant change from building
phase to building phase, possibly because of the comparatively
small number involved. On the other hand, from IVa onward
there is a considerable change from the sherds designated Early
Phase. In the debris, many sherds of Early Phase types occurred
but this is almost certainly due to erosion and the mixing due to
excavations of the past fifty years, Early types were also found in
fill, probably owing to the indiscriminate usc of rubbish deposits
by the constructors of the temples. In view of the fact that few
sherds were found in the fill, and that conclusions based on their
analysis are extremely subject to error, the writer has treated these
with the debris sherds as Late Phase. Further excavation in the fill
of the various constructions will be necessary to establish finer
gradations in the pottery sequence than here presented.

™ Annular bases have been found of this ware at Tazumal, but whether they be-

J. EARLY PHASE POTTERY
A. “Seraped-slip” Dichrome

Illustration. Fig. 30, d-f; pl. XIV, 3, 4-

Color, Whiteslipped or washed on buff to orange clay, exterior
and/or interior. An orange wash is sometimes used to cover the
entire vessel after the design has been imparted to the slip.

Surface. Very smooth or polished, often lustrous, Almost always
hard-fired, often smoke-blackened, especially exterior of the base.

Paste. Coarse, not hard-fired, often black in center, usually
brown or orange color.

Form. (1) Deep bowls with shallow-round or flattened bases."
Bodies of vessels are slightly bulging and the thickened rim
flares. Lip is flat or nearly so, broad, and generally inclines inward.
(fig. 30, d-f; pl. XIV-A, 4). (2) Jars: Several partially complete rims
and necks and a number of body sherds indicate a large, globular-
bodied form with vertical or slightly funneled neck, Rim and lip
resemble those of the bowl form. Bases were probably either round
or annular. Neck sherds are always much thicker than those
of the body and these alone are decorated on both interior and
exterior,

Design. Usually some simple wavy horizontal line negative
design. In a few instances the waves are so abrupt as to approximate
zig-zags. This design seems to have been applied by scraping of
part of the white slip, cither with the fingers or with a brush hav-
ing widely separated fibrous elements. As the slip in this type is
generally thick, such scraping must have been done while the paint
was still very wet as many examples show indistinct lines rather
more like smears than the definite scraped marks on type examples
{ic., Later Phase dichrome). In reslipped orange examples, follow-
ing the scraping of the white slip, the paint was allowed to dry
before the secondary coat was applied. This results in an orange
vessel with the scraped design showing clearly through the last slip.

long to howls or not Js unknown.

6                                             ARCHAEOLOGICAL INVESTIGATIONS IN EL SALVADOR

B. “Scraped-Slip” Polychrome

Color, Red and white or red and orange-over-white on orange
to brown clay, interior and sometimes exterior.

Surface, Always wellsmoothed, sometimes polished, rarely lus-
trous, Well-fired.

Paste, Fine reddish clay, generally fine-grained. Sometimes fired
black in center, Usually hard-fired, occasionally very hard.

Form. (1) Almost entirely simple-silhouette bowls with round
bases and sharply rounded lips. Some rims are turned slightly in-
ward. (2) Simple-silhouette small bowl with inset rim, sharply
rounded lip. (This form occurs only with design 7.) (3) Apparently,
some vase form with horizontal flutings. Only body sherds were
found. This form is atypical of “scraped-slip” ware.

Design. (1) Wavy lines scraped in white slip to give negative
brownish design. Red-painted rims (pl. XIV-A, 9). (2) Smeared
white slip with unclear lines. Usually red-painted rims. One sherd
shows this plus fine incised lines on exterior. (3) Unevenly applied
white slip, midway between solid white and true “scraped-slip,”
with red (sometimes hematite red)-painted rims and geometric de-
signs inside and sometimes outside the vessels. The decoration con-
sists of parallel horizontal wavy lines and/or rectangles, circles, etc.
(4) Red-painted rim and U's on exterior. This red design is usually
over an orange-over-white slip, with or without “scraped-slip”
designs. (5) Combination of red-painted rim with white “scraped-
slip" design interior, incised, red-filled geometric or bird designs
exterior (fig. 30, 0). (6) Red (sometimes hematite red)-slipped in-
terior, incised-outline birds exterior with white-filled rectangles
separating the birds, This lacks the “scraped-slip” technique but
retains the bowl form and paint characteristic of the ware. (7) In-
terior: orange wash over smeared white slip; Extcrior: Incised,
white-filled bird designs in orange rectangular areas. White rims.
‘This also is not “scraped slip” technique but according to ware and
paint must be considered closely related (fig. go, &).

C. White on Orange

Color. Thin white on light orange slip.

Surface. Well-smoothed, well-fired but never lustrous.

Paste, Of medium coarseness. Well-fired, reddish in color.

Form. (1) Tetrapodal, small bowls with basal ridges, flaring
rims, round lips. Bottoms of vessels are shallowly round. Feet are
solid, pointed, in form of stylized animal head with punctate eyes
and a ridge forming brows. Basal ridge is abrupt but well rounded
(fig. 30, 1). (2) This may be another version of form 1 but only
sherds of the upper portion of the vessel are known. Whether this
form was footed or not cannot be determined. The basal ridge of
this type is much sharper than in 1 and bears small affixed knobs
at intervals. The portion above the ridge is slightly convex on the
exterior with the result that the rim turns slightly in. The lip is
sharply rounded.

Design. On interior of bowls: oblique, broad, and narrow white
lines in groups of four or five lines. Small, upward-pointing hooks
join outside lines of the oblique clements.

Form 2 possesses design only on the exterior. This consists of
alternating panels of wavy lines separated by vertical parallel lines.

D. Crude Red on Orange

Ilustration. PL XIV-A, 7.

Color. Dark red on orange-brown clay, apparently unslipped.

Surface, Roughly polished, Not hard-fired. Many areas show
firing clouds. Red areas often lustrous.

MLongyear, 1ggna, fig, tt, $.

Paste. Fairly coarse. Central portion burned black, surface parts
orange brown. Not hard fired.

Form. Tripodal, flat-bottom vase with flaring rim, uneven round
lip, Feet are round, cascabel,

Design. Broad red-painted rim and basal bands encircling the
exterior. Between these areas are painted stylized monkeys in
profile,

FE. Red and Black on Cream

Color. Red and black on cream slip.

Surface. Polished, hard fred.

Paste. Fine, hard-fired.

Form. Probably a simple-silhouette bowl. Lip sharply rounded.

Design. The single rim sherd of this type does not indicate much
of the design. In color, technique, and style, however, it greatly
resembles pottery such as that in plate XIV-A, 2, and figured by
Longyear.*

F. Hematite Red and Black on Orange

Color. Hematite red and black on orange slip.

Surface. Polished, not hard-fired, surface and design badly erod-
ed, but only one sherd of this category.

Paste. Very fine, cream-colored, poorly fired. No large inclusions.

Form. Probably a simple-silhouette bowl. Round lip.

Design. Questionable. Apparently stylized Maya glyphs on ex-
terior in red (orange-filled?)-painted horizontal band below lip.

G. Red and Black on Orange

Color. Black and light red on orange slipped exterior; clay color,
interior.

Surface. Polished, poorly fired.

Paste. Fine, orange color, no large inclusions, not hard-fired.

Form. This sherd shows a basal flange and suggests a bowl shape.

Design. Apparently geometrical on the one sherd of this type.

H, Usulutan Technique Polychrome

Color, Dark orange slip with negative design in Usulutan tech-
nique in light orange, interior only, Exterior—orange slip, Over
this a secondary design in dark red was painted interior and ex-
terior.

Surface. Highly polished, hard. Interior of base shows areas
from which paint has scaled.

Paste. Red, medium-fine texture, light grey in center, not hard-
fired.

Form. Tetrapodal or tripodal (?) bowl with shallow-round base,
flat lip. Feet are pointed cascabel face effigies with concave eyes
and punctuate pupils, nose, etc. Basal ridge is low, well rounded.
Three centimeters below the interior rim is a low, shelf-like pro-
jection.

Design. Original Usulutan technique design was confined to the
interior side walls of the vessel. Apparently, two or three panels
were decorated as follows: vertical wavy thin lines in groups of
five (?) were separated by two large, irregular dots and the panels
themselves were bracketed by such dots. Between these decorated
arcas the vessel was unslippd,

The secondary, painted, design consists of: Exterior—pairs of
oblique, sharply waved parallel lines separated by undecorated
arcas in a horizontal band immediately above the basal ridge. Red
lines on the feet of the vessel emphasize features of the effigy faces
thereon portrayed. Interior—design in red covers the Usulutan

I. Miscellaneous Sherds with Usulutan Designs

Color. Dark orange slip; light grey to buff lines of design.

Surface. Very smooth, lightly polished; dull.

Paste. Tine, not hard-fired, reddish.

Form, Two sherds at least indicate bowls or vases (one is from a
footed vessel with wide flange).

Design. Parallel oblique lines. One sherd, apparently from the
tim of a bowl, shows this design both inside and outside. Another
sherd, from the base of a vesscl, bears broad, irregular lines on the
interior only. A third fragment has only one line on the interior,
at the basal angle.

J. Incieed 1

Tlustration. Fig. 30, i, j, 2, q-

Color, Dark brown to black slip with, usually, white- or yellow-
filled incisions.

Surface. Highly polished, generally hard-fired, lustrous.

Paste, Fine, dark red, well-fired.

Form. (1) Vases with three (2) or four (?) fect, shallow-round
bases, and round or sharply rounded lips. The partially complete
vessel excavated at Tazumal shows high, cascabel slab feet with
side slits, a low basal ridge, funneled body with slightly flaring
rim, Other sherds suggest this same form, but smaller, and with
tiny knob feet. These fect arc usually inset from the basal angle
and correlated with a flattened area in the center of the base, In
such cases the basal ridge is sharply defined (fig. go, i, j, n, q). (2)
*Tlaloc vase": Form probably varies according to vessel. Com-
plete form is flat-based, with nearly vertical side-walls, sharply
indented neck, and faring rim,

Design. Exterior only. The partially complete vessel shows two
horizontal bands encircling the vessel with panel elements repre-
senting the feathered serpent and a stylized Tlaloc similar to that
decorating the Temple of Quetzalcoatl at Teotihuacan, together
with other, incomplete incisions. Sherds from other vases follow
these designs closely. A "Tlaloc vase" of this same ware with
modeled face and, on the reverse, incised geometric ornament,
was found in the Corso collection from the Chalchuapa Zone (pl.
XII, 72).

K. Incised 11

Hlustration. Fig. 30, m.

Color. Unslipped clay color—cream to brown—or red-brown to
black.

Surface. Smoothed or lightly polished. Well-fired.

Paste. Generally similar to, but coarser than incised I.

Form. Sherds suggest vases similar to those of incised I, form 1.
Some highly curved body sherds also seem to indicate bowls, or,
more likely, globular-bodicd jars.

Design, Geometric, on exterior only. Usually consist of unevenly
incised hatched triangles in horizontal bands encircling vessel, or
crudely incised imitations of incised I designs.

La Incised UI

Illustration. Fig. 30, p.
Color. Reddish clay, unslipped, or with very thin orange wash.
Surface. Smoothed, unpolished, soft, poorly fired.

Paste. Reddish, usually black in center, poorly fired.

Form. Sherds are too small to indicate complete vessel forms.
The type, however, is well represented in the Corso collection [rom
the Chalchuapa Zone (pl. XIV-A, 1).

Design. Geometric hatched and linear designs on exterior only.

M. Orange Slipped

Color, Creamy to pinkish-orange slip.

Surface. Generally dull, wellsmoothed, never hard-fired.

Paste. Usually very fine.

Form. Three sherds suggest the flaring rims of small jars. An-
other is almost certainly from a small bowl with flaring rim and
sharp shoulder. Two sherds, from the bases of vessels, show very
low round knob feet.

Design. Four sherds show shallow grooves at rim, on lips, or
encircling body exterior, below rim.

N. Red Polished

Illustration. Fig. 30, ee, hh.

Color. All-over dark red slip.

Surface. Roughly polished. Seldom hard.

Paste. Many large inclusions common. Generally hard-fired,
dark brown in color. Occasionally soft, with unevenly fired spots
ranging from cream to black,

Form. (1) Probably a deep bowl or open-mouthed jar with
thickened, flaring rim, flat lip. (2) Jar with thickened, flaring rim,
flat lip.

0. Smooth Common

Illustration, Fig. go, v, ww.

Color. Red-slipped or reddish to grey clay color.

Surface, Well-smoothed, sometimes polished,

Paste. Generally reddish, fine, but with some large inclusions,
well-fired,

Form. (1) Globular-bodied jars with short necks, flaring rims,
rounded or flattened lips, apparently round bases. (2) Wide
mouthed jars or decp bowls. (5) Apparently bowls, represented
only by rim sherds.

P. Rough Common

Color. Dark grey to black or some shade of red.

Surface. Roughly smoothed, striations of smoothing implements
very clearly marked, never polished.

Paste. Similar to, but slightly coarser than, smooth common.

Form. (1) Bowls and jars of smooth common types. (2) Ollas and
jars with two vertical loop handles on upper body curve (fig. 30, 1).
(3) Low bowls or plates (fig. go, bh).

R. Incensarios

Color, Dark grey clay. In some places, traces of white wash, ap-
plied in splotches.

Surface. Rough, striated, poorly fired.

Paste. Very coarse, solt, dark grey to black.

Form. Sherds only of: (1) Punctate-base small bowl. (2) Annular-
base vase, resembling the San Andres vase forms.

S. Dark Fluted

Color. Dark red-brown to black.

Surface, Wellsmoothed to polished. Often unevenly fired with
black firing clouds.

Paste. Fine. Generally black in center, not hard-fired.

Form. Sherds seem to be from bases resembling incised I shape.

Design. Broad, shallow vertical flutings.

T. Miscellancous Pottery Objects

(1) PLaques or Tirar Jar Covers. Reddish-brown colors, pol-
ished surface; shallow, broad grooved designs, always curvilinear;
probably disc shape; brown paste, well-fired (pl, XIV, ro).

(2) Timer Fracmenrary FIGURINES. One is a solid head, red-

(3) Two Fracmenrary Wusties. Rough, red-brown, unslipped,

(4) Smatt. PLares or Distes, on Jar Lins. Red-painted rims.
Brown clay with sand inclusions. Polished surface.

1. LATER PHASE POTTERY
A. “Seraped-slip” Dichrome

Coloy, As in Early Phase ware.

Surface, Wellsmoothed but only two sherds were polished com-
parable to those of Early Phase. Surface usually soft.

Paste. Generally rather coarse (always coarser than Early Phase
ware of this decorative technique). Seldom hard-fired. Light brown
color.

Form. (1) Large, two-handled bowls with flat or round lips and,
genctally, a low ridge on exterior below rim (fig. 30, s, £). (2) Ollas
or jars; four sherds from necks; round lips. (5) Deep jars with open
mouths, frequently grooved lips, thick side walls (fig. go, d-f). (4)
Wide-mouthed bowls or jars with flaring rims, round bases; often
upper third of body inturned to form globular vessel (fig. 30, t, id).

Design, Simple waves, as in Early Phase. Usually on exterior
only of vessels, occasionally on rims.

B, “Seraped-slip” Polychrome

Comparable exactly with bowl forms of Early Phase. Probably
rubbish sherds of basic period used in constructions of later times.

C. White on Orange Dichrome

Appears to be identical with Early Phase ware of this category
in all respects.

D. Red on buff Dichrome

Color, Dark red on buff to orange slip.

Surface. Usually polished, seldom hard, rarely lustrous.

Paste, Fine but some large inclusions, often fired black in center.
Hard-fired.

Form. Apparently simple-sithouctte bowls with sharp-round lips
or vases with nearly vertical sides and sharp-round lips. No basal
sherds found.

Design. Sherds show only very simple, broad line geometric
motives on exterior of vessels, usually rectangles below rim bands.

E. Red on orange Dichrome
Color, Red on dark orange slip.

Surface. Polished, usually soft and poorly fired.

Paste, Fine, usually creamy color, often fired black in center.
Not usually fired very hard.

Form. (1) Vases with nearly vertical side walls, slightly flaring
rims, round lips. (2) Double-curve bowls, shallow-round bases,
marked basal angles, sharp lips (fig. 30, ff).

Design. Always geometric: checker-board, X, steps, triangles,
squares, etc., on exterior of vesscls, generally on lower two-thirds
of bowl, or side walls of vase below rim band, Sometimes, entire
exterior of bowl between rim and basal angle covered with designs
and portion below is left without adornment.

F. Red on white Dichrome

Color, Orange-red to dark red on dead white to grey, depending
on amount of firing.

Surface. Usually well-polished, sometimes lustrous. Hard.

Paste. Generally fine, dark red.

Form. Simple- or compositesilhouette bowls with round bases
and round to sharp lips, usually out-turned slightly (fig. go, ii).

Design. Usually geometric, Always on exterior of bowls, some-
times on interior, Generally in bands around vessel a short dis-
tance below rim, sometimes in vertical band running from rim to
base (pl, XIV-B, 6).

€. Dichrome and Polychrome with Usulatan Technique

Color. (1) Red-painted rims on orange slip, design showing clay
color. (2) Orange slip on clay, white-filled incised lines, (3) Orange
slip, design shows clay color.

Surface, Wellsmoothed to polished, generally hard.

Paste. Generally fine, especially in form 2. Usually hard-fired
but sometimes rather crumbling,

Form. (1) Simple-silhouette bowls with low-round to round lips
correlate with medium to broad line Usulutan designs and (often)
red-painted rims, (2) Double-curve bowls, some with sharp basal
ridges or flanges, flat rims, sharp lips, thicker (compared to form
1) side walls, and rarely appliquéed knobs at the basal angle
correlate in nearly all cases found with fine line, clear designs.
Frequently mammiform feet are found with this type (fig. 30, ge).

Design. Circles, wavy lines, or oblique, nearly vertical, parallel
lines, usually on interior only. A few show horizontal incised
lines on exterior, usually one or two fine lines immediately above
the basal angle,

H. Red and Purple on Orange Polychrome

Color. Red and purple on orange slip or red, purple and dark
orange on light orange slip.

Surface. Usually hard-fired, lightly to highly polished. Sometimes
surface is soft, paint does not resist cleaning brush.

Paste. Generally fine, dark red in color,

Form. Double-curve bowls and vases such as those of Tula Ia
polychrome, but no annular bases of this  type were found at
Tazumal.

Design. Always on exterior of vessels, and on interior as well of
bowl forms. Designs very much like those on ‘Tula la polychrome
(fig. go, a, a’; pl. XIV-B, »). Usually geometric curvilinear,

I. Red and Black on Orange 1

Jilustration. Pl. XIV-B, 2.

Color. Red and black on light to dark orange, depending on
firing.

Surface. Generally lightly, rarely well-polishcd. Seldom hard,

66                                                                    ARCHAEOLOGICAL INVESTIGATIONS IN EL SALVADOR

Paste. Fine, usually light grey color, well-fired.

Form. Simple- or, rarely, composite-silhouctte bowls. Bases are
round, lips shallow-round (nearly flat) to round,

Design, On exterior of bowls. Red rim band with, usually, hori-
zontal band encircling vessel below. This band contains geometric
or what appear crudely stylized glyph elements. Below this are
frequently silhouette animal figures: for example, monkeys, rab-
bits. Rarely a spiral broad red line winds downward from the rim
to the base, and space between the spiral's curves is adorned with
black monkey silhouettes.

J. Red and Black on Orange IT

Ilustration, Fig. 30, 99-85; pl. XIV-B, 22.

Color, Red (sometimes hematite red) and black on orange, or
red, black and orange on light buff to orange slip.

Surface. Generally wellsmoothed, sometimes polished, rarely
lustrous. Usually paint has largely disappeared because of soft sur-
face.                                        :

Form, Simple- or, most commonly, composite-silhouette bowls.
Some sherds suggest small open-mouthed jars, while others indicate
very low bowls or plates. The double-curve bow! sometimes shows
a marked basal ridge, shallow-round or flat base, low-round to
round lip. Rarely, the portion below the basal angle is fluted.

Paste, Generally fine, and usually burned black in center.

Design. Generally Maya glyphs, conventionalized birds, seated
Maya human figures in combination with geometrical hatched
areas, solid circles, vertical, or oblique lines. Always on exterior
and usually on interior of bowls. Sometimes ted and black on
orange polychrome I designs are found on interior of vessels.
Usual type is bowl (double-curve) with band below rim on exterior
bearing highly stylized glyphs, glyph clements {c.g., bars and dots),
or conventionalized birds. Below this, a hatched or otherwise
geometrically ornamented area with large circles on portion below
greatest body diameter of vessel. Interior may have black, geo-
metrically decorated, horizontal band below rim, with stylized
glyphs in wider band below. Interior base is generally filled with
red geometric design.

K. Red, Black and Orange on Orange Maya Polychrome

Tilustration. Pl. XIV, 3.

Color. Red (nearly always hematite red), black and orange on
orange slip,

Surface. Polished to highly polished. Generally rather soft and
easily scratched, occasionally hard.

Paste. Fine, often nearly white color. Well but not usually ex-
tremely hard-fired,

Form, Simple-silhouctte bowls usually. Round bases, round, or
sharp lips correlate with this form. Rarely, double-curve bowls
occur. One sherd shows a flat-based bowl shape with concave side
walls, Another strongly suggests a vase or very deep bowl. In both
latter specimens, the interior is undecorated.

L. Red, Black and Orange on Orange Maya Vases

Color. Red, black and dark orange (or red-orange) on light
orange slip.

Surface. Well-polished, sometimes lustrous.

Paste, Very fine. One sherd burned black in center; others dark
red color.

Form. Vases with flat bases, probably three or four fect. No rim
sherds found.

Y Lothrop, 1926b, pp. 116-45.

Design. Very fragmentary designs were represented on the Lhree
sherds of this type. Those noted appear to be of standing human
figures in ceremonial garb, separated one from the other by geo-
metrical (2) figures. Very fine red-line outlined figures are charac-
teristic, but black outlined figures are also present. Designs are
very intricate, with much detail.

M. Rapid Style, Fine Line Polychrome

Color. Red, black and orange-red on orange slip.

Surface, Well-polished, usually fairly hard and well-preserved,
occasionally soft, powdery.

Paste. Red color, medium-coarse, Generally well- but not hard-
fired, occasionally crumbling.

Form. Sherds suggest bowls with incurved rims, probably flat
bases, and double-curve bowls with four feet, basal ridges, flaring
rims, low-round lips.

Design. Usually geometric ligures in horizontal,  encircling

design include chain, vertical lines, hooks, etc. Rarely, human
figures apparently in imitation of those on type K or type T are
found.

N. Nicoya Polyehrome

Illustration. Fig. 30, jj; pl. XIV-=B, 7.

Color. Red, black and orange (sometimes yellow-orange) on white
slip, occasionally burned grey.

Surface, Very smooth, sometimes highly polished. Usually not
very hard.

Paste. Very fine usually, sometimes appearing without temper
to the eye. Usually fired dark grey in center, Dusty to the touch,
A few are dark red color, Usually not very hard but occasionally
nearly as hard as plumbate.

Form. (1) Animalefligy jars. Sherds of arms and legs of animals
and part of face of one animal found, (2) “Lamp-chimney” jars,
probably with annular bases. (3) Double-curve bowls with animal-

Design. Compare closely with those illustrated by Lothrop,"
and always in range portrayed for Costa Rica and Nicaragua.

O. Plaster Painted

Color. Very little remains on sherds. Pink and light green were
only colors found.

Surface, Smoothed clay below plaster, unpainted and unslipped
except for one very small fragment of polychrome secondarily
painted with pink plaster. Plaster is very fragile and flaky.

Paste. Varied individually with vase. Generally grey color, sel-
dom fine in texture, hard-fired,

Form, Flat-based vases with nearly vertical side walls, round lips.

Design. None sufficiently intact to recognize.

P. Plumbate

Color, Orange through olive to slaty-green.

Surface. Very soft to extremely hard, generally lustrous and
hard. Some more or less vitreous,

Paste. Very fine, hard, usually grey color.

Form. Animaleffigy jars, “lamp-chimneys,” bowls, One sherd
suggests a Mixtec incensario form with plumbate slipping. This
sherd, from the rim and upper body, shows four cut-out holes
encircled by incised lines, The shape is a globular body with flar-
ing rim, round lip. Slipping is an uneven slate-green much eroded.

Design. Apparently geomctric incised designs on some sherds,
others combine incising with moulded features to form animal

Q. Incised I

Exactly like Early Phase incised I, except that incisions show red
filling. Apparently, the one sherd of this type represents a small
Vase.

R. Incised Mtb

Illustration, Fig. 30, x, y.

Color. Generally buff clay, Few red-washed, some with red-
painted areas outlined by incising.

Surface, Smoothed, coarse, sandy to touch, or rough. Soft.

Paste. Medium-coarse but with much tempering material, Poor-
ly fired, generally grey in center.

Form. Commonest is large, simple-silhouette bowl with thick-
ened rim, round or flattened lip, round or shallow-round base. One
sherd suggests a vertical-walled vase. Walls usually much thicker
than other incised types. In form, wall thickness, and design, this

Design. Usually crudely incised simple geometric designs on
exterior only of vessels. Lines gencrally broad in comparison with
other incised types.

S. Incised IV

Hlustration, Fig, go, cc, dd; pl. XIV-B, 4.

Color. Grey or red shades, clay color, unslipped.

Surface, Smoothed, never polished (due to erosion of soft sur-
face ?), poorly fired, Sandy to touch,

Paste, Generally fine, very soft, and poorly fired, light red to
dark grey in color,

Form. Some vessels on high, effigy feet representing stylized

with round (sometimes thickened) lips, shallow-round bases, as
commonest shape. One fragment of an annular base and another

Design, Geometric incised patterns and/or punctate dots. Some-
times red paint in incised areas. Punctate areas usually on base,
interior, of vessels.

T. Incised Y

Hlustration. Fig. go, kk; pl. XIV-B, 9.

Color, Dark brown to black (usually slipped).

Surface. Well-smoothed to highly polished.

Paste. Medium-fine, usually burned black in center. Nearly
always hard-fired.

Form. Small bowls, generally double-curve with marked basal
angles, flaring rims, round to shallow-round bases, and round to

Design. Simple geometrical designs on exterior of bowls in hori-
zontal band encireling vessel 1 cm. below rim, usually covering

U. Incised VI

Tilustration. Fig. go, z, aa.

Color. Light brown clay or white slipped.

Surface. Well-smoothed to polished. White slip sometimes lus-
trous. Generally hard surface.

Paste. Medium-fine, hard-fired, pumice inclusions, generally
some shade of brown,

Form. Bowl form most common—flat base with nearly vertical
side walls, flaring rim, low-round to round lip. Occasionally double-
curve bowl with round base, round lip.

Design, Usually simple geometric designs, most frequently
curvilinear, in horizontal band encircling exterior of vesscl.

V, Miscellaneous Incised

Color. Generally light brown to red. Two seem to show red slip,
two white.

Surface. Smoothed, unpolished.

Paste. Usually fine, dark grey in center, hard-fired,

Form. (1) Jar with flaring neck, round lip. No rim sherds found.
(2) Flat-based vase resembling carved glyph type W. (3) Bowl
form—double-curve, sharp-round lip, flaring rim shape commonest.
No base sherds found.

Design, Usually geometric. ‘The two white-slipped sherds suggest

W. Carved Glyph Vases

Illustration. Fig. 30, bb; pl. XIV-B, 5.

Color. Clay color remains—cream to dark grey, One sherd bears

Surface, Very smooth, medium-hard. One sherd shows light

Paste. Very fine, creamy color, burned black in center.

Form. Apparently high vases, probably with flat bases. Rims

Design. Carved Maya glyphs in horizontal band encircling vessel
2 cm. below rim, exterior. Recessed carving usually,

X. Tripodal Red Bowls

Color, Slipped red to dark brown, depending on firing.

Surface. Polished, generally soft and easily scratched.

Paste, Medium-coarse, scldom hard-fired, generally brownish

Form. Shallow-round based bowls with slightly flaring, straight

Y. White Slipped

Color. Dead white to light grey, depending on firing.

Surface. Very smooth to highly polished. Sometimes crackled,
paint often chipped or flaked, Hard, in most examples,

Paste. Generally fine.

Form. Usually large ollas with flaring, occasionally grooved

ened, flat, or grooved lips or simple, round lips, Thickness of the

Z. Coarse Red on Buff

Color, Light red on thin buf slip.

Surface. Smooth, not polished. Hard.

Paste. Medium-fine, red-brown color, Hard-fired.

Form. Apparently thick-walled jars resembling the largest of

Design. Red dots and splashed in aimless fashion on exterior

AA. Incensarios

No complete vessels were found but many fragments of at least
three types are common:

63                                    ARCHAEOLOGICAL INVESTIGATIONS IN EL SALVADOR

(1) Knobbed, thick-walled, high vases on annular bases, with
oval bodies, flaring or flat rims. Thin white wash or splashes found
either on lower part of body, exterior, or interior. Surface is
smoothed, exterior, sometimes interior, Large vertical loop han-
dies of this ware were found. It is a close relative of the commonest
San Andres type.

(2) Effigy vases. This annular-based type may bear modeled gro-
tesque faces or human figures affixed to one-half of the exterior
body (pl. XIV-B, 70). The rim is commonly broad and flat, the
body ovate. Surface is well smoothed, and features of the face are
generally painted light red and blue. Firing is never hard.

(3) Mixtec type. Tripodal jars with cut-out sides and punctured
bases, usually red-slipped. This is the least common of the three
recognizable types. Legs are high, hollow knobs with onc usually
much longer than the other two.

BB. Miscellaneous Plain Wares

Color, Few orange or red-washed, but generally dark grey to
brown or black, unslipped.

Surface. Usually badly croded so that original surface cannot be
easily detected. A few sherds of smooth common or rough com-
mon of the Early Phase wares are present, but in such small num-
hers that they seem to represent material fallen from fill, The
general characteristic of all of this Later Phase class, the most
numerous of all, is soft surface, almost never polished, whereas
the Early Phase domestic wares were usually much harder and
better finished.

Paste. Thick-walled examples, such as shapes 1-3, generally pos-
sess dark grey or brown paste. Footed bowls are for the most part
fine, light to dark grey in color. In only rare examples are the
sherds hard-fired.

Form, (1) Globular-badicd ollas with funneled or kegshaped
necks, usually flattened rims. Usually two vertical loop handles.
(2) Large, simple-silhouette bowls with round bases, thick side
walls, round lips, occasionally ridge on exterior below rim. Lip
may be grooved, flat, or round, almost never sharp. (3) Large, open-
mouthed jars with round, flattened, or deeply recurved bases,
round lips, frequently thickened rim. A few sherds, apparently
{rom this form, indicate vertical loop handles, (4) Footed bowls.
No complete vessels have been found but several fragments indi-
cate tripods. The bowls generally possess flat or shallow-round
bases, flaring rims, round lips. Feet are solid, bear incised, stylized,
animal-head features, or Atlantean human figures.

Design. Usually plain, A few, apparently from the necks of ollas,
bear appliquéed, modeled human, or monkey faces, sometimes
with a little white paint retained in eye and mouth crevices, In-
cised horizontal lines rarely appear below bowl rims or above basal
angles,

CC. Miscellancous Pottery Objects

(1) Servpte Wuorts. Thirteen were found in the debris of
Mound 1. All were poorly fired, made of pumice and/or sand-
tempered clay, usually fine in texture. Surfaces were smoothed,
unpolished, unslipped, soft, Designs include threc-, four-, and five-
divisional geometric incisings (fig. go, mm, nn) impressions (fig.
go, 00) and whirl incisings (fig. 30, pp). Plain, bead-like forms,
differing from the commoner, vertically compressed decorated discs
are also found.

(2) Ficvrines, Eighteen fragmentary figurines were found in the
debris of Mounds 1 and 2. Certain other fragments may have been

* Lothrap, 1927b.

from figurines but appear more likely to be from figures affixed
to incensarios or vessels. Two heads greatly resemble one of the
Early Phase examples. Some of the others may be of the same
general class but are very much croded, making identification
difficult. No head accords very closely to “archaic” types figured
by Lothrop,’* but several possess certain elements of these. All are
made of sandy clay, white to light brown in color, with occasional-
ly fairly large inclusions of some crushed brown stone. All types
figured are common throughout the Chalchuapa Zone. Firing is
usually poor, white clay examples fecling powdery to the touch
(pl. XIV-B, 77).

(3) JAr Covers. Five complete discs of reused sherds were found
as well as a number of fragments. Diameters ranged from 8 to 15,
cm, In all cases, whatever painted design may have originally
existed on the sherds has disappeared.

(4) PLaques or Jar Covers. Five fragments of grooved sherds
of this type were found. All accord exactly to those noted for the
Early Phase.

(5) Dovs.e Rinc. In the Chalchuapa Zone, a number of these
curious clay objects have been found. They consist of two small
clay rings joined together at one point on their outer circumference
so that one ring is horizontal, the other vertical. Of those from this
zone, including one from the debris of Mound 1, Tazumal Group,
all are of light buff clay, fine-grained, moderately fired, unpainted,
and unslipped. Their purpose, so far as the writer knows, has never
been ascertained.

(6) Pires. Two tubes with longitudinal shaft-holes were found
in the debris of Mound 1. These were broken at one end, the
larger, but the other was smooth. In both examples, the shaft-hole
was extremely small—about .2 mm. in diameter, The larger meas-
ured 8 cm, in length. Both were made of a sandy, brown clay, un-
painted and unslipped. Whether they were for smoking, as is
probable, is not demonstrable because of the incompleteness of
the specimens.

(7) SMALL Cray Petters, Eleven of these were discovered in
debris, All were of a dark brown, rather coarse clay and measured
slightly more than 1 cm, in diameter. While it is possible that they
were used in cascabel feet of pottery bowls, their resemblance
to pellets from the cascabel handles of “coalscuttle” incensarios
such as those of Campana-San Andres suggests the use of that type
of vessel in Tazumal Group—though no cxamples of the form were
found,

(8) Seat Impression. One impression of a cylinder seal was
found in debris. Design is not clear but the impressions are deep
and generally curvilinear. Possibly a grotesque face is intended,

(g) CANDELERO. A rectangular clay object, 2.5 cm. high, 3.3 cm.
wide, and at least 6 cm. long (onc end has been broken) was found
in debris. Two wells, each 1.8 cm. in diameter and 2 cm. deep
remained, and it is possible that in the complete specimen other
such holes existed. This object is made of fine clay with crushed
stone and pumice temper, light brown in color, with some smoke-
blackened spots. It resembles some candeloros from Honduras and
Tcotihuacan'” but is interesting for the fact that such specimens
are almost unknown in El Salvador.

DD. Sherds from Pot-holes

While cleaning floors of the final building phases on the north
side of the platform of Mound 1, five holes, circular or oval in
outline and never penetrating into the adobe fill under the floors
more than 1 [t., were found. Two contained potsherds intentionally
placed and easily distinguished from the overlying debris material.

These openings were termed pot-holes for want of a better name

Pothole A (fig. 28) contained many sherds of the heaviest

represented was a very large, deep, open-mouthed jar with thick-

No other pothole contained more than debris sherds. In all,
excavations were carried down at least 3 ft. from the upper floor
