DISCUSSION

The superficial character of the explorations at Tazumal Group
must restrain any inclination to draw far-reaching conclusions
regarding the archaeology of western El Salvador. The investiga-
tions here presented, when considered jointly with those now being
conducted by the Museo Nacional of El Salvador,” however, throw
some light on the chronological position and suggest certain
cultural connections.

Let us consider first the chronological implications of Tazumal
material. Most important at present as aids to dating are, prima-
rily, the ceramic finds, and, secondarily, the architectural. The
Early Phase pottery deposit is, stratigraphically, the carliest at
Tazumal, and its contents are overwhelmingly of types clearly
distinguishable from the Later Phase wares. Relying as we must
entirely upon pottery remains—since this carliest deposit contained
no stone work and cannot be linked clearly with any building
construction—connections cannot be established entirely on the
same bases as those of the Later Phase.

EarLy Phase Potrery

Composition of a deposit, as well as its stratigraphic position, is
of fundamental importance in dating the material such a deposit
contains, In the case of the Farly Phase stratum at Tazumal, com-
position was entirely different from that of the fill of structures
rising above at this point. The great concentration of sherds in a
small area and the heavy content of charcoal alone militate against
this being building fill. Furthermore, several pottery vessels could
be reconstructed with sherds from one-third or half-complete re-
mains—at this site a condition seldom encountered in debris and
never in fill. Except for very occasional flecks, obviously results of
an ancient, random collection of rubbish to make adobe, charcoal
does not occur here save for that found in fire pits discovered in
situ. Therefore, we may say with reasonable security that the Early
Phase pottery deposit, at this point in the ruin, is earliest in posi-
tion and of a character foreign to that of Later Phase constructional
fill.

‘Turning to the pottery itself, we find that the following identifi-
cations can be established, tentatively at least:

(1) Ficurines: One of the two Early Phase fragments compares
with Miraflores Period Kaminaljuyu* and “archaic El Salva-
doran examples. The other figurine is yet untyped.

* Under the writers technical supervision, an expedition of the Museo Nacional of
El Salvador continued work on the north side of Mound 1 from December, 1943,
through January and February, 1943. Most of the platform north of the central
structure was cleared of debris and in the process, three small buildings, termed
Structures 1a, 1b, and 1c, came to light. Tomb 1 was discovered under Structure
te and excavated completely. Furthermore, a small amount of repair was undertaken
and explorations conducted at the Las Victorias Group. Since no definitive report
is possible at this time, outstanding discoveries are Incorporated in the present
paper and, for illustrations of several of the pottery vessels found, reference may
be made to Boggs, 1943b, fig. 5 and frontispiece.

“Information concerning the Kaminaljoyo dating and material culture was
obtained during a brief stay in Guatemala City, where Mr. Edwin Shook of the

(2) Potrrry:

(a) “Scraped-slip” ware, of Early Phase style and technique, ap-
pears in the Esperanza Period at Kaminaljuyu and in a mound “on
the northern border of Los Naranjos,” on the north shore of Lake
Yojoa, Honduras.

(b) Incised I, and possibly incised IT, wares of Early Phase Tazu-
mal are nearly, if not entirely, identical with types assigned to the
Fsperanza Period at Kaminaljuyu and believed connected with
early Teotibuacan incised tripodal vases.

(c) Heavy dichrome sherds, decorated with Usulutan technique,
are found as carly as Miraflores Period Kaminaljuyu, and in the
“archaic” stratum of the Cerro Zapote, near San Salvador. How-
ever, until more stratigraphic and technical proof has been amassed
regarding the chronological position of Usulutan ware, it serves as
a weak crutch to dating,

(d) Polychrome ware with Usulutan technique of the Farly
Phase is practically duplicated by one footed vessel from ‘Tomb 5,
Mound B, Kaminaljuyu, dated Esperanza Period, and by a similar
bowl from Burial 12, Copan, dated pre-Acropolis Phase.

(c) Early Phase white on orange compares in form and design
with certain small footed bowls of the Esperanza Period at Kami-
naljuyu.

(f) The one small, eroded sherd of bright red and black on or-
ange, Early Phase Tazumal, appears to be from a basal-flanged
bowl. Its form and its color combination are both extremely sug-
gestive of Tzakol Period Uaxactun Peten polychrome sherds,* but
the design is unfortunately so incomplete that exact identification
is out of the question.

(g) The red and black on cream sherd of the Early Phase is
small but in good condition. Its size prohibits positive identifica-
tion, but it resembles in style of painting and color combination,
as well as by the form it suggests, polychrome pottery dated by
Longyear to sub-Phase A of the Acropolis Phase at Copan.** The
type, in case the writer is not mistaken in this identification, is
common in the Later Phase at Tazumal as well as in many other
sites of western El Salvador and Honduras but in all other in-
stances known is associated with a pottery complex very different
from that discussed here,

(h) The hematite red and black on orange sherd of the Early
Phase is very badly eroded and almost nothing can be made of the
design. Ordinarily, such a sherd would not be used in dating, but
because of the scarcity of polychrome in this deposit, we consider

than upon published references. Appearance in print of the Kaminaljuyu report
may necessitate revision of time and cultural suggestions here set farth regarding

qo                                      ARCHAEOLOGICAL INVESTIGATIONS IN EL SALVADOR

it here simply to complete the record. Its color combination and
suggested form lead one to believe that it can hardly have been
earlier than the Acropolis Phase at Copan and is perhaps as late as
sub-Phase 5."

Of the remaining Early Phase sherds, none can yet be used in
making comparisons, mainly because the majority represent plain
wares. The smooth common and rough common sherds, however,
bearing general form resemblances to the early “‘scraped-slip” ware,
were almost certainly made at or near Tazumal and undoubtedly
should be considered as products of this civilization.

To summarize the facts known about the Early Phase, then, we
find that the deposit stratigraphically carliest possesses a distinctive
composition suggestive, if not of a rubbish heap in situ, at least of
pre-plalform construction. Its ceramic content overwhelmingly
proposes a dating comparable to that of the Esperanza Period of
Kaminaljuyu and the Tzakol Period of Uaxactun, but there are
two equivocal objections; namely, the two polychrome sherds
which seem to refer to the Copan Acropolis Phase. There are but
three possible explanations for the occurrence of these sherds in a
deposit which, from other evidence, should date to a much earlier
period: (a) The two sherds may represent imports, were found de-
posited naturally, and the entire body of ceramic material is no
earlier than Acropolis Phase Copan. (b) These two sherds have
been assigned incorrectly to the Copan Acropolis Phase. (c) During
excavation, through accidental brushing of the side walls of the
exploratory pit, or inexcusable carelessness on the part of cither,
or both, workmen and the writer, the two sherds dropped into the
deposit from some constructional level above and should not be
considered relevant to this Early Phase at all. The final is the most
likely explanation, but for the moment we are able to say only
the probable, rather than the certain, dating of the Early Phase
is equivalent to that of the Esperanza Period at Kaminaljuyu, at
least until more thorough and carcful testing of this stratum is
possible.

Laren Phase Porrrry

The Later Phase at Tazumal permits not only broader ceramic
comparisons but also some correlation with periods of building
activity. Sherds and some complete vessels of this period were re-
covered from fill, from one tomb, from a post-constructional burial,
and from debris, Relatively few Early Phase wares are found in
either building fill or debris and none can be assigned to post-
construction burials.

OF most value for purposes of comparison are the objects found
in ‘Tomb 1, which included twenty intact pottery containers, two
pyrite mirrors, a jadeite mosaic mask, and three necklaces. OF the
nine polychrome vessels found, five at least are of well-known
Maya types. Onc of these may be referred in time to Tepeu 1 Phase
at Uaxactun, one to Tepeu 2 Phase, two others to Acropolis Phase,
sub-Phase B, at Copan, and one to the Bold Geometric Ulua poly-
chrome era in Honduras, The final four bowls are of types yet
unidentified but are interesting because the interiors of two show
a “scraped-slip" imitation of a design usually portrayed on this
variety of polychrome by the Usulutan process, and two are second-
arily plaster-painted. Another pottery object from this tomb, a low
bowl incensario with a long, horizontal, effigy-head handle, is use-
ful for comparison with incensarios from the Campana-San Andres
Group and central El Salvadoran sites generally.

Tomb 1 appears to have been formed during the construction of

This sherd most closely resembles the Copan types shown by Longyear, 19302,
fig. 14, 6, and Later Phase Tazumal sherds shown in pl. XIV-B, 3, 12.

™ Strong, Kidder, and Paul, 1038, pl. 7, e.

a small temple on the north side of the platform. The building
itself must date to the middle periods of over-all construction of
the Mound 1 complex and the tomb is cut into an carlier structure.
The tomb contents cannot relate to the two carliest platform con-
structions.

Five polychrome bowls other than those from the tomb were
uncovered in 1943 on the platform and surely must be referred to
the latest constructional phases, for one of these was found below a
building, although two appear to have been covered by the latest
—now almost completely destroyed—floor. All of these bowls bear
what appear to be Maya derivatives in combinations of colors,
crude stylizations of glyphs, and animal motives.

Fragments of large jars, some with late “scraped-slip” designs,
occurred in several wide, shallow holes cut into late platlorm-floors,
These must have been deposited by builders of the final construc-
tions, or perhaps are even as late as post-construction times. From
debris, as well as from fill, many late "scraped-slip” sherds were
taken. Unquestionably, these are results of a developmental process
in—or better, deterioration from—a specialized decorative tech-
nique which appears to have begun here in the Early Phase. To
date, the only comparisons with Tazumal late "scraped-slip” have
been discovered at Hacienda Tula and Campana-San Andres."

The great majority of debris and fill sherds are cither so badly
worn that exact classification is impossible, or cannot be identified
with wares already defined at other sites. A number of badly
croded sherds from this Later Phase material appear to refer to
Bold Geometric Yojoa polychrome, though the Tazumal examples
often combine fine lines with rather broad ones, Large effigy in-
censarios, sometimes with Tlaloc faces, sometimes with human
features (portraits ?), date to the end of occupation. Plumbate,
Nicoya polychrome, and fragments apparently from the so-called
“Mixtec incensarios,” are late constructional, and the types may
have persisted after the finish of construction, Sherds of the brown
ware which included the tripodal bowl from Burial 1-42 were
found also in fill and may represent late developments of Teoti-
huacanoid slab-footed tripods. Twistand-bud designs on Tazumal
rapid style, fine line polychrome must bear some relationship to
like clements of Pusilha?* and Acropolis Phase Copan” vesscls,
This element, however, is so commonplace on El Salvadoran vessels
that it is dangerous to assign it exclusively to the one class on which
it appears at ‘lazumal. Designs of the ‘Vazumal red and black on
orange IT scem reflected in “ware with specular hematite in red
slip or paint,” assigned by Thompson to San Jose TT and Tran-
sition IEL-IV at San Jose, British Honduras." The ware is rare in
the north, according to ‘Thompson, and is believed by him to have
come from the Guatemala-Salvador-Copan region. This agrees
with the common occurrence in both Copan and Tazumal of
this ware.

ARCHITECTURE

Since it is true that several wares dateable to Acropolis Phase
Copan correlate with certain building periods at Tazumal, it may
be well to seek architectural comparisons, thus increasing our store
of dateable traits referring to the Tazumal civilization, Between
Copan and Tazumal, there are as yet no obvious likenesses in cither
building materials used nor in methods of their use. That Tazumal
structures are not simply examples of a locally developed architec-
ture is proved by the fact that many similarities exist between
buildings of Tazumal, Campana-San Andres,” and Esperanza Pe-
riod Kaminaljuyu. The lack of complete reports and plans illus-

* Joyce, 1939, pls, XLI, XLUL,

* Thompson, 1989. p. 117, fig. Gr, b.

APPENDIX C. EXCAVATIONS IN CENTRAL AND WESTERN EL SALVADOR                            7

trating both Kaminaljuyu and Campana-San Andres renders verbal
comparison extremely difficult, but we may say that, while all ele-
ments characteristic of one site are not found in each of the others,
more than enough are common to all three to convince the visitor
to these ruins that all have sprung from the same architectural tra-
dition. Core composition is, of course, locally conditioned, but the
special kind and texture of mortar used to face floors and walls is
not only common to these sites but is completely different from
that used in such ruins as Copan and Quirigua. Steeply battered,
high basal terraces with low stcp foundations are common to both
Kaminaljuyu and Tazumal, and the idea seems to have been re-
tained at Campana-San Andres. Finally, balustraded stairways,
analogous in construction and style, have been found at all three
ruins, In the description of Tazumal architecture, a diflerence has
been pointed out between the probably earliest and the certainly
latest constructions. The finer finish, and in some instances white
painting, of walls in the early constructions compares most closely
to white-plastered, well-finished surfaces of certain Kaminaljuyu
substructures, while the coarser, less carefully built, late Tazumal
walls resemble rather the CampanaSan Andres constructions.
Further excavation at Tazumal may make it possible for us eventu-
ally to correlate the Early Phase pottery with an early building
period.

In summary, let us list our chief aids to dating Mounds 1 and 2
of Tazumal:

(1) Size of mounds, the area covered by them, and especially the
profusion of architectural phase strongly suggest that many years
of continuous occupation elapsed between the founding of the
earlicst settlement and the arrival of the Conquistadores.

(2) Early Phase sherds prove existence in this zone, if not at this
site, ol cultures more or Jess contemporary with those of Esperanza
Period Kaminaljuyu and Tzakol Period Uaxactum. Thus dating
may be as early as 9.1.0.0.0 to 9.8,0.0.0 (c. 500-600 A.p.)* for the
earliest Tazumal occupation, though this may be pre-construc-
tional at Mound 1.

(3) Chief periods of building construction, on the basis of Tomb
1 vessels and fill sherds, would appear roughly equivalent in time
to the Copan Acropolis Phase and the Uaxactun Tepeu Period,
9.12.0,0.0 t0 10,3.0.0.0 (€. 00-900 A.D.).

(4) Late construction is perhaps no earlier than 10.10.0,0,0 to
10,19.0,0,0 (€, 1000-1200 A.D.), to judge from the presence of plum-
bate and Nicoya polychrome fragments.

(5) Post-constructional, pre-Conquest evidences may involve sev-
eral sub-phases of occupation, or as appears more likely, occasional
use of the mounds for burial purposes.

SuMMARY

The question of ethnic identifications and relationships of mate-
rial objects found during excavation is never easy Lo answer, seldom
certain, and often misleading with regard to determination of what
race or Janguage-group built the structures, made the pottery, and
carved the stone objects. Exact dating of the finds in this connec-
tion is of the greatest importance. At Tazumal, the evidence is to
some extent contradictory, and the dating is by no means as firm
as might be wished.

At present, it is safe to say only that the Early Phase finds sug-
gest northern penetration, possibly by some Valley of Mexico cul-
ture related to or actually Toltecan. The suggestion that such añ
incursion took place during, or slightly after, the Esperanza-Tzakol
Phase may be capable of demonstration. Whether the elements of

© Maya dates given in earmection with Tazumal chronology refer to the 11.16.0.0.0
correlation of Maya-Christian calendars,

* Lothrop, 1939. P. 47.

such a culture were borne to the Chalchuapa Zone by relatives of
the builders of Kaminaljuyu cannot yet be proved, though the
chances are fair that this was the case,

The pottery, as well as the development of the building complex
of what has here been called, temporarily, the Later Phase, not
only bears out the hints of northern immigration at an carly date
~and, via the architectural style and construction, from the Kami-
naljuyu region—but demonstrates some local development of pot-
tery wares plus reception of several Maya styles recognized at
Copan as dating to the Acropolis Phase, influences from western
Honduras generally, and some late imports of Nicoya polychrome
and plumbate. The implications from this are that a culture estab-
lished here by one people, differing in architectural, and to some
extent ceramic, tradition from the classic Old Empire Maya, be-
came over a period of time, greatly influenced by the Maya, ce-
ramically at least. Latest constructional phases suggest trade with
both northern and southern peoples as well as with the Maya.
Since we know very little concerning the material culture of either
the Pipil or Pokoman Maya in El Salvador, it is probably futile to
attempt final identification of Tazumal artifacts as pertaining to
cither, though the writer believes plumbate and the Tlaloc-face
incensarios are to be considered products of the former.

Large stone sculptures from this group, all of which have been
removed to the Musco Nacional in San Salvador, pose a number
of problems. The stela has usually been considered Maya, in in-
spiration at least,** though the carvings on the sides bear little, if
any, resemblance to Maya glyphs of well-known Old Empire mono-
liths. This carving is relatively crude and presents a great contrast
to that of Copan, Quirigua, and like sites. It may be—if Maya—
the work of a people speaking a dialect different from that of
Chorti, Setting aside for the moment fancied Maya similarities, the
fact that the "glyphs" of the Tazumal stela differ from known
Maya types leaves open two alternative possibilities: that these are
glyphs of some other language, one completely different from Maya,
or that they are not glyphs at all. Thompson™ lists and discusses a
considerable body of evidence pointing to the existence of a sys-
tem of glyph writing distinct from that of the Maya, a system
found along the Pacific Coast of Guatemala and probably Pipil in
origin. There is ample documentary evidence that the Pipil in-
habited a large part of western El Salvador, and one village of this
people, Izalco, mentioned by Torquemada,* is still known to con-
tain Nahuatl speakers. ‘The Ivalco area is not quite go km. south
of Chalchuapa in a straight line. Furthermore, carved pottery,
distinctly un-Maya in appearance and execution, and bearing what
are apparently bar-and-dot numerical designs which may refer to
a non-Maya system of writing,** occurs in several sites of central
and western El Salvador. The chac-mool from the Casa Blanca
Group, the fragmentary large stone head from the east end of
Mound 4, Tazumal Group, and a number of oval jaguar-efligy
heads from Casa Blanca Group and El Refugio testify to non-
Maya, possibly Pipil, influence in the Chalchuapa area. In the col-
lection of Father Corso of Chalchuapa, there is a carved stone hu-
man figure standing on a column or vertical tenon which does not
compare with Maya sculpture known to the writer. It was found
near Tazumal and may also point to Mexican influence. Unfor-
tunately, no large stone figure can be assigned to any specific build-
ing period at Tazumal, since all were discovered on the surface and
thus no ceramic correlations are possible,

Recalling that on the basis of ceramic and architectural com-
parisons there is no more reason to believe that Tazumal was Maya
than that it was built by some other cultural group, we may be

* Thompson, 1941.

> Torquemada, 1723, t. $, ch. 40      "Thompson, 21941. pp. 17, E
* The F. O, Salazár and O. de Sola collections contain fine examples of this type.

## Page 74

_Extraction method: OCR._

qa                                   ARCHAEOLOGICAL INVESTIGATIONS IN EL SALVADOR

permitted to speculate briefly as to whether the stela is not actually
the fabrication of some northern people who inhabited the region
at an early time and subsequently mixed ethnically or traded ex-
tensively with Mayan neighbors. To date, the writer knows of no
stela or large figure strictly comparable to that of Tazumal. Mr.
Edwin Shook has informed me, however, that the headdress prob-
ably represents a Tlaloc face and closely resembles comparable
features of some carvings from the Pasaco area of Guatemala. Aside
from these similarities, however, the figure appears locally inspired.
Tnasmuch as all other large sculptures from the Chalchuapa Zone
seem allied rather to Mexican than to Mayan styles, the stela also
may have been produced by a northern people. For the present,
we must consider this a possibility.

On the other hand, there is reason to believe that Pokoman
Maya inhabited the Chalchuapa Zone, if not Tazumal Group it-
self. Almost all students of Central American linguistics have con-
sidered Ghalchuapa as one of several Pokoman-speaking communi-
ties in sixteenth century El Salvador.” Large amounts of Maya
polychrome pottery have been unearthed not alone in Tazumal
but all over the Zone, as the Corso, Mendoza, Mathics, and other
collections amply demonstrate. This pottery, however, connects
especially with Copan and the area of Lake Yojoa, Honduras,
areas of Chorti Maya, and the majority of it was almost certainly
imported.

Longyear, in writing of the Copan Acropolis Phase, notes: “By
far the greater bulk of Copan polychrome pottery, however, is
found in the form of simple bowls with embellishment of stereo-
typed non-functional glyph bands, a type of ceramic native to El
Salvador. At the same time, the latter country evolved the poly-
chrome vase with thickened rim and low tripod slab support,
adopted by the Ulua Valley-Lake Yojoa region, but only rarely
present at Copan." Later, the same author writes that “The
Ulua-Yojoa drainage contains no Copan types and appears to have

* Johnson, 1940; Lothrop, 1939. p. 44 Lardé, 1926b, p. 172; Documentos Histori-
cos, 1921, p. 292.

derived most of its own from el Salvador, thus ruling it out of
consideration as the birth-place of the Copan style and forcing us
to turn to el Salvador, where polychrome vessels in the decorative
techniques of both the Ulua-Yojoa region and Copan are found.”*
While it is perfectly possible that this polychrome pottery devel-
oped in El Salvador, evidence from Tazumal Group cannot be
said to support the belief. The Maya glyph polychrome style was
already developed when it appeared at Tazumal.

Faced with a large body of material which is cither definitely
not Maya or apparently connected with the Chorti rather than
Pokoman dialectic areas, it is difficult to account for the presence
historically of a Pokoman enclave in the Chalchuapa Zone. It is,
of course, possible that the Pokoman had nothing whatsoever to do
with the archaeological remains from this zone here discussed and
were comparatively late-comers. In that event, it is difficult to
understand how they could have established themselves so se-
curely that they successfully resisted the Pipil invasion and yet
left nothing distinctly their own save language. One would sup-
pose that in a Pokoman area, Pokoman material would be pro-
fuse. Yet in the Chalchuapa Zone, objects recognizably Maya scem
most closely related with Chorti sites. Is it possible that the fine
polychrome styles which Longyear believes originated in neither
Copan nor the Ulua-Yojoa areas, and which were certainly not
developed at Tazumal, actually were products of some Maya
group other than that of Chorti speech, perhaps Pokoman; that
this group, from some region other than Chalchuapa, moved into
the zone, gradually took over from or mixed with the original,
putatively non-Mayan inhabitants about the time of the Acropolis
Phase of Copan or later and by the time of the Conquest had so
imposed its culture and language that all inhabitants were con-
sidered Pokoman by the Spanish? This we are in no position to
answer at present, but the question suggests interesting possibili-
ties for future research.

APPENDIX D. SITE LIST FOR EL SALVADOR

Three lists of sites in the Republic of El Salvador have been
published,* but all are written in Spanish and appear in El Salva-
doran publications not readily available to the average student.
Tt was thought advisable, consequently, to translate and combine
these lists in the following appendix, eliminating duplicates and
adding such new sites as came to the attention of Mr. Boggs and
the writer during their investigations in the field.

The writer, in translating the site lists, made no attempt at a
literal rendition, but all facts given by the original authors arc
included. In each instance, the initials in parentheses following
the site description identify the source,? and in cases of duplicates,
the earliest source is credited. In places where site descriptions by
carlier authors could be amplified by Boggs or the writer, the
appended material, duly credited, follows the original description.

The majority of sites in this list have never actually been visited
by a competent investigator and are known only through the de-
scriptions of natives “interested” in antiquities. Pending further
reconnaissance, therefore, they must be considered merely as pos-
sibilities, for it is the writer’s painful experience that glowing de-
scriptions by natives are often composed much more of fancy than
fact. In previously published site lists, only Lothrop’s indicated
which sites had been visited by him, and which had not. We have
included this information in the pages which follow and have also
indicated those localities seen by Boggs and the writer, all sites
thus confirmed being marked with bold face type.

The sites are grouped alphabetically, by Departments of the Re-
public. Many of them are discussed in greater detail in Part II of
this Memoir and suitable references have been made wherever
these sites occur. On the map which accompanies this Appendix,
an attempt has been made to indicate, not only the location, but
also the type of most of the sites.

Pedro, Sitio del Niño, and Zapotitan. Much pottery and stone.

Santa Tecla. Capital of Department. Pottery and stone found

Tula, Hacienda of Señor don Bernado Alfonso, in jurisdiction

ULapa, Ruins between Teotepeque and Mizata. (J. L.)

DEPARTMENT OF LA PAZ

La Acuja. Island in Estero Jaltepeque. Much unslipped pottery
of domestic type on surface. (A. S,)

Cmnameca. In jurisdiction of San Francisco Chinameca is a
tock with Indian petroglyphs. (J. L.) The rock is 3 km. south of

San Pepro Masanuar. Sites reported south of here near El

DEPARTMENT OF LA UNION

CarrizaL (EL). Town now called Nueva Esparta. Here are found
artifacts of obsidian, grecnstone, and pottery. (J. L.)

Isla Conchagua. Golfo de Fonseca. On the island so-called are
two ruins, Conchagua and Teca. The ruins of Conchagua are now
no more than terraces and remains of houses situated on a hog back
some 500 ft. above sea level. Still standing are the walls of the
church where Alonso Ponce preached on June 22, 1586. (S. K. L.)
See also Conchagua Vieja and Teca in Part 11 of this Memoir.

Ista Goncnacurta. Current local name for Isla Conchagua.

Teea, Golfo de Fonseca. Ruins on the Isla Conchagua.

Yucuayquin. The town rests on pre-Columbian remains,

DEPARTMENT OF MORAZÁN

ARAMBALA. Town in the northern part of the Department. Here
there is a place called Pueblo Viejo, (J. L.)

Gasrras BLancas, Place situated north of Coroban, a hill north-
nottheast of Gotera, Here is found a sheltered rock bearing artistic
Indian paintings. They are popularly supposed to represent the
sun, moon, and stars, but this is not so. (J. L.)

Crexo Peron. Hill cast of Ciudad Barrios (Department of San

Corrapia (La). Place 2 km, northwest of Jocoro, where there

Corinto. Near here is a cave with pre-Columbian Indian paint-
ings. (J. L.)

Coroban. Hill situated northeast of Gotera. On its summit are
remains of rectangular Indian buildings. On the northern part of
the hill is a rock with Indian paintings, and on all parts of the hill
are found remains of Indian artifacts, especially knives and points
of obsidian, It is said that here was the old town of Gotera. (J. L.)
See also Cerro Coroban in Part II of this Memoir.

ERENTIQUE. See Cerro Pelon. (J. L.)

Espiritu Santo. Cave near Corinto. It contains interesting

Frerros (Los). A well west of the town of Guatajiagua. Here is
a rock with petroglyphs. (J. L.)

Huenuechos (Los). Hill near Cacaopera. Here is a rock with
a carved design of a snake and two lions. (J. L.)

Ipoto (Et). Hill near San Isidro. It is thus called because there
was found here a large idol of stone. (J. L.)                        :

Las Labranzas. Rock with petroglyphs near Cacaopera. (S. K. L.)

Macuey (EL). Group of houses 1 km. south of Guatajiagua. Here
are found the ruins of Potorostique. (J. L.)

Montocu, Place in the jurisdiction of Chilanga, where are found
remains of an ancient scttlement, whose inhabitants, according to
tradition, moved to Sensembra. (J. L.)

Ocorrregur. Hill in the jurisdiction of Sociedad. On it are re-
mains of an ancient town. According to the tradition of the inhabi-
tants of Jocoro, their ancestors came rom this place. (J. L.) See also
Cerro Ocotepeque in Part LL of this Memoir.

PoTokrosTIQUE. See Maguey (El). (J. L.)

PuesLo Viejo. The settlement of Yoloaiquin. (J. L.)

Puesto Viejo. Place near Osicala. (J. L.)

Pursio Viejo, See Arambala. (J. L.)

SABANETAS (Las). Mountain near the Honduras border. On its

Serquin. (J. L.)                                           3

Saw Franersco. Finca near Ciudad Barrios, Department of San

Sociedad. See Ocotepeque. (J. L.)

Tizate. Hill in the jurisdiction of Gotera. On it are ruins and

Yamasat. There is a rock with Indian paintings 1 km. to the

AZACUALPIA. South of Uluazapa. (S. K. L.)

Chiwameca, Many specimens from this town and neighboring

CincaLrique. Hacienda. Here are found archaeological speci-

Los Bonetes, North of the town of Carolina 10-12 km. (across

Rio Ojos de Agua. Small river 4.7 km. south of San Miguel.

Quezalcoatitan or Zalcoatitan, Ancient name of the valley

DEPARTMENT OF SANTA ANA

ATLEMPA OR MazAnua-ÁTLEMPA. Along the west bank of Rio

Avacualpa. Hacienda north of Lake Guija, where once existed

Bren. Village near Metapan. It is situated to the south and near
the outlet of Lake Guija. The village is also called Belen-Guijat
and Tablon de Belen, Anciently, it was called Uixaca-Mazahua
and Uxaca-Mazahua. In this region are found stone remains of an
Indian town, probably that of Guijar, which erroneously was sup-
posed to be submerged in the lake. Not far from here is the island
of Ipaltepeque [Igualtepeque], with remains of buildings and nu-
merous petroglyphs, (J. L.)

Casa Blanea. North of Chalchuapa. Mounds. (S. K. L..) See also

Chalchuapa. Here arc two large mounds, numerous conical

COATFPEQUE, In the lake of this name is an island on which is

Conacaste (EL). Village in the jurisdiction of Coatepeque. Near

ous Indian figures carved in the rock. (J. L)

El Congo or El Bigote. Town 20 km. southeast of Santa Ana,

Near the railroad station is a monumental stone head. (J. L.) Seven

Fraice (Ex). A deep spot in the Rio Lempa, jurisdiction of At-

(J. L.) See also under Guija Zone in Part II of this Memoir.

Guiyan, See Belen. (J. L.)

Hourxacan. Ruins situated near Tablon de Belen-Guijat, near

Igualtepeque. See Guija. (J. L.) See also under Guija Zone in

Metapan, Pottery. (H. J. S.}

MiLackros (Los). See Conacaste. (J. L..)

Pampe. River north of Chalchuapa. Betwcen this river and

Teotipa. See Guija. (J. L.) See also under Guija Zone in Part

Texistepeque, Town 20 km, north of Santa Ana. Mounds on

Ticuama, Artificial cave of unknown origin in the region of

Trapiche (El). Place 1 km. northeast of Chalchuapa. Here are

Uxaca, See Belen. (J. L.)

ACAJUTLA. Here are the ruins of a fortress constructed in Span-

Azacualpa. Village near Armenia. In its jurisdiction exist

Bocas DEL SUNZUNAT. In the terraces of this river have been

CaLuco. In its environs are found remains of buildings and

APPENDIX E

PRIVATE COLLECTIONS OF POTTERY AND STONE
IN EL SALVADOR

## Page 84

APPENDIX E. PRIVATE COLLECTIONS OF POTTERY
AND STONE IN EL SALVADOR

Plates VII-XIV-A show representative specimens, mostly of
pottery, from various private collections in the Republic. For
convenience, these have been grouped into three regions: eastern,
central, and western, and the short discussion below follows this
same grouping. The captions for cach plate list the wares of the
individual specimens, and the owners of the collections contain-
ing them. All photographs, unless otherwise credited, are repro-
duced here through the courtesy of the Carnegie Institution of
Washington.

Very few photographs of El Salvadoran pottery have ever been
published, and the present illustrations are included here, not
because they have a great deal of bearing on this report, but be-
cause they may prove uscful to future students wishing comparative
material. Collections such as those of Dr, E. O, Salazár, Sefiors
Victor Zanotti and Inocente Aguirre, and many others, contain
hundreds of specimens, and it was impossible to reproduce even
a majority of these in the space available in this Memoir. An
effort has been made, however, to show the principal wares and
shapes from each region, avoiding duplication as far as possible.

EASTERN EL SALVADOR

(Plates VIL-TX.)

The outstanding ceramic type in eastern El Salvador is un-
doubtedly Usulutan ware (pls, VIL, 1-17, 20-22; VIII, 24). Vessels
decorated with this technique figure largely in collections from
eastern sites, and Usulutan ware is, in fact, named for a Department
in this region. Shapes vary considerably but are usually deep
bowls, set on four small solid knob feet. Tripod supports are quite
rare (pl. VIL 4). Grotesque cffigy vases, often spouted, are a feature
of this ware (pls. VII, 20-22; VIII, 24). Usulutan technique is also
combined with positive painting and other forms of decoration
(pls. VIL, 79, 30; VILL, 26, 20, 33).

Plain wares cover a large range of colors and shapes (pls. VIT,
18, 23,24, 26-29, 32-35, 37-393 WII, 1-12, 14-18, 20, 22, 23; 1X, 24,
26, 34, 33), and appliqué and moulded decoration, usually in
grotesque animal and human features, is the rule. The two-handled
storage jar with a crude face applied on the neck (pl. VIII, 2) is
very common.

Red on orange wares most frequently appear in decp composite-
silhouette bowl shapes with two handles (pl. VIII, 32), although
tripod and tetrapod deep dishes also figure largely (pl. VIII, 35,
36). The execution of the restrained, fine line designs is excellent.

Most of the polychrome pottery divides easily into three general
types. The first obviously stems from the Lake Yojoa-Ulua River
region and reflects the Maya, or Maya-Lenca culture (pl. IX, 6, 7,
9, TA-15, 27). Most of these are undoubted imports into eastern El
Salvador. A second group is Nicoya polychrome (pl. IX, 77), an
introduction of the Chorotegan peoples of Nicaragua and Costa
Rica. The third type (pl. IX, 5, 78-20) must be considered in-
digenous for the present.

Plumbate ware is rare in eastern collections, only one specimen
having been observed (pl, VILL 27).

"Lothrop, 1927b, p. 155.

Figurines are frequently of the solid, modeled, cleft foot variely
(pl. 1X, 23, 37, 32), but other “archaic types are also common
(pl. IX, 25, 28, 29, 33). At Quelepa, grotesque monkey heads with
crowns (pl. 1X, 25) and the “pensamiento” figure (pl. IX, 33) are
characteristic. Hollow figurines and whistles {pl IX, 27, 30) are
quite frequent.

STONE OBJECTS FROM EASTERN EL SALVADOR

(Plate XII, 4, 7-22.)
The specimens illustrated represent only a very few of the many
stone artifacts to be found in El Salvadoran collections, The “duck-

billed" amulets (pl. XIT, 78) and the fine Xipe head (pl. XIT, 20)
are especially worthy of note,

CENTRAL FL SALVADOR

(Plates X; XI, 1-70, 13; XIII, 7, 9, 11; XIV-A, 2,8.)

Usulutan ware is rarer in central than in eastern El Salvador
and is not pictured here, but Lothrop has shown that it does occur.*
Not much can be said about the plain wares, since only the more
exotic forms find favor with collectors, but applied and modeled
decoration results in effigies (pl. X, 3, 4, 8), and a crudely painted
vessel (pl. X, 22) shows the presence of the eastern effigy storage
jar. The presence of Mixtec influence in incensarios (pl. X, 5, 9
is worthy of note. Judging from collections alone, a long-legged
tripod bowl with simple red designs is very characteristic of
Nejapa (pl. X, 17, 14, 15).

Polychrome ware in central El Salvador reflects much more Maya
influence than it did in the cast (pls. X, 20, 23, 27, 28-30; XT, 2,2,
4, 9; XUL, 77, 1g), and in addition to the Yojoa-Ulua region, re-
semblances to Copan (pls. X, 20; XUI, 15 and the Guatemala
highlands (pl. XI, 9) also occur. Nicoya polychrome vessels (pls.
X, 18, 24; XI, 3) give connections with the south, The indigenous
polychrome imay be represented by the specimens pictured on
plates X, 17, 27, 25, 32, 35, 36, and XI, 5:6.

Plumbate ware is common in central El Salvador (pls, X, y;
XIV-A, 2 and Lothrop, 1927b, figs. 21, 22). Lothrop also gives
examples of solid, cleft foot figurines from this region,* and other
types are found in collections (pl, XL 13).

WESTERN EL SALVADOR

(Plates XI, 41, 12, 74-33; XU, 1-3, 5, 6; XII, ro, 12-74; XIV-A,
4, 3,70.)

Usulutan ware is present, though not frequent, in this arca (pl.
XI, 77, 12, 20), but effigy modeling and appliqué on storage jars
and other plain ware vessels, judging from collections, is common
(pls. XI, 15, 76, 78; XII, 1, 2; XUL 12). In polychrome ware, many
vessels closely approximate Copan forms and decoration (pl. XI,
19, 21, 22, 24), and Nicoya polychrome is also present (pl. XL 33).
Plumbate ware vessels are numerous (pl. XL, 29, 37, 32). Figurines
comprise both the plain “archaic” (pl, XU, 3) and the solid cleft
foot varicties (pl. XII, 5, 6).

* Lothrop, 1927b, fig. 11.

APPENDIX E. PRIVATE COLLECTIONS OF POTTERY

AND STONE IN

Plates VII-XIV-A show representative specimens, mostly of
pottery, from various private collections in the Republic, For
convenience, these have been grouped into three regions: castern,
central, and western, and the short discussion below follows this
same grouping. ‘The captions for cach plate list the wares of the
individual specimens, and the owners of the collections contain-
ing them. All photographs, unless otherwise credited, are repro-
duced here through the courtesy of the Carnegie Institution of
Washington.

Very few photographs of El Salvadoran pottery have ever been
published, and the present illustrations are included here, not
because they have a great deal of bearing on this report, but be-
cause they may prove useful to future students wishing comparative
material. Collections such as those of Dr. F. O. Salazar, Señors
Victor Zanotti and Inocente Aguirre, and many others, contain
hundreds of specimens, and it was impossible to reproduce even
a majority of these in the space available in this Memoir. An
effort has been made, however, to show the principal wares and
shapes from each region, avoiding duplication as far as possible.

EASTERN EL SALVADOR

(Plates VILEX,)

The outstanding ceramic type in eastern El Salvador is un-
doubtedly Usulutan ware (pls. VIL 7-17, 20-22; VIII, 24). Vessels
decorated with this technique figure largely in collections from
eastern sites, and Usulutan ware is, in fact, named fora Department
in this region. Shapes vary considerably but are usually deep
bowls, set on four small solid knob feet. Tripod supports are quite
rare (pl. VII, 4). Grotesque efligy vases, often spouted, are a feature
of this ware (pls. VIL, 20-22; VILL, 24). Usulutan technique is also
combined with positive painting and other forms of decoration
(pls. VIL 19, 30; VIII, 26, 29, 33).

Plain wares cover a large range of colors and shapes (pls. VII,
18, 23, 24, 26-29, 31-35, 37-395 VUL, 1-72, 14-18, 20, 22, 23; IX, 24,
26, 34, 35), and appliqué and moulded decoration, usually in
grotesque animal and human features, is the rule. The two-handled
storage jar with a crude face applied on the neck (pl. VILL, 7) is
very common,

Red on orange wares most frequently appear in deep composite-
silhouette bowl shapes with two handles (pl. VIL, 32), although
tripod and tetrapod deep dishes also figure largely (pl. VII, 35,
36). The execution of the restrained, fine line designs is excellent.

Most of the polychrome pottery divides easily into three general
types. The first obviously stems from the Lake Yojoa-Ulua River
region and reflects the Maya, or Maya-Lenca culture (pl. IX, 6, 7,
9, 21-75, 27), Most of these are undoubted imports into eastern El
Salvador. A second group is Nicoya polychrome (pl. IX, 17), an
introduction of the Chorotegan peoples of Nicaragua and Costa
Rica, The third type (pl. EX, 5, 18-20) must be considered in-
digenous for the present.

Plumbate ware is rare in eastern collections, only one specimen
having been observed (pl, VIII, 27).

TLothtop, 1927b, p. 074.

EL SALVADOR

Figurines are frequently of the solid, modeled, cleft foot variety
(pl. IX, 23, 32, 32), but other “archaic” types are also common
(pl. IX, 25, 28, 29, 33). At Quelepa, grotesque monkey heads with
crowns (pl. TX, 25) and the “pensamiento” figure (pL TX, 33) are
characteristic. Hollow figurines and whistles (pl. IX, 27, 30) are
quite frequent.

STONE OBJECTS FROM EASTERN EL SALVADOR

(Plate XL, 4, 7-22.)

The specimens illustrated represent only a very ew of the many
stone artifacts to be found in El Salvadoran collections, The “duck-
billed” amulets (pl. XUL 28) and the fine Xipe head (pl, XI, 20)
are especially worthy of note.

CENTRAL EL SALVADOR

(Plates X; XL, 2-20, 13; XUL, 7, 9, 12; XIVA, 2, 8.)

Usulutan ware is rarer in central than in eastern El Salvador
and is not pictured here, but Lothrop has shown that it does occur
Not much can be said about the plain wares, since only the more
exotic forms find favor with collectors, but applied and modeled
decoration results in elligies (pl. X, 3, 4, 8), and a ctudely painted
vessel (pl. X, 22) shows the presence of the castern effigy storage
jar. The presence of Mixtec influence in incensarios (pl. X, 5, 9)
is worthy of note. Judging from collections alone, a long legged
tripod bowl with simple red designs is very characteristic of
Nejapa (pl. X, 17, 14, 15).

Polychrome ware in central El Salvador reflects much more Maya
influence than it did in the east (pls. X, 20, 23, 27, 28-30; XI, 7,3,
4,9; XUL 27, 74), and in addition to the Yojoa-Ulua region, re
semblances to Copan (pls, X, 20; XIII, 74) and the Guatemala
highlands (pl. XL, 9) also occur. Nicoya polychrome vessels (pls,
X, 18,24; XI, 3) give connections with the south. ‘The indigenous.
polychrome may be represented by the specimens pictured on
plates X, 77, 21, 25, 32, 35, 36, and XI, 5, 6.

Plumbate ware is common in central El Salvador (pls. X, 7:
XIVA, 2 and Lothrop, 1927b, figs. 21, 22). Lothrop also gives
examples of solid, cleft foot figurines from this region,? and other
types are found in collections (pl. XI, 13).

WESTERN EL SALVADOR

(Plates XI, rz, 12, 14-33; XUL 1-3, 5, 6; XIII, ro, 12-24; XIV-A,
7,3, 10.)

Usulutan ware is present, though not frequent, in this arca (pl.
XI, 77, 72, 20), but effigy modeling and appliqué on storage jars
and other plain ware vessels, judging from collections, is common
(pls. XL, 75, 16, 28; XML, 7, 2; XIU, 72). In polychrome ware, many
vessels closcly approximate Copan forms and decoration (pl. XI,
19, 21, 22, 24), and Nicoya polychrome is also present (pl. XL, 33).
Plumbate ware vessels are numerous (pl. XI, 29, 37, 32). Figurines
comprise both the plain “archaic” (pl. XH, 3) and the solid cleft
